FRCA Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Zero-order kinetics, where a constant amount is eliminated per unit time): Once phenytoin saturates hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, elimination becomes capacity-limited rather than concentration-dependent, so a fixed amount is cleared regardless of plasma level. Small dose increases beyond this saturation point cause disproportionate, unpredictable rises in plasma concentration, explaining the toxicity described. First-order kinetics (option A) describes most drugs at therapeutic concentrations, where a constant fraction is cleared, and stops applying once the enzymes saturate. Options B, D and E misdescribe the underlying kinetics and do not reflect the true mechanism of phenytoin toxicity.
  2. The correct answer is A (The drug is highly lipophilic and distributes extensively into peripheral tissues): A large apparent volume of distribution implies that most of the administered drug has left the plasma and is sequestered in tissues, which is characteristic of highly lipophilic agents. This tells the clinician relatively little plasma drug remains available for a given dose, and such drugs often need larger loading doses. Option B describes the opposite scenario, where extensive protein binding keeps a drug intravascular with a small volume of distribution. Options C, D and E describe unrelated pharmacokinetic properties that are not implied by a large volume of distribution.
  3. The correct answer is E (A greater proportion of free, pharmacologically active drug is now available): Reduced hepatic synthesis of albumin in liver disease lowers the amount of binding protein available, so a smaller fraction of the drug is bound and a larger unbound, active fraction circulates for the same total dose. This can produce an exaggerated clinical effect even though the total plasma concentration measured may appear normal or even low. Option A is incorrect because total concentration typically falls, not rises, while the free fraction increases. Options B, C and D describe outcomes that do not follow from reduced albumin and hepatic synthetic failure.
  4. The correct answer is B (Context-sensitive half-time, which increases with longer infusion duration): This describes the time needed for the plasma or effect-site concentration to fall by fifty percent after stopping an infusion, and for drugs like propofol it lengthens progressively the longer the infusion has run because peripheral compartments become increasingly saturated. This is why recovery becomes less predictable after very long TIVA cases. Elimination half-life (option A) is a fixed constant describing terminal elimination and does not capture this duration-dependent behaviour. Options C, D and E describe different, unrelated pharmacokinetic parameters.
  5. The correct answer is D (Hofmann elimination and non-specific ester hydrolysis occurring in plasma): Atracurium undergoes spontaneous, temperature- and pH-dependent Hofmann degradation in plasma alongside ester hydrolysis, both organ-independent processes, which is why its duration of action is largely unaffected by renal or hepatic failure. This makes it a rational choice for neuromuscular blockade in a patient with significant renal impairment. Option A wrongly attributes atracurium's breakdown to the plasma cholinesterase pathway used by suxamethonium, which is a distinct enzyme system. Options B, C and E describe organ-dependent elimination routes that would be unreliable in renal failure.
  6. The correct answer is A (Immature hepatic enzyme systems and reduced glomerular filtration at birth): Neonates have reduced activity of hepatic conjugating and oxidative enzymes together with a lower glomerular filtration rate relative to adults, both of which slow clearance of morphine and its metabolites. This produces a longer duration of action and a greater risk of accumulation with repeated dosing or infusion. Option B is incorrect because neonates actually have a relatively larger volume of distribution for water-soluble drugs due to higher total body water. Options C, D and E describe features that either do not exist in neonates or would not explain reduced clearance.
  7. The correct answer is C (Increased proportion of body fat raising the volume of distribution and storage): Ageing is typically associated with a relative increase in body fat and a decrease in lean mass and total body water, which increases the volume of distribution for lipophilic sedatives and allows drug to be stored and slowly released from adipose tissue. This produces a prolonged and exaggerated sedative effect from a standard adult dose. Option B describes the opposite physiological change and would actually reduce, not increase, distribution of a lipophilic drug. Options A, D and E describe changes that are either inaccurate for elderly patients or would tend to shorten rather than prolong the drug's effect.
  8. The correct answer is E (Drug X is more potent than Drug Y but the two have similar maximal efficacy): On the curve shown, Drug X achieves any given percentage effect at a consistently lower dose than Drug Y, indicating greater potency, while both curves approach the same maximal effect at higher doses, indicating similar efficacy. This distinguishes potency, a comparison of dose required, from efficacy, a comparison of maximal achievable effect. Option B incorrectly reverses which drug is more potent based on the curve positions shown. Options A, C and D misread either the potency difference or the converging maximal effect visible on the curve.
  9. The correct answer is B (Increased plasma volume and cardiac output can increase the volume of distribution): Pregnancy causes a substantial rise in plasma volume and cardiac output, which increases the volume of distribution for many drugs and can necessitate dose adjustment to achieve the same plasma concentration. This is a well recognised physiological adaptation relevant to induction agent dosing at term. Option A is incorrect because plasma volume increases rather than decreases in pregnancy. Options C, D and E each misstate the direction of the physiological change or its clinical significance.
  10. The correct answer is D (Morphine-6-glucuronide, an active metabolite that accumulates and potentiates opioid effect): This metabolite is a potent mu agonist in its own right and is renally excreted, so in chronic kidney disease it accumulates well beyond what the parent morphine dose would suggest, producing progressive sedation and respiratory depression. This is a key reason morphine is used cautiously in renal impairment. Morphine-3-glucuronide (option A) is the more abundant metabolite but has little analgesic or respiratory depressant activity. Options C and E describe metabolites unrelated to morphine, and option B describes an oxycodone metabolite rather than a morphine one.
  11. The correct answer is B (Rapid, predictable clearance that is independent of infusion duration or organ function): An ideal TIVA agent should be cleared quickly and consistently regardless of how long the infusion has run or the patient's renal or hepatic status, giving predictable offset and recovery. Propofol approximates this ideal reasonably well compared with older agents like thiopental. Option A describes an undesirable property that produces unpredictable, prolonged recovery after long infusions. Options C, D and E describe features that would make an agent poorly suited to continuous infusion, not well suited.
  12. The correct answer is C (Effect-site targeting allows the pump to briefly overshoot the plasma level to speed equilibration): Because there is a delay between plasma concentration and the concentration at the site of drug action across the blood-brain barrier, effect-site targeting deliberately drives a higher initial plasma concentration to accelerate equilibration and achieve the desired clinical effect sooner. This produces a faster onset of anaesthesia than plasma targeting alone. Option D is incorrect because the equilibration delay is not eliminated, only compensated for by the overshoot strategy. Options A, B and E make claims about dosing, total drug use or patient restriction that are not accurate.
  13. The correct answer is A (First-pass metabolism, which reduces the systemic bioavailability of the oral dose): Drugs absorbed from the gut first pass through the portal circulation to the liver before reaching systemic circulation, and extensive hepatic extraction at this stage can substantially reduce the fraction of the dose that ultimately reaches the systemic circulation intact. This is why some drugs have much lower oral than intravenous bioavailability. Option B describes a separate process involving biliary excretion and reabsorption rather than pre-systemic metabolism. Options C, D and E describe unrelated pharmacokinetic phenomena that do not match the scenario described.
  14. The correct answer is E (Phenytoin induces hepatic cytochrome enzymes, increasing rocuronium clearance and altering receptors): Chronic phenytoin use is a well recognised enzyme inducer and is also associated with upregulation of nicotinic acetylcholine receptors at the neuromuscular junction, both of which contribute to resistance to non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking agents like rocuronium. This combination means higher or more frequent dosing is often required. Option B is incorrect because a genuine and clinically significant interaction does exist. Options A, C and D describe mechanisms that are not the recognised basis for this interaction.
  15. The correct answer is D (Erythromycin inhibits cytochrome P450 3A4, reducing midazolam metabolism and prolonging its effect): Erythromycin is a well known inhibitor of the CYP3A4 isoenzyme responsible for midazolam metabolism, so co-administration reduces midazolam clearance and prolongs its sedative and respiratory depressant effects. This interaction is clinically significant and should be anticipated when combining these drugs. Option A describes the opposite effect, enzyme induction, which would reduce rather than prolong midazolam's action. Options B, C and E describe mechanisms that are not the established basis of this well documented interaction.
  16. The correct answer is C (Competitive antagonism, where increasing agonist concentration can shift the response curve rightward): Naloxone competes reversibly with opioid agonists for the same receptor binding site, so its antagonism is surmountable, meaning sufficiently high opioid concentrations can partially overcome the blockade, which is why repeated naloxone doses or an infusion may be needed for long-acting opioids. This reversibility distinguishes competitive from non-competitive antagonism. Option B describes irreversible binding, which does not apply to naloxone. Options A, D and E describe mechanisms that do not accurately characterise the naloxone-opioid receptor interaction.
  17. The correct answer is B (Propofol is preferable because its half-time remains comparatively short even at ten hours): The curve shown demonstrates that propofol's context-sensitive half-time rises only modestly with prolonged infusion, remaining under twenty minutes even at ten hours, while thiopental's half-time rises steeply into many hours, making recovery highly unpredictable after a long infusion. This favours propofol for maintenance during extended TIVA cases. Option A wrongly attributes the more favourable, gradual profile to thiopental when the curve clearly shows the opposite pattern. Options C, D and E misread the diverging trends clearly shown on the curve.
  18. The correct answer is A (A lower pKa relative to tissue pH increases the unionised fraction, speeding membrane crossing): Local anaesthetics must cross the lipid nerve membrane in their unionised, lipid-soluble form, and a pKa closer to physiological pH increases the proportion of drug existing in this unionised state at tissue pH, speeding onset of block. This is why agents like lidocaine, with a pKa near physiological pH, have a faster onset than agents with a higher pKa such as bupivacaine. Option D incorrectly states that ionised drug crosses the membrane fastest, which is the reverse of the true mechanism. Options B, C and E misattribute or dismiss the relationship between pKa and onset.
  19. The correct answer is D (High extraction ratio drug clearance is flow-limited; low extraction ratio drugs are capacity-limited): For a drug like propofol with a high hepatic extraction ratio, the liver removes nearly all the drug presented to it in a single pass, so clearance is dictated mainly by how much blood, and therefore drug, is delivered per unit time. Low extraction ratio drugs are instead limited by enzyme capacity and protein binding rather than blood flow. Option A is incorrect because high extraction ratio drugs are in fact quite sensitive to changes in hepatic blood flow. Options B, C and E misstate the relationship between extraction ratio and the rate-limiting step in clearance.
  20. The correct answer is E (Atypical plasma cholinesterase with reduced ability to hydrolyse suxamethonium normally): Suxamethonium apnoea most commonly results from an inherited atypical variant of plasma cholinesterase that hydrolyses the drug far more slowly than the normal enzyme, leading to prolonged neuromuscular blockade lasting hours rather than minutes. This is confirmed by genetic testing and dibucaine number assessment. Option A incorrectly describes excess rather than deficient enzyme activity, which would shorten rather than prolong the block. Options B, C and D describe enzyme systems or mechanisms unrelated to suxamethonium metabolism.
  21. The correct answer is D (Volatile anaesthetic agents combined with the depolarising agent suxamethonium): Malignant hyperthermia is classically triggered by exposure to volatile anaesthetic agents and suxamethonium in genetically susceptible individuals, producing uncontrolled calcium release within skeletal muscle and the hypermetabolic state described. Recognising this specific combination is essential for prompt diagnosis and management. Options B, C and E describe drug combinations that are not established malignant hyperthermia triggers. Option A describes local anaesthetic and adrenaline use, which is unrelated to this condition.
  22. The correct answer is B (Therapeutic index, calculated from the ratio of toxic dose to effective dose): The therapeutic index expresses the margin of safety between the dose producing the desired therapeutic effect and the dose producing toxicity, with a narrow index meaning a small increase above the effective dose risks toxicity. Drug A in this scenario has a narrow therapeutic index and requires careful dose titration and monitoring. Option A describes bioavailability, an unrelated concept concerning the fraction of drug reaching the circulation. Options C, D and E describe potency, efficacy and clearance respectively, none of which describe a margin of safety.
  23. The correct answer is E (Buprenorphine can precipitate withdrawal or reduce analgesia by displacing morphine at the receptor): As a high-affinity partial agonist at the mu receptor, buprenorphine can displace a full agonist like morphine from the receptor while itself producing a submaximal effect, which can precipitate withdrawal in an opioid-dependent patient or reduce overall analgesia compared with morphine alone. This interaction has practical implications for perioperative pain management in patients on buprenorphine. Option A is factually incorrect since buprenorphine acts substantially at mu receptors, not exclusively at kappa receptors. Options B, C and D misdescribe buprenorphine's receptor pharmacology and its interaction with full agonists.
  24. The correct answer is A (Neonates have a proportionally larger volume of distribution for water-soluble drugs): Neonates have a greater proportion of total body water and extracellular fluid relative to body weight compared with adults, which increases the volume of distribution for water-soluble drugs and can necessitate a relatively higher milligram per kilogram dose to achieve equivalent plasma concentrations. This is a key consideration in neonatal drug dosing. Option D incorrectly states the opposite relationship regarding extracellular fluid compartment size. Options B, C and E describe features that are not characteristic of neonatal physiology or would not explain the dosing requirement described.
  25. The correct answer is C (Redistribution, where drug moves from the plasma into peripheral tissue compartments): After a bolus of a highly lipophilic drug like thiopental, rapid awakening occurs because drug moves from the highly perfused central compartment, including the brain, into less well perfused peripheral tissues such as muscle and fat, lowering the plasma and brain concentration well before true elimination has occurred. This explains why clinical recovery is much faster than the drug's long elimination half-life would suggest. Option B describes irreversible removal from the body, which is a separate and much slower process. Options A, D and E describe processes that do not account for the rapid awakening seen after a single bolus.
  26. The correct answer is E (A parallel rightward shift of the curve with the same maximal effect eventually reached): A competitive, surmountable antagonist reduces the apparent potency of the agonist, requiring a higher dose to achieve the same effect, but because the antagonism can be overcome by sufficient agonist concentration, the same maximal effect is eventually reached, producing a parallel rightward shift as shown on the curve. This is the hallmark distinguishing competitive from non-competitive antagonism. Option B describes a non-competitive pattern, where maximal effect is reduced and cannot be overcome regardless of agonist dose. Options A, C and D misrepresent the direction or nature of the shift actually demonstrated on the curve.
  27. The correct answer is D (Low molecular weight, high lipid solubility and low degree of ionisation): Drugs that are small, lipid soluble and predominantly unionised at physiological pH cross the placental membrane most readily by passive diffusion, which is why many opioids and induction agents transfer substantially to the fetus. This has direct clinical relevance when timing drug administration during caesarean section. Option A describes properties that would each independently reduce, not increase, placental transfer. Options B, C and E describe combinations of properties that limit rather than promote transfer across the placenta.
  28. The correct answer is B (High lipid solubility, low molecular weight and low maternal plasma protein binding): Drugs that are lipid soluble, small and only weakly bound to maternal plasma proteins diffuse more readily into breast milk, where they can then be absorbed by the breastfeeding infant. This informs the choice of safer analgesic options postoperatively in breastfeeding mothers. Option A describes properties, high molecular weight and extensive ionisation, that would instead limit transfer into breast milk. Options C, D and E describe drug characteristics that reduce rather than promote transfer into milk.
  29. The correct answer is A (Reduced baseline clotting factor synthesis means warfarin's anticoagulant effect is additive and exaggerated): In cirrhosis, the liver already produces fewer clotting factors, so the further reduction caused by warfarin's vitamin K antagonism has a proportionally greater effect on coagulation than in a patient with normal synthetic function, increasing bleeding risk at standard doses. This means lower warfarin doses and closer monitoring are typically required in liver disease. Option C is incorrect because cirrhosis usually reduces rather than increases albumin synthesis. Options B, D and E each misstate the direction or existence of the effect of cirrhosis on warfarin sensitivity.
  30. The correct answer is C (Receptor downregulation and desensitisation following prolonged repeated receptor stimulation): Continuous or frequent stimulation of beta-2 receptors can lead to receptor downregulation and uncoupling from intracellular signalling pathways, reducing the clinical response to a previously effective dose over time. This tolerance phenomenon is well recognised with regular beta-agonist use. Option B describes a pharmacokinetic explanation involving clearance, which is not the primary mechanism behind beta-agonist tolerance. Options A, D and E describe mechanisms that do not accurately explain this well documented pharmacodynamic tolerance.
  31. The correct answer is A (Active tubular secretion of the drug by transporters in the proximal tubule): When a drug's renal clearance exceeds the glomerular filtration rate, this indicates that active transport mechanisms in the proximal tubule are secreting additional drug into the tubular fluid beyond what is filtered at the glomerulus. Organic anion and cation transporters are commonly responsible for this process. Option B describes reabsorption, which would reduce rather than increase net renal clearance. Options C, D and E describe processes that would reduce, not exceed, expected clearance based on glomerular filtration alone.
  32. The correct answer is D (The Schnider model incorporates age and lean body mass, unlike the fixed Marsh model): The Schnider pharmacokinetic model adjusts its calculations for patient age and estimated lean body mass, making it generally more suitable for elderly or lower-weight patients, whereas the original Marsh model uses fixed parameters based only on total body weight. Selecting the appropriate model can meaningfully affect predicted plasma and effect-site concentrations in such patients. Option B incorrectly reverses which model incorporates these adjustments. Options A, C and E make claims about model validation or design that do not accurately describe either model.
  33. The correct answer is C (Reduced hepatic blood flow and reduced liver mass reducing clearance of flow-limited drugs): Ageing is associated with a reduction in hepatic blood flow and overall liver mass, both of which reduce the clearance of drugs whose elimination is flow-limited, such as those with a high hepatic extraction ratio, producing a prolonged and exaggerated clinical effect from a standard dose. This is a key reason for cautious dose titration in elderly patients. Option A incorrectly states that hepatic blood flow increases with age, which is the opposite of the recognised physiological change. Options B, D and E misstate the direction or relevance of hepatic changes with ageing.
  34. The correct answer is E (The increased free fraction is rapidly redistributed and cleared, returning to a new steady state): When a drug is displaced from protein binding, the transient rise in free concentration increases the amount available for both distribution into tissues and elimination by the liver or kidneys, so the system generally re-equilibrates to a new steady state without sustained toxicity, provided clearance mechanisms are not themselves impaired. This is why displacement interactions are usually less clinically significant than once assumed. Option D incorrectly suggests an indefinite rise in total concentration, which does not occur once redistribution and clearance compensate. Options A, B and C make claims that are factually inaccurate about protein displacement interactions.
  35. The correct answer is B (Approximately two hours, based on the time for the concentration to halve): Reading the trace shown, the plasma concentration falls from eighty milligrams per litre at time zero to approximately forty milligrams per litre at two hours, and again halves to approximately twenty milligrams per litre by four hours, consistent with an elimination half-life of about two hours. This pattern of a constant proportional fall over equal time intervals is characteristic of first-order elimination. Options A, C and D read the halving interval incorrectly against the values shown on the curve. Option E is incorrect because a plasma concentration versus time curve is precisely the standard method used to estimate half-life.
  36. The correct answer is D (Loading dose equals volume of distribution multiplied by the desired target concentration): This standard pharmacokinetic formula allows a clinician to calculate the dose needed to rapidly achieve a desired plasma concentration by accounting for how much of the body the drug will distribute into, avoiding the delay of waiting for a maintenance infusion alone to reach that level. This is routinely applied when starting drugs with a long time to steady state. Option B confuses clearance, which relates to maintenance dosing, with the volume of distribution used for loading dose calculation. Options A, C and E describe formulas that do not correctly calculate a loading dose.
  37. The correct answer is A (Steady state takes approximately five elimination half-lives to be effectively reached): For a constant-rate infusion without a loading dose, plasma concentration rises exponentially and is considered to have effectively reached steady state after about five elimination half-lives, by which point over ninety-five percent of the final steady-state concentration has been achieved. This principle explains why a loading dose is often used clinically to bypass this delay. Option B is incorrect because steady state is never reached almost immediately without a loading dose. Options C, D and E misstate the relationship between infusion duration, half-life and the loading dose.
  38. The correct answer is C (The mu opioid receptor, which mediates analgesia alongside respiratory depression and miosis): Morphine acts predominantly at mu opioid receptors, which are responsible for the characteristic combination of strong analgesia together with respiratory depression, pupillary constriction and reduced gastrointestinal motility seen clinically. Understanding this receptor profile explains the typical side effect pattern of morphine and other mu agonists. Option B describes the kappa receptor, which is more associated with spinal analgesia and dysphoria with comparatively less respiratory depression. Options A, D and E describe receptor systems that do not primarily account for the described combination of effects.
  39. The correct answer is B (Lean body weight, since this better reflects the well-perfused induction compartment): Propofol's induction dose should be based on lean body weight because the drug initially distributes into the well-perfused central compartment, which scales with lean tissue rather than total body mass, and excess adipose tissue is comparatively poorly perfused and does not require proportional dosing. Using total body weight in obese patients risks overdosing and haemodynamic instability at induction. Option A incorrectly assumes equal distribution into fat and lean tissue, which does not reflect propofol's actual pharmacokinetics. Options C, D and E describe dosing scalars that are not the standard, validated approach for propofol induction in obesity.
  40. The correct answer is B (Once elimination pathways are saturated, phenytoin follows zero-order kinetics with a constant amount cleared per unit time): At higher plasma concentrations, phenytoin's hepatic metabolising enzymes become saturated, so a constant amount rather than a constant proportion is cleared per unit time. Small dose increases beyond this saturation point cause disproportionately large rises in plasma concentration, unlike first-order drugs. Option A describes standard first-order kinetics, which does not apply once saturation occurs. Options C, D and E describe plausible but unrelated pharmacokinetic phenomena that do not explain this specific nonlinear behaviour.
  41. The correct answer is D (A large volume of distribution reflecting extensive uptake into peripheral tissues outside plasma): Volume of distribution relates the dose given to the resulting plasma concentration, so a low plasma concentration for a given dose indicates the drug has distributed widely into tissues rather than remaining in plasma. Options B and C describe properties that would keep the drug within plasma and therefore produce a higher, not lower, measured concentration. Option A describes clearance rather than distribution, and option E describes elimination rather than the initial distribution pattern being asked about.
  42. The correct answer is A (An increased free fraction of drug raises the risk of exaggerated pharmacological and toxic effects): With less albumin available, a smaller proportion of drug is protein-bound, leaving a higher unbound fraction free to act at receptors and cross membranes, increasing effect and toxicity risk for a standard dose. Option B is incorrect because total concentration may actually fall as clearance of the unbound fraction increases. Option C is physiologically implausible in cirrhosis, and options D and E describe effects not directly caused by reduced albumin binding.
  43. The correct answer is C (Context-sensitive half-time, the time for plasma concentration to fall by half after stopping the infusion): This term specifically describes how the time needed for plasma concentration to halve increases with the duration of a continuous infusion, as peripheral compartments become saturated and continue redistributing drug back into plasma. Elimination half-life, option A, is a fixed pharmacokinetic constant that does not itself change with infusion duration. Options B, D and E describe unrelated pharmacokinetic or pharmacodynamic concepts that do not explain this duration-dependent recovery pattern.
  44. The correct answer is A (Rapid hydrolysis by non-specific plasma and tissue esterases independent of hepatic or renal function): Remifentanil contains an ester linkage cleaved rapidly by non-specific esterases present throughout blood and tissues, giving it a clearance that does not depend on hepatic or renal function and remains constant regardless of infusion duration. Option B describes fentanyl-like hepatic metabolism, which is not how remifentanil is cleared. Options C, D and E describe distribution or excretion patterns that would produce accumulation with prolonged infusion, the opposite of remifentanil's actual behaviour.
  45. The correct answer is E (The Schnider model adjusts clearance and compartment volumes for age, weight, height and lean body mass): The Schnider model incorporates patient covariates including age and lean body mass into its pharmacokinetic parameters, generally producing smaller induction doses in older or leaner patients than the Marsh model. Option A incorrectly attributes this covariate adjustment to the Marsh model, which uses a fixed central compartment volume based on total body weight alone. Option B misattributes a fixed volume to Schnider, and options C and D describe features that do not accurately distinguish the two models.
  46. The correct answer is D (It accounts for the equilibration delay, briefly raising plasma concentration to reach the desired brain concentration faster): Because drug takes time to equilibrate between plasma and the effect site, an effect-site targeting algorithm calculates a transiently higher plasma concentration to drive drug into the brain more quickly, achieving the desired clinical effect sooner. Option A is incorrect because equilibration delay is accounted for, not eliminated. Option B overstates the difference, and options C and E describe mechanisms unrelated to effect-site versus plasma targeting.
  47. The correct answer is B (A pKa near physiological pH means more drug exists in the unionised, lipid-soluble form able to cross membranes): The unionised base form of a local anaesthetic crosses lipid nerve membranes more readily, and a pKa close to tissue pH increases the proportion present in this form, speeding onset. Option A is an overgeneralisation that ignores the effect of tissue pH itself. Option C concerns duration and safety rather than onset, and options D and E describe factors affecting metabolism or duration rather than the speed of onset described here.
  48. The correct answer is C (Immature hepatic glucuronidation reduces morphine clearance, prolonging its elimination half-life considerably): Neonatal hepatic glucuronide conjugation enzymes are immature at birth, slowing morphine metabolism and prolonging its half-life, so standard weight-based doses can accumulate and cause respiratory depression. Option A is incorrect because neonates actually have proportionally greater total body water and a larger volume of distribution. Option B is the reverse of typical neonatal physiology, and option D is incorrect since the neonatal blood brain barrier is relatively immature and more, not less, permeable to opioids, while option E is not the principal mechanism here.
  49. The correct answer is A (Reduced hepatic blood flow and enzyme activity slow midazolam clearance, prolonging its elimination half-life): Ageing reduces hepatic blood flow and cytochrome P450 enzyme activity, slowing midazolam's hepatic metabolism and prolonging its half-life, which combines with increased sensitivity to produce an exaggerated and prolonged sedative effect. Option B is incorrect because elderly patients typically have an increased proportion of body fat, increasing rather than decreasing the volume of distribution for lipid-soluble drugs. Option C is the opposite of the typical finding since albumin tends to fall with age, and options D and E describe mechanisms that are not the principal explanation for midazolam's altered kinetics.
  50. The correct answer is E (Accumulation of the active metabolite morphine-6-glucuronide occurs because its renal excretion is impaired): Morphine is metabolised hepatically to morphine-6-glucuronide, an active metabolite with potent analgesic and respiratory depressant effects that is normally cleared by the kidneys, so renal impairment allows it to accumulate and prolong opioid effect. Option A describes an unrelated route of administration effect, and option B is factually wrong since morphine undergoes extensive hepatic metabolism. Options C and D describe mechanisms that are not the principal cause of prolonged effect seen in renal failure.
  51. The correct answer is D (Increased total body water and plasma volume expand the volume of distribution for water-soluble drugs): Pregnancy is associated with a substantial increase in plasma volume and total body water, which expands the volume of distribution for water-soluble drugs and can necessitate dose adjustment. Option A is the opposite of the actual physiological change, since plasma volume increases rather than decreases in pregnancy. Option B is incorrect because plasma protein concentration, particularly albumin, typically falls in pregnancy due to dilution, and options C and E describe other pregnancy-related changes not directly relevant to this volume of distribution question.
  52. The correct answer is B (Low molecular weight, high lipid solubility and low degree of ionisation at physiological pH): Drugs that are small, lipid-soluble and predominantly unionised at physiological pH cross the lipid placental membrane most readily by simple diffusion, which is why most induction agents and opioids transfer efficiently to the fetus. Option A describes properties that would markedly limit placental transfer rather than favour it. Option C describes maternal protein binding, which tends to limit rather than promote free drug crossing the placenta, and options D and E describe unrelated or implausible mechanisms.
  53. The correct answer is A (The oral dose produces a much lower systemic bioavailability than the equivalent intravenous dose): Drugs absorbed from the gut pass through the portal circulation to the liver before reaching systemic circulation, so extensive hepatic first-pass metabolism can substantially reduce the proportion of an oral dose that ultimately reaches systemic circulation compared with an intravenous dose that bypasses this step entirely. Option B is incorrect because bioavailability, not just absorption rate, differs markedly between the two routes. Option C reverses the typical relative onset speed, and options D and E are factually incorrect descriptions of first-pass metabolism.
  54. The correct answer is C (Rifampicin induces hepatic cytochrome enzymes, increasing vecuronium metabolism and reducing its clinical duration): Rifampicin is a potent inducer of hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, which increases the metabolic clearance of vecuronium and shortens its duration of action, requiring higher or more frequent dosing to maintain adequate neuromuscular blockade. Option A describes a protein-binding interaction that is not the principal mechanism here, and option B describes an implausible direct neuromuscular effect. Option D is not a recognised mechanism of tuberculosis or rifampicin therapy, and option E describes an effect that would prolong rather than shorten vecuronium's action.
  55. The correct answer is A (Cimetidine inhibits hepatic cytochrome enzymes, reducing warfarin metabolism and increasing its anticoagulant effect): Cimetidine is a well-recognised inhibitor of hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes involved in warfarin metabolism, so co-administration reduces warfarin clearance and increases its plasma concentration and anticoagulant effect, raising bleeding risk. Option B describes an effect that would reduce rather than increase free warfarin activity. Option C describes enzyme induction, the opposite of cimetidine's actual action, and options D and E describe mechanisms inconsistent with the clinical picture of increased anticoagulation described.
  56. The correct answer is D (An atypical or deficient plasma pseudocholinesterase enzyme genetically reducing hydrolysis of suxamethonium): Suxamethonium is normally rapidly hydrolysed by plasma pseudocholinesterase, and an inherited atypical or quantitatively deficient enzyme variant markedly slows this breakdown, producing prolonged paralysis and apnoea after a standard dose. Option A is incorrect because suxamethonium is not primarily cleared hepatically. Option B misattributes the mechanism to renal excretion rather than plasma enzyme hydrolysis, and options C and E describe implausible or unrelated mechanisms for this clinical scenario.
  57. The correct answer is B (Therapeutic index, the ratio between the toxic dose and the effective therapeutic dose of a drug): The therapeutic index specifically quantifies the safety margin between the dose producing a desired therapeutic effect and the dose producing toxicity, with a narrow index indicating a drug requiring closer monitoring. Option A describes systemic availability rather than a safety margin, and option C describes dose-response sensitivity rather than a comparison between therapeutic and toxic doses. Options D and E describe maximal effect and elimination respectively, neither of which describes this margin of safety.
  58. The correct answer is E (Buprenorphine is a partial agonist at the mu receptor, producing a submaximal response even with receptor saturation): As a partial agonist, buprenorphine cannot produce the same maximal intrinsic effect as a full agonist even when all receptors are occupied, giving it a ceiling effect on respiratory depression that improves its safety profile at higher doses. Option D incorrectly describes buprenorphine as having no agonist activity, when it does produce analgesia through partial agonism. Option C misattributes its principal receptor action, and options A and B describe pharmacokinetic rather than pharmacodynamic explanations that do not account for a dose-response ceiling.
  59. The correct answer is C (Competitive antagonist, whose effect can be overcome by increasing agonist concentration to restore maximal response): A competitive antagonist binds reversibly at the same receptor site as the agonist, and sufficient agonist concentration can displace it and restore the maximal response, shifting the dose-response curve to the right without reducing the maximum. Option A describes the opposite scenario of irreversible binding that reduces maximal response regardless of agonist concentration. Option B describes a different pharmacological concept entirely, and options D and E describe mechanisms not matching the description given.
  60. The correct answer is A (Tachyphylaxis, a rapid reduction in response to repeated doses due to depletion of noradrenaline stores): Ephedrine acts partly indirectly by releasing noradrenaline from sympathetic nerve terminals, and repeated dosing over a short period depletes these stores, producing a rapid and characteristic reduction in pressor response known as tachyphylaxis. Option B describes a slower process developing over a much longer timescale than seen in this scenario. Option C describes an unrelated and unpredictable reaction, and options D and E describe mechanisms occurring over days to weeks rather than within a single procedure.
  61. The correct answer is D (Reductions in hepatic blood flow, such as during major haemorrhage, will proportionally reduce propofol clearance): For a high extraction ratio drug like propofol, clearance is flow-limited, meaning it depends primarily on the rate of delivery of drug to the liver via hepatic blood flow rather than on enzyme capacity, so states of reduced hepatic perfusion proportionally reduce clearance. Option A is the opposite of the correct relationship, and option B describes a situation more relevant to low extraction ratio, capacity-limited drugs. Option C similarly misapplies capacity-limited reasoning to a flow-limited drug, and option E incorrectly emphasises renal rather than hepatic perfusion for a hepatically cleared drug.
  62. The correct answer is B (Loading dose equals the target plasma concentration multiplied by the volume of distribution): The loading dose required to rapidly achieve a target plasma concentration is calculated as that target concentration multiplied by the volume of distribution, since volume of distribution relates dose to resulting plasma concentration. Option A actually describes the calculation used for maintenance infusion rate at steady state, not loading dose. Options C and D combine parameters in ways that do not correspond to any standard pharmacokinetic formula, and option E describes an unrelated relationship between bioavailability and infusion rate.
  63. The correct answer is E (Clearance multiplied by the desired steady state plasma concentration of the drug): At steady state, the rate of drug administration must equal the rate of elimination, so the maintenance infusion rate is calculated as clearance multiplied by the desired steady state plasma concentration. Option A describes the loading dose, which is a separate calculation used to establish rather than maintain plasma concentration. Option B describes an unrelated physicochemical property, and options C and D describe distribution-related concepts that do not directly determine the required maintenance rate at steady state.
  64. The correct answer is D (Dosing by total body weight in obesity risks overdose since initial effect correlates most closely with lean tissue mass): Because the central compartment volume and initial drug effect correlate more closely with lean tissue mass than with total body weight, using total body weight to calculate an induction dose in obesity risks overdosing and exaggerated haemodynamic effects, so lean body weight based dosing is preferred for induction. Option A is an overgeneralisation not universally true across all drugs and patients. Option B describes a redistribution phenomenon relevant to maintenance rather than induction dosing, and option E is factually incorrect regarding propofol's route of elimination.
  65. The correct answer is C (Minimum alveolar concentration declines progressively with age due to age-related neuronal changes): Minimum alveolar concentration is well established to decrease progressively with age beyond roughly forty years, reflecting age-related central nervous system changes that increase sensitivity to volatile anaesthetic agents. Option A is incorrect since hepatic metabolism has minimal influence on volatile agent requirement, which is determined mainly by alveolar and brain partial pressure. Option B describes a pharmacokinetic effect on onset speed rather than requirement, and options D and E describe changes not primarily responsible for the reduced anaesthetic requirement seen with ageing.
  66. The correct answer is E (A relatively immature and more permeable blood brain barrier allows greater opioid penetration into the neonatal brain): The neonatal blood brain barrier is less fully developed than in older infants, allowing greater central nervous system penetration of opioids at a given plasma concentration, which combines with immature hepatic clearance to increase the risk of respiratory depression. Option A incorrectly describes protein binding as the principal mechanism of increased central sensitivity. Option B describes an implausible receptor distribution pattern, and option C describes a separate pharmacokinetic factor affecting plasma concentration rather than central sensitivity itself, while option D is the opposite of the actual finding in neonates.
  67. The correct answer is E (Reduced hepatic synthesis of clotting factors increases baseline sensitivity to warfarin's anticoagulant action): In chronic liver disease, reduced hepatic synthesis of vitamin K dependent clotting factors means baseline clotting factor levels are already low, so warfarin's inhibition of further synthesis produces an exaggerated anticoagulant effect for a given dose, a pharmacodynamic rather than pharmacokinetic mechanism. Options A, B and C describe pharmacokinetic mechanisms affecting drug concentration rather than the sensitivity of the clotting system itself. Option D similarly describes an unrelated pharmacokinetic pathway not relevant to warfarin, which is hepatically rather than renally cleared.
  68. The correct answer is B (Accumulated uraemic toxins compete with the drug for albumin binding sites, displacing it): In renal failure, accumulated uraemic metabolites and organic acids can compete with acidic drugs for the same albumin binding sites, displacing bound drug and increasing the free, pharmacologically active fraction even when total drug concentration is unchanged. Option A is incorrect because albumin synthesis and concentration typically fall rather than rise in chronic kidney disease. Option C describes a mechanism more relevant to basic rather than acidic drugs, and options D and E are factually incorrect descriptions of the recognised effect of uraemia on protein binding.
  69. The correct answer is C (Drug X is more potent than drug Y, as it achieves an equivalent effect at a lower dose): Potency refers to the dose required to produce a given effect, and since drug X's curve reaches a comparable analgesic response at a substantially lower log dose than drug Y, drug X is the more potent agent, while their similar plateau heights indicate comparable efficacy. Option A incorrectly reverses which drug requires the lower dose. Option B confuses potency with efficacy, which refers to maximal effect rather than dose required, and options D and E misinterpret the curve's shape and the definitions of potency and efficacy respectively.
  70. The correct answer is A (First-order elimination kinetics, in which a constant proportion of drug is cleared per unit time): The curve shows the plasma concentration halving during each equal time interval, which is the defining feature of first-order kinetics, where a constant fraction rather than a constant amount of drug is eliminated per unit time, producing a constant half-life. Option B describes zero-order kinetics, which would instead show a straight-line linear decline rather than the proportional fall shown. Option C describes a pattern not evident here since the proportional decline remains constant throughout, and options D and E describe mechanisms unrelated to the simple exponential decline depicted.
  71. The correct answer is D (Recovery after a prolonged propofol infusion becomes markedly less predictable than after remifentanil due to accumulation): The rising propofol curve reflects progressive accumulation in peripheral compartments with longer infusions, extending recovery time unpredictably, while remifentanil's flat curve reflects its rapid organ-independent esterase metabolism that prevents such accumulation regardless of duration. Option A misreads the trends, which diverge rather than remain comparable. Option B is the reverse of what the graph shows, and options C and E are inconsistent with the flat remifentanil curve and the clear difference in behaviour between the two drugs shown.
  72. The correct answer is E (Competitive antagonism, shifting the curve rightward while the maximal response remains ultimately achievable): A parallel rightward shift of the dose-response curve without a reduction in the maximal achievable response is the classic signature of competitive antagonism, since sufficient agonist concentration can still displace the reversible antagonist and achieve the same maximum effect. Option A describes non-competitive antagonism, which would instead depress the maximal response rather than merely shift the curve. Option B describes an entirely different mechanism not shown here, and options C and D describe irreversible or partial agonist effects that would also alter the maximal response rather than only its position.
  73. The correct answer is B (The rate constant keo, which describes how quickly drug equilibrates between plasma and the effect site): The keo constant specifically describes the rate of drug transfer between plasma and the theoretical effect compartment, explaining the lag seen between peak plasma concentration and peak effect-site concentration and clinical effect. Option A describes elimination from the body as a whole rather than distribution to the effect site specifically. Option C describes overall distribution volume rather than the rate of equilibration, and options D and E describe unrelated pharmacokinetic properties that do not govern this specific plasma-to-brain equilibration delay.
  74. The correct answer is D (Remifentanil is hydrolysed by non-specific plasma and tissue esterases independent of hepatic and renal function): Remifentanil's ester structure allows rapid breakdown by esterases present in blood and tissues throughout the body, a pathway that does not rely on hepatic or renal function, making its clearance reliably predictable even in combined organ failure. Option A is factually incorrect regarding remifentanil's degree of protein binding. Option B describes an implausible mechanism inconsistent with remifentanil's low lipid solubility relative to fentanyl, and option C incorrectly suggests the drug is not metabolised, when in fact it undergoes rapid metabolism by an organ-independent route, while option E describes a route not relevant to remifentanil.
  75. The correct answer is C (Sugammadex and the sugammadex-rocuronium complex are cleared renally, so elimination is prolonged in renal failure): Sugammadex and its encapsulated complex with rocuronium are eliminated almost entirely unchanged by the kidneys, so severe renal impairment prolongs elimination of the complex, though reversal itself still typically occurs effectively since the drug remains bound. Option A incorrectly attributes hepatic rather than renal elimination to sugammadex. Option B is not the correct description of the binding mechanism regarding renal relevance, and options D and E describe mechanisms that do not accurately reflect sugammadex's actual pharmacokinetics.
  76. The correct answer is A (Pethidine's inhibition of serotonin reuptake combines with impaired serotonin breakdown from the monoamine oxidase inhibitor, causing serotonin toxicity): Pethidine has serotonergic activity through inhibition of serotonin reuptake, and when combined with a monoamine oxidase inhibitor that prevents normal serotonin breakdown, excessive central serotonergic activity produces the classic triad of agitation, hyperthermia and rigidity seen in serotonin toxicity. Option B incorrectly describes an effect on metabolism rather than the true serotonergic mechanism. Option C describes an unrelated protein-binding interaction, and options D and E describe implausible mechanisms not responsible for this well-recognised drug interaction.
  77. The correct answer is E (The mother is an ultrarapid CYP2D6 metaboliser, producing excess morphine that transfers into breast milk): Codeine requires CYP2D6-mediated conversion to morphine for its analgesic effect, and mothers who are genetically ultrarapid metabolisers produce unusually high morphine concentrations that can transfer into breast milk in sufficient quantity to cause severe sedation and respiratory depression in a breastfed infant. Option A describes an infant-based mechanism rather than the maternal genetic cause responsible. Option B is not an accurate general statement about codeine's transfer into breast milk, and options C and D describe mechanisms that do not primarily account for this specific, well-recognised phenomenon.
  78. The correct answer is C (It potentiates GABA-A receptor chloride channel opening, increasing inhibitory neurotransmission.): Propofol potentiates GABA-A receptor mediated chloride influx, hyperpolarising neuronal membranes and producing rapid, dose-dependent loss of consciousness. Its high lipid solubility allows swift blood-brain barrier penetration, accounting for onset within one arm-brain circulation time. Ketamine, not propofol, is the NMDA receptor antagonist listed among the distractors. Local anaesthetics act on sodium channels, and anticholinesterases such as neostigmine act on acetylcholinesterase, neither of which explains propofol's hypnotic action.
  79. The correct answer is A (Thiopental, because it induces hepatic delta-aminolaevulinic acid synthase activity substantially.): Thiopental and other barbiturates induce hepatic ALA synthase, the rate-limiting enzyme of haem synthesis, which can precipitate an acute porphyric crisis in susceptible patients. Propofol, ketamine and opioids are generally regarded as safe alternatives in acute porphyria. Etomidate's adrenal suppression is unrelated to porphyrin metabolism. Volatile agents are not established porphyrinogenic triggers in the way barbiturates are.
  80. The correct answer is E (It stimulates the sympathetic nervous system, typically preserving blood pressure and heart rate.): Ketamine indirectly stimulates the sympathetic nervous system by inhibiting noradrenaline reuptake, which usually maintains or increases heart rate and blood pressure, making it favourable in hypovolaemic or shocked patients. This contrasts with propofol, which causes dose-dependent vasodilation and myocardial depression that can worsen hypotension. In catecholamine-depleted patients ketamine's direct myocardial depressant effect may become unmasked. The other listed mechanisms do not reflect ketamine's actual cardiovascular pharmacology.
  81. The correct answer is B (Reversible inhibition of 11-beta-hydroxylase, suppressing adrenal cortisol and aldosterone synthesis.): Etomidate reversibly inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase, an enzyme required for cortisol and aldosterone synthesis, and this adrenocortical suppression can persist for many hours even after a single bolus. This is why etomidate infusions are avoided and single-dose use in septic patients remains debated. Histamine release, QT prolongation and malignant hyperthermia triggering are not characteristic etomidate effects. Etomidate is not a recognised trigger agent for malignant hyperthermia.
  82. The correct answer is D (Its MAC-reducing effect is additive with volatile agents, lowering their required concentration.): Nitrous oxide's alveolar concentration effect is additive with volatile agents, so co-administration reduces the required MAC of the volatile agent while contributing useful analgesia, even though nitrous oxide alone cannot reliably produce surgical anaesthesia. It has low blood-gas solubility, giving rapid onset and offset, not delayed. It undergoes negligible hepatic metabolism. It is not an opioid receptor agonist, though it does have some interaction with endogenous opioid pathways.
  83. The correct answer is A (It has a pleasant, non-pungent smell with minimal airway irritation on inhalation.): Sevoflurane is non-pungent and well tolerated on inhalation, making it the agent of choice for gaseous induction, particularly in children who will not tolerate a facemask with an irritant vapour such as desflurane or isoflurane. Desflurane, not sevoflurane, has the lowest blood-gas coefficient among current volatiles. Sevoflurane causes only modest myocardial depression, and it is metabolised hepatically to compounds including inorganic fluoride, not renally. Laryngospasm is an undesirable complication, never a desirable sign of depth.
  84. The correct answer is C (Remifentanil maintains a short, near-constant half-time regardless of infusion duration.): Remifentanil is metabolised by non-specific plasma and tissue esterases, so its context-sensitive half-time remains short and essentially constant regardless of infusion duration, as the curve shown demonstrates with a flat line. Propofol's half-time, in contrast, rises progressively with longer infusions because of accumulation in peripheral compartments, seen as the climbing curve. This is why remifentanil is favoured for rapid emergence after prolonged infusions. The two drugs clearly behave differently, not identically, on the curve shown.
  85. The correct answer is E (It causes coronary vasodilation that may theoretically divert flow away from stenosed vessels.): Isoflurane is a potent coronary vasodilator, and concern has historically existed regarding coronary steal, where dilation of normal vessels theoretically diverts blood flow away from fixed stenotic segments supplying ischaemic myocardium. It does not reliably cause bradycardia and instead tends to preserve or slightly increase heart rate. Halothane, not isoflurane, is most notorious for myocardial catecholamine sensitisation. Isoflurane produces less myocardial depression than halothane at equivalent MAC.
  86. The correct answer is B (It has a very low blood-gas partition coefficient, allowing rapid alveolar equilibration.): Desflurane has one of the lowest blood-gas partition coefficients of the volatile agents, meaning relatively little drug needs to leave the alveoli to saturate the blood, so alveolar and therefore brain concentrations rise and fall rapidly, producing fast induction and, more clinically relevant, fast emergence. It undergoes minimal hepatic metabolism, unlike agents such as halothane. It is not particularly lipid soluble compared with more potent agents, and elimination is almost entirely via exhalation, not the kidneys.
  87. The correct answer is D (Increasing patient age beyond infancy, which is associated with a falling MAC requirement.): MAC requirement peaks in infancy and then falls progressively with increasing age throughout adulthood, so older patients typically require a lower volatile concentration for the same depth of anaesthesia. Acute amphetamine or cocaine intoxication and chronic alcohol use both increase MAC requirement, not decrease it. Hyperthyroidism is not a well established cause of increased MAC, whereas hypothermia and hypothyroidism reduce it. Younger children, not younger patients generally, actually require higher MAC than adults.
  88. The correct answer is C (It binds and persistently activates nicotinic receptors, causing sustained endplate depolarisation.): Suxamethonium binds nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and persistently activates them, causing sustained endplate depolarisation that first produces visible fasciculation and then flaccid paralysis because the depolarised membrane cannot repolarise and respond to further stimuli. Unlike non-depolarising agents, it is an agonist rather than a competitive antagonist at the receptor. It is hydrolysed by plasma cholinesterase, not acetylcholinesterase at the junction itself. Only patients with atypical or deficient plasma cholinesterase experience markedly prolonged block, not everyone.
  89. The correct answer is A (Life-threatening hyperkalaemia from widespread extrajunctional acetylcholine receptor upregulation after denervation.): After denervation injuries such as spinal cord trauma, burns or prolonged immobility, extrajunctional nicotinic receptors proliferate across the muscle membrane, and suxamethonium-induced depolarisation of this vastly expanded receptor population can cause massive potassium efflux and life-threatening hyperkalaemic cardiac arrest, typically from around 48 hours to many months after injury. This risk is unrelated to malignant hyperthermia susceptibility. Anaphylaxis and cholinesterase deficiency are separate, much less predictable phenomena not specifically linked to denervation.
  90. The correct answer is D (At this higher dose it produces onset comparable to suxamethonium within about sixty seconds.): Rocuronium at a higher dose of around 1.2 mg/kg produces intubating conditions within approximately sixty seconds, rivalling suxamethonium, which makes it a useful alternative for rapid sequence induction when depolarising agents are contraindicated. Its duration at this dose is actually prolonged, not shortened. Atracurium and cisatracurium, not rocuronium, undergo significant Hofmann elimination. Rocuronium causes minimal histamine release compared with older benzylisoquinolinium drugs, and unlike many agents it can now be rapidly reversed with sugammadex.
  91. The correct answer is B (It depends substantially on hepatic and, to a lesser degree, renal clearance mechanisms.): Vecuronium is eliminated predominantly by hepatic metabolism and biliary excretion, with a smaller contribution from renal clearance, so significant hepatic or renal impairment can prolong its duration of action considerably, which is particularly relevant during prolonged intensive care sedation. Hofmann elimination is characteristic of atracurium and cisatracurium, not vecuronium. Plasma cholinesterase metabolises suxamethonium and mivacurium, not vecuronium. Vecuronium is not eliminated unchanged and rapidly by the kidneys alone.
  92. The correct answer is E (Rocuronium reaches maximal block fastest, consistent with its use in rapid sequence induction.): The curve shown demonstrates that rocuronium's twitch height falls to near-maximal block within roughly ninety seconds, faster than vecuronium or atracurium at standard intubating doses, which is precisely why rocuronium is favoured when a rapid, suxamethonium-like onset is required. Vecuronium and atracurium both reach maximal block considerably later than rocuronium on the curve shown. The three agents clearly do not share an identical onset time. Atracurium is the slowest, not the fastest, of the three agents depicted.
  93. The correct answer is C (It undergoes organ-independent Hofmann elimination, though laudanosine accumulation may occur with prolonged infusion.): Atracurium undergoes Hofmann elimination, a spontaneous, temperature- and pH-dependent chemical degradation that is independent of hepatic or renal function, making it useful in organ failure. Its breakdown produces laudanosine, a metabolite that can accumulate during prolonged high-dose infusion and has been associated with central nervous system excitation in animal studies. It is not renally excreted unchanged, is not primarily hepatically metabolised, and does not depend on cholinesterase activity, distinguishing it from suxamethonium and mivacurium.
  94. The correct answer is A (Cisatracurium causes substantially less histamine release than atracurium at equipotent doses.): Cisatracurium is a single stereoisomer of atracurium that is considerably more potent and causes substantially less histamine release at clinically equivalent doses, making it preferable in patients with reactive airway disease such as severe asthma where bronchospasm from histamine release would be particularly hazardous. It still undergoes Hofmann elimination like its parent compound, not purely hepatic metabolism. Its duration is broadly similar to atracurium, and it is not the only agent usable in asthma, though it is often preferred. Its onset is slower than rocuronium's, not faster.
  95. The correct answer is D (Neostigmine inhibits acetylcholinesterase, and glycopyrrolate counters resulting muscarinic effects such as bradycardia.): Neostigmine inhibits acetylcholinesterase, increasing acetylcholine availability at the neuromuscular junction to reverse non-depolarising block, but this also increases acetylcholine at muscarinic sites throughout the body, causing bradycardia, salivation and bronchospasm. Glycopyrrolate is an antimuscarinic given alongside it specifically to block these unwanted muscarinic effects without affecting the desired nicotinic reversal. Glycopyrrolate itself has no neuromuscular reversal action, and the two drugs act on different receptor subtypes rather than synergistically reversing paralysis together.
  96. The correct answer is E (It encapsulates free rocuronium molecules within a cyclodextrin ring, forming an inactive complex.): Sugammadex is a modified gamma-cyclodextrin that forms a tight, inactive complex by encapsulating free rocuronium (or vecuronium) molecules in the plasma, lowering free plasma concentration and creating a gradient that draws the drug away from the neuromuscular junction, allowing rapid reversal even of deep block. It does not act on acetylcholinesterase, unlike neostigmine. It does not act at the receptor itself or hydrolyse the aminosteroid molecule. It has no effect on benzylisoquinolinium blockers such as atracurium.
  97. The correct answer is B (Sugammadex can bind progestogens, reducing contraceptive efficacy for the following seven days.): Sugammadex can bind progestogens due to structural similarity within its cyclodextrin cavity, and a single dose is considered equivalent to a missed oral contraceptive pill, so patients should be advised to use an additional method of contraception for the following seven days. This effect applies to both combined and progestogen-only contraceptives, not combined preparations exclusively. The effect is temporary and related to a single administration, not permanent or lifelong. It reduces rather than increases effective hormonal exposure.
  98. The correct answer is C (Bupivacaine dissociates far more slowly from cardiac sodium channels than lidocaine does.): Bupivacaine binds cardiac sodium channels avidly and dissociates from them much more slowly than lidocaine, a phenomenon described as fast-in, slow-out kinetics, meaning channels remain blocked for longer between action potentials and predisposing to malignant arrhythmias and profound myocardial depression at toxic doses. Bupivacaine actually has higher lipid solubility than lidocaine, not lower. Its greater cardiotoxicity is not explained by metabolite formation, and it does not spare cardiac sodium channels, which is precisely the problem.
  99. The correct answer is A (Intravenous 20% lipid emulsion, given as an initial bolus followed by an infusion.): The Association of Anaesthetists guidance for local anaesthetic systemic toxicity recommends intravenous 20% lipid emulsion as a bolus followed by an infusion, alongside standard airway, breathing and circulation support and seizure control, since lipid emulsion is thought to act partly as a lipid sink that sequesters the local anaesthetic away from cardiac tissue. Adrenaline doses should actually be reduced below standard advanced life support doses if used, not escalated. Bicarbonate and dialysis are not first-line specific treatments for this condition. LAST can be fatal and requires active treatment, not observation alone.
  100. The correct answer is D (Start cardiopulmonary resuscitation and give intravenous lipid emulsion alongside standard advanced life support.): The trace shown demonstrates ventricular tachycardia, a recognised and life-threatening manifestation of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity, and management requires immediate cardiopulmonary resuscitation with modified advanced life support alongside intravenous 20% lipid emulsion given as a bolus and infusion, since local anaesthetic cardiotoxicity can be resistant to standard resuscitation drugs alone. Giving further local anaesthetic would worsen toxicity. Standard high-dose adrenaline boluses are actually reduced in this specific scenario. Ventricular tachycardia of this type does not reliably self-terminate and requires active treatment.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Adding adrenaline increases the maximum safe dose by causing local vasoconstriction and slower absorption.): Adding adrenaline to lidocaine causes local vasoconstriction, which slows systemic absorption from the injection site, prolongs the local anaesthetic effect and allows a higher total maximum dose to be used safely compared with plain lidocaine. This is why standard references quote a higher milligram-per-kilogram limit for lidocaine with adrenaline than for lidocaine alone. Adrenaline-containing local anaesthetics remain contraindicated for use around end arteries such as digits, the nose and the penis because of ischaemic risk, but this is unrelated to the systemic maximum dose principle.
  2. The correct answer is E (Lidocaine has a pKa closer to physiological pH, giving a higher unionised fraction available.): Lidocaine has a pKa of around 7.9, closer to physiological pH than bupivacaine's pKa of around 8.1, meaning a relatively larger proportion exists in the unionised, lipid-soluble form at tissue pH, which crosses nerve membranes more readily and produces a faster onset. Lidocaine actually has lower protein binding than bupivacaine, which relates more to duration than onset. Bupivacaine is more lipid soluble and more potent than lidocaine, yet has a slower onset because of its less favourable pKa.
  3. The correct answer is B (The active metabolite morphine-6-glucuronide accumulates due to reduced renal clearance in renal impairment.): Morphine undergoes hepatic glucuronidation to morphine-3-glucuronide and morphine-6-glucuronide, and the latter is an active metabolite with potent analgesic and respiratory depressant properties that is renally excreted, so in renal impairment it accumulates and can cause progressive sedation and respiratory depression even with an unchanged morphine dose. Morphine does undergo significant hepatic metabolism, contrary to one distractor. This is a well recognised reason to use caution or alternative opioids such as fentanyl in significant renal impairment.
  4. The correct answer is A (Fentanyl is considerably more lipid soluble, allowing rapid penetration of the blood-brain barrier.): Fentanyl is highly lipid soluble, allowing it to cross the blood-brain barrier rapidly and produce a fast onset of analgesic and respiratory depressant effects, whereas morphine is comparatively hydrophilic and crosses more slowly, delaying its peak central effect. Fentanyl actually has a shorter context-sensitive half-time after single doses than morphine, not a longer one. It is metabolised hepatically by cytochrome P450 enzymes, not plasma esterases, which instead describes remifentanil. Both drugs act primarily at the mu opioid receptor.
  5. The correct answer is D (It has a low pKa, giving a high unionised fraction at physiological pH for fast entry.): Alfentanil has a relatively low pKa of around 6.5, so at physiological pH a much larger proportion exists in the unionised, lipid-soluble form compared with fentanyl, allowing rapid diffusion across the blood-brain barrier and a very fast onset of action, despite alfentanil being less potent overall than fentanyl. Its overall lipid solubility is actually lower than fentanyl's, but the unionised fraction available is what drives fast onset. Its short context-sensitive half-time, not slow metabolism, explains its brief duration.
  6. The correct answer is C (It is hydrolysed rapidly by non-specific plasma and tissue esterases, independent of organ function.): Remifentanil is unique among opioids in being rapidly hydrolysed by non-specific plasma and tissue esterases, a pathway independent of hepatic or renal function, which gives it an extremely short and essentially constant context-sensitive half-time regardless of how long the infusion has run, allowing predictable, rapid offset. It is not stored significantly in fat, and it is not metabolised by cytochrome P450 enzymes or excreted unchanged renally, unlike many other opioids. Its receptor binding is reversible, like other opioids, rather than irreversible.
  7. The correct answer is E (Both opioids show increasing MAC reduction with rising plasma concentration up to a ceiling.): The curve shown demonstrates that both opioids produce progressively greater reduction in volatile agent MAC as plasma concentration rises, but this effect plateaus at higher concentrations rather than continuing indefinitely, reflecting a ceiling effect on the opioid contribution to anaesthetic depth. The two curves are similar but not identical in shape and magnitude. Neither line remains flat at zero, so both drugs clearly reduce MAC requirement. Since a plateau is reached, MAC reduction does not continue rising without limit at very high concentrations, consistent with option E's description of a ceiling.
  8. The correct answer is D (Naloxone has a shorter duration of action than morphine, risking renarcotisation if not monitored.): Naloxone has a relatively short half-life and duration of action compared with longer-acting opioids such as morphine, so its reversal effect may wear off before the opioid has been fully eliminated, risking renarcotisation with recurrent respiratory depression unless the patient is closely monitored or a repeat dose or infusion is given. It does not bind receptors permanently and is a competitive antagonist. Its duration relative to the opioid used varies depending on which opioid was administered, since some opioids are longer acting than others.
  9. The correct answer is C (Propofol infusion syndrome; stop the propofol infusion and provide aggressive supportive care.): This clinical picture of metabolic acidosis, hyperkalaemia, rhabdomyolysis, cardiac failure and arrhythmias in the context of prolonged high-dose propofol infusion is classic for propofol infusion syndrome, a rare but often fatal complication thought to relate to impaired mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation. Management is supportive, requiring immediate cessation of the propofol infusion and aggressive haemodynamic and metabolic support, sometimes including extracorporeal support. Malignant hyperthermia and serotonin syndrome present differently, and local anaesthetic toxicity and anaphylaxis are unrelated to a prolonged propofol infusion in this way.
  10. The correct answer is A (Pain on injection can be reduced by pre-treatment with intravenous lidocaine before propofol.): Pain on injection with propofol is common and is thought to relate to activation of free nerve endings by the aqueous phase of the lipid emulsion, and it can be reduced by giving intravenous lidocaine before or mixed with the propofol, or by injecting into a larger proximal vein. Propofol is formulated as a lipid emulsion, not a simple aqueous high pH solution. Injection pain also occurs with etomidate, so it is not unique to propofol. Injecting into a smaller vein tends to worsen, not eliminate, injection pain, and this phenomenon does not indicate allergy.
  11. The correct answer is B (Myoclonic movements are common with etomidate and are thought to reflect disinhibition of subcortical activity.): Myoclonic movements are a well recognised and common side effect of etomidate induction, thought to result from disinhibition of subcortical structures rather than true seizure activity, and they are not typically associated with increased cerebral metabolic activity or epileptiform discharge on EEG. This is not an allergic reaction and does not require adrenaline. It does not necessarily indicate inadequate anaesthetic depth requiring further dosing. It is unrelated to malignant hyperthermia, and etomidate is not considered a significant epileptogenic trigger in the way some other agents can be.
  12. The correct answer is E (Co-administer a benzodiazepine such as midazolam and allow recovery in a quiet, calm setting.): Emergence phenomena including vivid dreams, hallucinations and disorientation are well recognised with ketamine and can be reduced by co-administering a benzodiazepine such as midazolam and by ensuring the patient recovers in a quiet, calm, minimally stimulating environment. Benzodiazepines reduce rather than worsen these reactions, so avoiding them is incorrect. Increasing the ketamine dose does not reliably prevent this effect and may worsen recovery. Giving further ketamine at emergence would not blunt an established reaction. A bright, noisy environment with frequent stimulation tends to worsen rather than reduce emergence phenomena.
  13. The correct answer is B (Continue sedation and ventilatory support until neuromuscular function recovers spontaneously over time.): In suxamethonium apnoea due to atypical or deficient plasma cholinesterase, the drug cannot be broken down at the normal rate, and the correct management is to keep the patient adequately sedated and mechanically ventilated until neuromuscular function recovers spontaneously, which may take several hours. Neostigmine can paradoxically prolong a depolarising block rather than reverse it and should be avoided. Sugammadex is specific to aminosteroid non-depolarising agents such as rocuronium and vecuronium and has no effect on suxamethonium. Further suxamethonium or dialysis are not appropriate management steps for this condition.
  14. The correct answer is A (Neuromuscular blocking drugs are the most commonly identified cause of perioperative anaphylaxis in reported series.): Neuromuscular blocking drugs, including rocuronium, are consistently identified as the most common cause of perioperative anaphylaxis in national audit and allergy series, often related to IgE-mediated sensitisation to quaternary ammonium epitopes shared across multiple agents and even some household products. True anaphylaxis is immunologically mediated, not simply non-immunological histamine release, which is a separate, generally milder phenomenon. Cross-reactivity between different neuromuscular blockers is relatively common, not rare, because of shared antigenic epitopes. Skin testing is a valuable investigation after suspected anaphylaxis, and reactions can occur on apparent first exposure due to prior sensitisation from unrelated products.
  15.
