The correct answer is C (Glycopyrrolate does not readily cross the blood-brain barrier, avoiding central anticholinergic effects.): Glycopyrrolate is a quaternary ammonium compound that does not readily cross the blood-brain barrier, so it produces the desired peripheral antimuscarinic effects to counter neostigmine-induced bradycardia and secretions without causing central anticholinergic side effects such as confusion, unlike atropine which does cross into the central nervous system. Glycopyrrolate actually has a slightly slower onset than atropine, which is why some clinicians prefer atropine when very rapid heart rate correction is needed. It generally causes less tachycardia than atropine and has a comparable or longer duration of action, not shorter.
  16. The correct answer is D (True allergy is more common with ester local anaesthetics, related to a para-aminobenzoic acid metabolite.): True allergic reactions are more commonly associated with ester local anaesthetics such as procaine, because their hydrolysis by plasma cholinesterase produces para-aminobenzoic acid, a recognised allergen, whereas genuine amide allergy is rare. Esters and amides are metabolised by entirely different pathways, plasma cholinesterase for esters and hepatic microsomal enzymes for amides, so cross-reactivity between the two groups is unlikely. Amide agents are not metabolised by plasma cholinesterase. Preservative-free amide local anaesthetic preparations are available and can be used when preservative sensitivity, rather than true local anaesthetic allergy, is suspected.
  17. The correct answer is B (Potentiation of GABA-A receptors increasing chloride channel conductance): Propofol binds to the beta subunit of the GABA-A receptor, enhancing chloride ion influx and hyperpolarising the neuronal membrane. This potentiation reduces neuronal excitability throughout the central nervous system, producing rapid hypnosis. Propofol has no clinically significant action at NMDA, nicotinic or opioid receptors, unlike ketamine or opioid drugs. This GABA-A mechanism also explains propofol's anticonvulsant and antiemetic properties.
  18. The correct answer is D (Mixing lidocaine with propofol or giving lidocaine before injection): Premixing lidocaine with propofol, or administering intravenous lidocaine shortly before induction with a venous occlusion technique, significantly reduces the incidence and severity of injection pain. Pain arises from free propofol activating perivascular pain receptors and kininogen release in the vessel wall. Using a larger, more proximal vein such as the antecubital fossa also helps, whereas small hand veins worsen pain. Rapid injection and further dilution do not reliably reduce discomfort.
  19. The correct answer is A (Thiopental, as barbiturates induce hepatic delta-aminolevulinic acid synthase activity): Thiopental and other barbiturates upregulate the rate-limiting enzyme in haem synthesis, delta-aminolevulinic acid synthase, which can precipitate a severe acute porphyric crisis in susceptible patients. Propofol, ketamine and etomidate are all regarded as safe induction agents in acute porphyria and are the preferred alternatives. Remifentanil has no meaningful porphyrinogenic risk despite its ester structure. This is a well-established, examinable safety principle for any patient with acute intermittent porphyria.
  20. The correct answer is E (Non-competitive antagonism of NMDA glutamate receptors in the cortex and limbic system): Ketamine non-competitively blocks the NMDA receptor, functionally disconnecting the thalamocortical and limbic systems to produce the characteristic dissociative state with intact airway reflexes, preserved respiratory drive and sympathomimetic cardiovascular stimulation. This differs fundamentally from GABA-A potentiating agents such as propofol or thiopental. Ketamine has additional actions at opioid and monoaminergic sites, but NMDA antagonism is the primary mechanism underlying dissociative anaesthesia.
  21. The correct answer is C (Reversible inhibition of 11-beta-hydroxylase causing adrenocortical suppression): Etomidate reversibly inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase, an enzyme required for cortisol and aldosterone synthesis, and this suppression can persist for many hours even after a single induction dose. In critically ill or septic patients this adrenal suppression has been associated with worse outcomes, which is why etomidate infusions are avoided and single-dose use in shock remains debated. Etomidate does not typically cause significant histamine release, myocardial depression or malignant hyperthermia.
  22. The correct answer is B (Rapid hydrolysis by nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases independent of infusion time): Remifentanil is metabolised by ubiquitous plasma and tissue esterases at a constant rate regardless of total dose or infusion duration, so its context-sensitive half-time remains around three to four minutes even after prolonged infusion. Propofol, by contrast, redistributes into and accumulates within peripheral fat compartments, so its context-sensitive half-time rises the longer the infusion continues. This makes remifentanil uniquely predictable for titration during long procedures. The flat curve reflects organ-independent elimination rather than any hepatic or renal process.
  23. The correct answer is A (A low blood:gas partition coefficient allowing rapid equilibration with alveolar gas): Sevoflurane has a relatively low blood:gas solubility coefficient, meaning less agent is required to saturate the blood before alveolar and brain partial pressures rise, producing a rapid onset and offset of anaesthesia. It is also non-pungent, which further supports its use for inhalational induction in children. Oil:gas coefficient relates to potency rather than speed of onset, and molecular weight and hepatic metabolism are not the principal determinants of induction speed.
  24. The correct answer is D (Clinical significance of isoflurane-induced coronary steal is now considered minimal in most patients): Although early laboratory studies raised concern that isoflurane could redistribute coronary blood flow away from ischaemic myocardium in patients with steal-prone coronary anatomy, subsequent clinical evidence has shown this effect is of limited practical importance provided haemodynamic stability, adequate coronary perfusion pressure and normal carbon dioxide levels are maintained. Isoflurane remains widely used safely in patients with ischaemic heart disease. The other options overstate or misattribute the phenomenon.
  25. The correct answer is E (Its marked pungency provokes airway irritation, coughing and laryngospasm): Desflurane has a strong pungent odour that irritates the airway, commonly triggering coughing, breath-holding and laryngospasm if used for inhalational induction, which is why it is reserved for maintenance of anaesthesia after intravenous or sevoflurane induction. Its low blood:gas solubility actually supports rapid emergence rather than delaying it. It does not smell of rotten eggs and has no clinically relevant negative inotropic collapse at standard concentrations.
  26. The correct answer is C (Diffusion hypoxia caused by rapid outward movement of nitrous oxide diluting alveolar oxygen): At the end of a nitrous oxide anaesthetic, large volumes of nitrous oxide diffuse rapidly out of the blood into the alveoli, diluting alveolar oxygen and carbon dioxide and reducing the alveolar partial pressure of oxygen, which can cause transient hypoxaemia if the patient is not given supplemental oxygen. This is why 100% oxygen should be administered for several minutes after discontinuing nitrous oxide. The second gas effect describes the opposite process occurring during induction, not emergence.
  27. The correct answer is D (The alveolar concentration preventing purposeful movement to a standard surgical incision in 50% of patients): Minimum alveolar concentration is defined as the alveolar concentration of an inhalational agent at one atmosphere that prevents purposeful movement in response to a standard surgical skin incision in 50% of subjects. It is a measure of potency used to compare different volatile agents and is unrelated to loss of consciousness, ventilation, gag reflex or sympathetic blunting, each of which requires a different, higher or lower, multiple of MAC.
  28. The correct answer is B (Intravenous 20% lipid emulsion bolus followed by continuous infusion): Local anaesthetic systemic toxicity causing ventricular arrhythmia or cardiovascular collapse should be treated immediately with intravenous 20% lipid emulsion, given as a bolus followed by an infusion according to the AAGBI guideline, alongside standard airway management and cardiopulmonary resuscitation if required. Lipid emulsion is thought to act as a lipid sink, drawing lipophilic bupivacaine away from cardiac sodium channels. Standard resuscitation drugs such as high-dose adrenaline may worsen arrhythmias in this setting and reduced doses are recommended. Amiodarone, magnesium and calcium are not first-line treatments for confirmed local anaesthetic systemic toxicity.
  29. The correct answer is E (Upregulated extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors cause exaggerated potassium efflux and hyperkalaemia): Following burns, denervation injury or prolonged immobility, extrajunctional nicotinic acetylcholine receptors proliferate across the muscle membrane beyond the normal neuromuscular junction. Suxamethonium depolarises all of these receptors simultaneously, causing massive potassium efflux and potentially fatal hyperkalaemia. This risk begins around 24 to 48 hours after injury and persists for many months, so suxamethonium is avoided in this patient population from that point onward.
  30. The correct answer is A (An atypical or deficient plasma cholinesterase enzyme unable to hydrolyse suxamethonium): Suxamethonium is normally rapidly broken down by plasma cholinesterase. Patients with an atypical genetic variant, or an acquired deficiency of this enzyme, hydrolyse suxamethonium far more slowly, causing prolonged neuromuscular blockade and apnoea lasting hours rather than minutes. The dibucaine number quantifies the enzyme's ability to be inhibited by dibucaine and helps distinguish atypical genotypes from simple quantitative deficiency. Myasthenia gravis and malignant hyperthermia are unrelated to this specific pattern.
  31. The correct answer is C (1.2 mg/kg, providing onset of blockade comparable to suxamethonium): At a dose of 1.2 mg/kg, rocuronium produces onset of profound neuromuscular blockade within approximately 60 seconds, closely approximating the speed of suxamethonium, making it a suitable alternative for rapid sequence induction. Lower doses such as 0.6 mg/kg have a slower onset more appropriate for routine induction. Doses of 2.0 mg/kg exceed what is required and prolong recovery without a meaningful benefit in onset speed.
  32. The correct answer is A (Vecuronium relies mainly on hepatic and renal elimination, so both organs affect duration): Vecuronium undergoes significant hepatic metabolism and biliary excretion with a smaller renal component, so both hepatic and renal impairment can prolong its duration of action, unlike atracurium and cisatracurium, which undergo organ-independent Hofmann elimination and ester hydrolysis. This distinction is clinically important when selecting a muscle relaxant for patients with combined organ dysfunction, where atracurium or cisatracurium are generally preferred.
  33. The correct answer is D (Hofmann elimination, a temperature- and pH-dependent spontaneous chemical degradation): Atracurium undergoes Hofmann elimination, a non-enzymatic spontaneous degradation that occurs at physiological temperature and pH, alongside minor ester hydrolysis, meaning its breakdown is independent of hepatic or renal function. This makes it a useful choice in patients with combined hepatic and renal failure. It is not eliminated by plasma cholinesterase, unlike suxamethonium, nor does it rely on renal excretion or hepatic conjugation.
  34. The correct answer is B (Cisatracurium causes significantly less histamine release than its parent compound atracurium): Cisatracurium is a single stereoisomer of atracurium that is considerably more potent and produces substantially less histamine release at clinically equipotent doses, making it a safer choice in patients prone to bronchospasm such as those with asthma. It still undergoes Hofmann elimination like atracurium, so its organ-independent clearance profile is unchanged, and its onset and reversal characteristics are broadly similar.
  35. The correct answer is C (Glycopyrrolate blocks unwanted muscarinic effects such as bradycardia and excessive secretions): Neostigmine inhibits acetylcholinesterase, increasing acetylcholine at both nicotinic neuromuscular junctions and muscarinic autonomic sites, so unopposed muscarinic stimulation can cause bradycardia, bronchospasm and excessive salivation. Glycopyrrolate is an antimuscarinic agent that blocks these unwanted muscarinic effects while leaving the desired nicotinic reversal of neuromuscular blockade intact. Glycopyrrolate has no independent reversal action and does not itself affect neuromuscular transmission.
  36. The correct answer is E (It forms a tight encapsulation complex with rocuronium, removing free drug from plasma): Sugammadex is a modified gamma-cyclodextrin that forms a tight, water-soluble host-guest complex encapsulating free rocuronium molecules in the plasma, rapidly lowering the plasma concentration of unbound rocuronium and drawing more drug away from the neuromuscular junction down a concentration gradient. This differs fundamentally from neostigmine, which works indirectly by inhibiting acetylcholinesterase rather than binding the relaxant directly. Sugammadex has no effect on acetylcholine release or plasma cholinesterase.
  37. The correct answer is B (Sugammadex selectively encapsulates aminosteroid relaxants and does not bind benzylisoquinoliniums): Sugammadex's cyclodextrin structure is specifically shaped to encapsulate aminosteroid neuromuscular blocking agents such as rocuronium and vecuronium. Atracurium and cisatracurium are benzylisoquinolinium compounds with a different molecular structure that sugammadex cannot bind, so it has no reversal effect on their neuromuscular blockade. Neostigmine remains the appropriate reversal agent for benzylisoquinolinium relaxants. Sugammadex is not contraindicated by renal impairment for rocuronium reversal in the same way.
  38. The correct answer is A (Reversible blockade of voltage-gated sodium channels preventing action potential propagation): Local anaesthetics diffuse into the neuronal axon and bind the intracellular portion of voltage-gated sodium channels, stabilising them in an inactivated state and preventing the sodium influx required for action potential propagation, thereby producing reversible conduction block. This is distinct from the GABA-A or NMDA receptor mechanisms of general anaesthetic agents. Local anaesthetics have no clinically significant action on calcium channels or cyclooxygenase enzymes at standard doses.
  39. The correct answer is D (Bupivacaine dissociates far more slowly from cardiac sodium channels than lidocaine): Bupivacaine binds cardiac sodium channels with high affinity and dissociates very slowly during diastole compared with lidocaine, a phenomenon described as fast-in, slow-out kinetics. This prolonged channel blockade produces profound conduction slowing and refractory ventricular arrhythmias that are difficult to treat, explaining bupivacaine's disproportionately severe cardiotoxicity relative to its local anaesthetic potency. Lidocaine's rapid dissociation makes it comparatively much safer if accidental intravascular injection occurs.
  40. The correct answer is C (The ED95, the dose producing 95% suppression of twitch height): The dose on the dose-response curve that produces 95% depression of the twitch response, as shown for rocuronium, is termed the ED95 and is used to standardise comparisons of neuromuscular blocking drug potency. Intubating doses are typically expressed as multiples of the ED95, for example two to three times ED95 for rapid sequence induction. The ED95 is a pharmacodynamic measure of potency, distinct from onset time, context-sensitive half-time or minimum alveolar concentration, which apply to different classes of drug or different parameters entirely.
  41. The correct answer is E (3 mg/kg plain lidocaine and 7 mg/kg with adrenaline): The standard maximum safe dose of plain lidocaine for infiltration anaesthesia is approximately 3 mg/kg, rising to approximately 7 mg/kg when combined with adrenaline, because adrenaline-induced vasoconstriction slows systemic absorption from the injection site. Exceeding these limits significantly increases the risk of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity. These figures should always be calculated using the patient's actual body weight before infiltration begins.
  42. The correct answer is D (Early local anaesthetic systemic toxicity from inadvertent intravascular or excessive dosing): Perioral tingling, a metallic taste and tinnitus are classic early central nervous system warning signs of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity, typically preceding more severe features such as seizures, arrhythmias and cardiovascular collapse. Recognising these early features allows prompt cessation of local anaesthetic administration and preparation of lipid emulsion before deterioration occurs. This presentation is not typical of anaphylaxis, opioid toxicity, vasovagal syncope or malignant hyperthermia.
  43. The correct answer is B (1.5 ml/kg bolus followed by an infusion of 15 ml/kg/hour): The AAGBI guideline for management of severe local anaesthetic systemic toxicity recommends an initial intravenous bolus of 20% lipid emulsion at 1.5 ml/kg over approximately one minute, followed by a continuous infusion at 15 ml/kg/hour, with further boluses repeated if cardiovascular stability is not restored, up to a defined maximum cumulative dose. A single small bolus with no infusion is inadequate to sustain the lipid sink effect required to reverse toxicity.
  44. The correct answer is A (Accumulation of the active metabolite morphine-6-glucuronide due to reduced renal clearance): Morphine is metabolised in the liver to morphine-6-glucuronide, an active metabolite with potent analgesic and respiratory depressant properties that is normally cleared renally. In renal impairment this metabolite accumulates, producing exaggerated and prolonged sedation and respiratory depression even when the morphine dose itself seems modest. This is a key reason why morphine is used cautiously, and often at reduced doses or with extended intervals, in patients with significant renal dysfunction.
  45. The correct answer is E (Fentanyl's high lipid solubility causes accumulation in fat, raising context-sensitive half-time with longer infusions): Fentanyl is highly lipophilic and readily redistributes into and accumulates within peripheral fat and muscle compartments during prolonged infusion. As the infusion continues, these compartments become saturated and continue to release drug back into plasma after the infusion stops, meaning the context-sensitive half-time increases substantially with infusion duration. This contrasts with remifentanil, whose organ-independent ester metabolism keeps its context-sensitive half-time essentially constant.
  46. The correct answer is C (A low pKa resulting in a high proportion of unionised drug at plasma pH): Alfentanil has a lower pKa than fentanyl, meaning a much higher proportion of the drug exists in the unionised, lipid-soluble form at physiological plasma pH, allowing rapid diffusion across the blood-brain barrier despite alfentanil's overall lower total lipid solubility. This produces a notably faster onset of effect-site concentration than fentanyl, which is useful for rapidly blunting the response to laryngoscopy.
  47. The correct answer is A (Remifentanil is metabolised by nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases independent of organ function): Remifentanil contains an ester linkage that is rapidly hydrolysed by widespread nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases, a pathway entirely independent of hepatic or renal function. This gives remifentanil a rapid, predictable and consistent offset regardless of infusion duration or organ impairment, making it particularly useful in patients with combined hepatic and renal failure. It is not excreted unchanged renally and is not affected by plasma cholinesterase deficiency, unlike suxamethonium.
  48. The correct answer is D (Bupivacaine slows cardiac conduction by blocking cardiac sodium channels, which may progress to complete heart block): Bupivacaine's high-affinity, slowly dissociating blockade of cardiac sodium channels progressively slows conduction through the atrioventricular node and His-Purkinje system, which can manifest as widening QRS complexes, second-degree block and ultimately complete heart block or asystole in severe toxicity. This conduction effect occurs through direct cardiac sodium channel blockade rather than central nervous system depression alone, and requires prompt treatment with lipid emulsion alongside advanced life support, not spontaneous resolution.
  49. The correct answer is B (Nitrous oxide diffuses into closed air-filled spaces faster than nitrogen can leave, expanding them): Nitrous oxide is approximately 30 times more soluble in blood than nitrogen, so it diffuses into any closed gas-filled space, such as a pneumothorax, bowel gas or an air embolus, considerably faster than nitrogen can diffuse out. This causes the trapped gas space to expand in volume or rise in pressure, which can rapidly worsen an undiagnosed pneumothorax into a tension pneumothorax. Nitrous oxide is therefore contraindicated whenever a closed gas space is known or suspected.
  50. The correct answer is E (Desflurane has a substantially higher global warming potential than sevoflurane): Desflurane has a considerably longer atmospheric lifetime and a much higher global warming potential than sevoflurane, meaning its use contributes disproportionately to the carbon footprint of anaesthetic practice even at equivalent clinical doses. Many departments have therefore reduced or eliminated routine desflurane use in favour of sevoflurane or total intravenous anaesthesia as part of sustainability initiatives. This concern relates to atmospheric persistence and radiative properties rather than vaporiser design or scavenging efficiency.
  51. The correct answer is C (Ketamine produces bronchodilation through sympathomimetic and direct smooth muscle relaxant effects): Ketamine causes release of endogenous catecholamines and has a direct relaxant effect on bronchial smooth muscle, producing clinically useful bronchodilation that makes it a favoured induction agent in patients with severe bronchospasm or status asthmaticus. This contrasts with propofol, which lacks a comparable bronchodilator effect and can be associated with bronchospasm in some patients. Ketamine does not abolish airway reflexes and does not meaningfully reduce airway secretions.
  52. The correct answer is D (Propofol infusion syndrome, associated with prolonged high-dose administration): Propofol infusion syndrome is a rare but potentially fatal complication of prolonged, high-dose propofol sedation, characterised by severe metabolic acidosis, rhabdomyolysis, hyperkalaemia, acute kidney injury, hepatomegaly and cardiac failure, thought to relate to impaired mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation. Risk increases with higher doses, longer duration and concurrent catecholamine or steroid use. Recognition is important because early cessation of propofol and supportive care improve survival, and it is distinct from malignant hyperthermia or adrenal insufficiency.
  53. The correct answer is A (Etomidate causes minimal myocardial depression and preserves haemodynamic stability at induction): Etomidate has minimal effect on myocardial contractility, systemic vascular resistance or heart rate compared with other induction agents such as propofol or thiopental, making it particularly useful for induction in haemodynamically unstable or hypovolaemic trauma patients. It does not raise blood pressure directly and has no meaningful analgesic properties, so opioid coadministration is still required. Its adrenal suppression risk means repeated dosing or infusion is avoided, but a single induction dose remains a reasonable choice in profound shock.
  54. The correct answer is B (Anaphylaxis to sugammadex is rare but recognised and patients should be monitored after administration): Although uncommon, sugammadex has been associated with hypersensitivity reactions ranging from mild skin changes to severe anaphylaxis with hypotension and bronchospasm, and this risk is now specifically highlighted in prescribing guidance. Patients should be observed for a period after administration, and standard anaphylaxis management should be instituted promptly if a reaction occurs. It is incorrect to assume such reactions are always attributable to residual rocuronium rather than sugammadex itself, and prior rocuronium exposure is not a prerequisite for a reaction.
  55. The correct answer is A (Gq protein activation increasing intracellular calcium via inositol trisphosphate): Alpha-1 adrenoceptors are coupled to Gq proteins, which activate phospholipase C to generate inositol trisphosphate and diacylglycerol. Inositol trisphosphate releases calcium from the sarcoplasmic reticulum, raising intracellular calcium and triggering smooth muscle contraction. This pathway underlies the vasoconstrictor and mydriatic effects of alpha-1 agonists such as noradrenaline and phenylephrine, distinct from the Gs-cAMP pathway used by beta receptors.
  56. The correct answer is C (Reduced central sympathetic outflow from the vasomotor centre): Clonidine stimulates presynaptic alpha-2 adrenoceptors in the brainstem, which inhibits noradrenaline release and dampens sympathetic outflow to the periphery. This lowers heart rate, peripheral vascular resistance and blood pressure without directly blocking peripheral receptors. Abrupt withdrawal can cause rebound hypertension due to sudden restoration of sympathetic tone, which is why the drug should be tapered perioperatively.
  57. The correct answer is B (Beta-1 receptors predominate in the myocardium, beta-2 in bronchial smooth muscle): Beta-1 receptors are the dominant subtype in cardiac tissue, mediating increased heart rate and contractility, while beta-2 receptors predominate in bronchial and vascular smooth muscle, mediating relaxation. A cardioselective agent such as bisoprolol has greater affinity for beta-1 than beta-2 receptors, reducing but not eliminating the risk of bronchospasm. Selectivity is dose-dependent and is lost at higher doses, so caution is still required in reactive airway disease.
  58. The correct answer is D (Increased cyclic AMP activating protein kinase A and reducing calcium): Beta-2 receptors are coupled to Gs proteins, which activate adenylate cyclase to raise intracellular cyclic AMP. Protein kinase A then phosphorylates myosin light chain kinase and reduces intracellular calcium availability, causing smooth muscle relaxation. This is the opposite pathway to the calcium-raising Gq mechanism used by alpha-1 receptors. Ipratropium works by a different, antimuscarinic mechanism and can be used synergistically.
  59. The correct answer is E (500 micrograms intramuscular adrenaline into the anterolateral thigh): The Resuscitation Council UK anaphylaxis algorithm recommends 500 micrograms of 1 in 1000 adrenaline given intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh, repeated after five minutes if there is no improvement. Intravenous adrenaline is reserved for refractory anaphylaxis or peri-arrest situations and requires titrated small boluses under close monitoring due to the risk of arrhythmia. Intramuscular injection provides reliable absorption without the cardiovascular risks of an inadvertent intravenous bolus.
  60. The correct answer is B (Noradrenaline, titrated to a mean arterial pressure of 65 mmHg): Noradrenaline is recommended as the first-line vasopressor in septic shock because its predominant alpha-1 mediated vasoconstriction raises mean arterial pressure with relatively modest chronotropic effect. Vasopressin or adrenaline may be added as second agents if the target mean arterial pressure is not achieved with noradrenaline alone. Dopamine is no longer favoured due to a higher incidence of arrhythmia and tachycardia.
  61. The correct answer is A (0.5 to 1 mg given as an intravenous bolus dose): Metaraminol is a predominantly alpha-1 agonist typically given in small intravenous boluses of 0.5 to 1 mg to correct spinal-induced hypotension in obstetric anaesthesia. Compared with phenylephrine it has a slightly longer duration and some direct beta activity, but at obstetric doses it preserves uteroplacental perfusion with minimal fetal acid-base disturbance. Larger doses risk severe hypertension and reflex bradycardia.
  62. The correct answer is D (Depletion of noradrenaline stores from presynaptic nerve terminals occurs): Ephedrine acts largely indirectly by promoting the release of endogenous noradrenaline from presynaptic sympathetic nerve terminals, with a smaller direct receptor effect. Repeated administration progressively depletes these noradrenaline stores, producing tachyphylaxis where the same dose becomes less effective. This contrasts with directly acting agents such as phenylephrine, which do not rely on neurotransmitter stores and so do not exhibit this pattern.
  63. The correct answer is C (Baroreceptor-mediated vagal reflex triggered by the rise in blood pressure): Phenylephrine is a pure alpha-1 agonist with no direct cardiac activity, but the rapid rise in arterial pressure it produces stimulates the carotid and aortic baroreceptors. This triggers a reflex increase in vagal tone and reduction in sympathetic outflow, slowing the heart rate. The bradycardia is therefore an indirect physiological response rather than a direct pharmacological effect of the drug on the heart.
  64. The correct answer is E (V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle cells): Vasopressin produces vasoconstriction by acting on V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle, activating a Gq-coupled pathway independent of adrenergic receptors. This makes it particularly useful in catecholamine-resistant or vasoplegic shock, where adrenoceptor responsiveness may be reduced or downregulated. Its separate receptor mechanism allows a synergistic effect when combined with noradrenaline, often permitting a reduction in catecholamine dose.
  65. The correct answer is D (Increased myocardial contractility with modest vasodilation and little change in resistance): Dobutamine is a predominantly beta-1 agonist that increases myocardial contractility and cardiac output, with additional beta-2 mediated peripheral vasodilation that offsets much of the alpha-1 vasoconstrictor effect. The net result is improved cardiac output with relatively unchanged or slightly reduced systemic vascular resistance, making it useful in cardiogenic shock where afterload reduction is desirable. It can cause tachycardia and arrhythmia at higher doses.
  66. The correct answer is A (Increased intracellular cyclic AMP by inhibiting its breakdown in cardiac and smooth muscle): Milrinone inhibits phosphodiesterase type 3, which normally breaks down cyclic AMP, so cyclic AMP accumulates in both cardiac myocytes and vascular smooth muscle. In the myocardium this increases intracellular calcium and contractility, while in smooth muscle it causes vasodilation, giving milrinone its characteristic inodilator profile. Because its action bypasses the beta receptor, it remains effective even when receptors are downregulated by chronic catecholamine exposure or beta-blockade.
  67. The correct answer is E (Continue the established beta-blocker throughout the perioperative period): Patients already established on chronic beta-blocker therapy should continue this medication throughout the perioperative period, including on the morning of surgery, to avoid rebound tachycardia and myocardial ischaemia from abrupt withdrawal. Newly initiating beta-blockade immediately before surgery is not recommended, as trials have shown an increased risk of stroke and hypotension without clear mortality benefit. Continuation of established therapy remains standard practice in national perioperative guidance.
  68. The correct answer is C (Severe bradycardia, heart block or cardiac depression from additive AV nodal blockade): Verapamil is a non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker with significant negative chronotropic and negative inotropic effects, largely through slowing atrioventricular nodal conduction. Combining it with a beta-blocker produces additive suppression of sinoatrial and atrioventricular node activity, risking severe bradycardia, heart block or profound myocardial depression. This combination should generally be avoided or used only with careful monitoring.
  69. The correct answer is D (Thyroid dysfunction and pulmonary fibrosis with long-term use): Amiodarone contains a high proportion of iodine by weight and can cause either hypothyroidism or hyperthyroidism, requiring regular thyroid function monitoring. Long-term use is also associated with pulmonary fibrosis, corneal microdeposits, hepatotoxicity and photosensitivity, reflecting its extensive tissue accumulation and very long half-life of several weeks. These effects make preoperative assessment of thyroid and respiratory function relevant in chronic amiodarone users.
  70. The correct answer is A (Predominant alpha-1 mediated vasoconstriction with minimal counteracting vasodilation): The curve reaching the higher maximal mean arterial pressure represents noradrenaline, whose dominant alpha-1 mediated vasoconstriction produces a steep, sustained rise in pressure with increasing dose. Dobutamine, in contrast, combines beta-1 inotropy with beta-2 mediated vasodilation, which limits the overall rise in mean arterial pressure even as the dose increases. This distinction explains why noradrenaline rather than dobutamine is chosen when a substantial pressor effect is required.
  71. The correct answer is B (Transient atrioventricular nodal block interrupting a re-entrant circuit): Adenosine acts on A1 receptors in the atrioventricular node, activating potassium channels and transiently hyperpolarising nodal tissue, which causes brief but profound atrioventricular block. This interrupts re-entrant circuits that depend on the atrioventricular node, terminating most paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardias. Its extremely short half-life of a few seconds means effects resolve rapidly, and warning the patient of transient chest tightness or a sense of impending doom is recommended before administration.
  72. The correct answer is E (Competitive antagonism of acetylcholine at muscarinic M2 receptors): Atropine is a competitive antagonist at muscarinic acetylcholine receptors, principally the M2 subtype found in the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes. By blocking the normal vagal inhibitory tone on the heart, atropine increases heart rate and can improve atrioventricular conduction. Because it works by removing parasympathetic inhibition rather than direct sympathetic stimulation, its effect depends on the level of existing vagal tone, and it may be less effective in denervated hearts such as after transplantation.
  73. The correct answer is C (Glycopyrrolate is a quaternary ammonium compound that does not cross the blood-brain barrier): Glycopyrrolate is a quaternary ammonium antimuscarinic, meaning it is charged and lipid-insoluble, so it does not readily cross the blood-brain barrier or the placenta. Atropine is a tertiary amine that crosses the blood-brain barrier freely and can cause central anticholinergic effects such as confusion or restlessness, particularly in elderly patients. Both drugs are effective antisialogogues, but glycopyrrolate is often preferred when central side effects are a concern.
  74. The correct answer is D (Sedation and amnesia through central antimuscarinic action): Hyoscine hydrobromide is a tertiary amine antimuscarinic with high lipid solubility, allowing it to cross the blood-brain barrier readily and produce central effects including sedation, amnesia and occasionally paradoxical excitement or hallucinations. It reduces salivary and bronchial secretions peripherally in a similar way to atropine, but its central sedative profile makes it useful as a premedicant, unlike the peripherally restricted glycopyrrolate. Caution is needed in elderly patients due to the risk of central anticholinergic confusion.
  75. The correct answer is B (Tachycardia and mild hypokalaemia from intracellular potassium shift): Although salbutamol is selective for beta-2 receptors, higher systemic doses can cause some beta-1 cross-stimulation causing tachycardia, and beta-2 stimulation of skeletal muscle sodium-potassium ATPase pumps drives potassium into cells, producing mild hypokalaemia. This is the same mechanism exploited therapeutically when nebulised salbutamol is used as an adjunct treatment for hyperkalaemia. Monitoring heart rate and serum potassium is sensible with repeated high-dose nebulised salbutamol.
  76. The correct answer is E (Competitive antagonism of muscarinic receptors reducing bronchoconstrictor tone): Ipratropium bromide is a quaternary antimuscarinic that blocks acetylcholine at M3 muscarinic receptors on bronchial smooth muscle, reducing the vagally mediated bronchoconstrictor tone. This mechanism is complementary to the beta-2 agonism of salbutamol, which is why the two drugs are often combined in nebulised therapy for additive bronchodilation. Its quaternary structure limits systemic absorption, minimising systemic antimuscarinic side effects such as dry mouth or tachycardia.
  77. The correct answer is A (To replace the physiological cortisol surge normally produced by surgical stress): Chronic exogenous steroid use suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, so the adrenal glands may be unable to mount the normal cortisol surge that occurs in response to the stress of surgery. Supplementary perioperative hydrocortisone replaces this missing physiological response and reduces the risk of an Addisonian crisis, which can present as refractory hypotension. The dose and duration of supplementation depend on the degree of adrenal suppression and the magnitude of surgery.
  78. The correct answer is D (Activation of glucagon receptors increasing intracellular cyclic AMP independent of beta receptors): Glucagon binds to its own Gs-coupled receptor on cardiac myocytes, activating adenylate cyclase and raising intracellular cyclic AMP through a pathway that bypasses the blocked beta-adrenoceptor. This increases heart rate and myocardial contractility despite ongoing beta-blockade, making glucagon a recognised specific antidote in severe beta-blocker overdose. High-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy and lipid emulsion are additional treatments used in refractory cases, particularly with combined calcium channel blocker toxicity.
  79. The correct answer is C (Ondansetron blocks cardiac potassium channels and can prolong the QT interval): Ondansetron, like other 5-HT3 receptor antagonists, can block the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current in cardiac myocytes, prolonging the QT interval in a dose-dependent manner. This effect is more significant with intravenous administration and higher doses, and caution or ECG monitoring is advised in patients with existing QT prolongation, electrolyte disturbance or concurrent QT-prolonging drugs. The manufacturer recommends avoiding rapid intravenous bolus dosing to reduce this risk.
  80. The correct answer is B (At induction of anaesthesia, before the surgical stimulus begins): Dexamethasone has a delayed onset of antiemetic action, thought to relate to its anti-inflammatory and central mechanisms, so it is most effective when given early, typically at induction of anaesthesia. Giving it towards the end of surgery does not allow sufficient time for its antiemetic effect to develop before the patient wakes. It is often combined with a 5-HT3 antagonist such as ondansetron given closer to the end of surgery for a complementary time course of action.
  81. The correct answer is D (Its anticholinergic and antihistaminic actions can cause tachycardia and increase cardiac workload): Cyclizine has combined antihistaminic and anticholinergic properties, which can produce tachycardia and a modest increase in myocardial oxygen demand, an effect that is undesirable in patients with ischaemic heart disease or heart failure. It can also cause a small rise in blood pressure and reduce the beneficial haemodynamic effects of opioids used concurrently. For this reason, ondansetron or dexamethasone are often preferred antiemetics in patients with significant cardiac disease.
  82. The correct answer is A (Extrapyramidal symptoms and worsening of Parkinsonian features): Droperidol is a butyrophenone dopamine D2 receptor antagonist, and blocking central dopaminergic transmission can precipitate extrapyramidal side effects such as dystonia, akathisia or worsening of pre-existing Parkinsonian symptoms. Droperidol also carries a recognised risk of QT prolongation, which led to restrictions on its use and requires ECG assessment in higher-risk patients. Both properties mean droperidol should be used cautiously or avoided in patients with movement disorders or known QT prolongation.
  83. The correct answer is E (Its strongly cationic charge binds and neutralises the negatively charged heparin molecule): Protamine sulfate is a strongly positively charged protein that binds ionically to the negatively charged heparin molecule, forming a stable inactive complex and neutralising its anticoagulant activity. It reverses unfractionated heparin more completely and reliably than low molecular weight heparin, where only partial reversal is achieved. Rapid administration can cause hypotension or, rarely, anaphylactoid reactions, particularly in patients with prior exposure to protamine-containing insulin.
  84. The correct answer is C (Intravenous vitamin K together with prothrombin complex concentrate): Prothrombin complex concentrate rapidly replaces the vitamin K dependent clotting factors depleted by warfarin, providing immediate correction of the INR within minutes, while intravenous vitamin K restores the liver's own synthesis of these factors over several hours to sustain the reversal. Fresh frozen plasma is a slower and less effective alternative, requiring larger volumes and carrying a higher risk of fluid overload. Protamine reverses heparin, not warfarin, and has no role in this scenario.
  85. The correct answer is B (Intravenous magnesium sulfate given promptly regardless of serum magnesium level): This drug-induced polymorphic ventricular tachycardia associated with QT prolongation and hypokalaemia is consistent with torsades de pointes, and intravenous magnesium sulfate is the first-line treatment even if serum magnesium is normal. Correction of hypokalaemia and withdrawal of the causative QT-prolonging drugs, in this case ondansetron and droperidol, are also essential. If the patient becomes haemodynamically unstable, synchronised cardioversion or defibrillation should be performed without delay according to the advanced life support algorithm.
  86. The correct answer is A (At least 72 hours after the last dose, assuming normal renal function): Current UK guidance recommends withholding apixaban for at least 72 hours, equivalent to three elimination half-lives, before performing a spinal or epidural block in a patient with normal renal function, to minimise the risk of spinal or epidural haematoma. This interval should be extended in renal impairment, since apixaban has partial renal clearance and accumulation prolongs its anticoagulant effect. Anticoagulant timing around neuraxial blockade should always be individualised and cross-checked against current national guidance.
  87. The correct answer is E (A minimum of 12 hours after the last prophylactic dose): Association of Anaesthetists guidance recommends a minimum interval of 12 hours after a prophylactic dose of low molecular weight heparin before performing neuraxial blockade, extending to 24 hours after a treatment dose. This interval allows the anticoagulant effect to diminish sufficiently to reduce the risk of spinal or epidural haematoma, a rare but potentially devastating complication. Subsequent doses of low molecular weight heparin should similarly be delayed for a defined interval after the block or catheter removal.
  88. The correct answer is C (Combined alpha-1 antagonism and non-selective beta antagonism): Labetalol blocks both alpha-1 and beta-1 and beta-2 adrenoceptors, though its beta-blocking activity is considerably more potent than its alpha-blocking activity. The alpha-1 blockade produces vasodilation and a fall in systemic vascular resistance, while the beta blockade prevents the reflex tachycardia that would otherwise accompany vasodilation alone. This combined profile makes it particularly useful for controlling acute hypertension without causing marked tachycardia.
  89. The correct answer is B (300 mg given as an intravenous bolus after the third shock): The Resuscitation Council UK adult advanced life support algorithm recommends amiodarone 300 mg intravenously after the third defibrillation attempt in shockable rhythms such as ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia, followed by a further 150 mg after the fifth shock if the rhythm remains shockable. Amiodarone is a class III antiarrhythmic that prolongs the cardiac action potential and refractory period, helping to terminate refractory ventricular arrhythmias. It should be flushed with a compatible fluid as it can interact with plastic giving sets.
  90. The correct answer is D (Beta-1 receptor downregulation and desensitisation with sustained high-dose stimulation): Sustained exposure to high-dose beta-1 agonism causes receptor downregulation and desensitisation, meaning further increases in dobutamine dose produce progressively smaller increases in contractility, which is reflected as a plateau on the dose-response curve. This phenomenon limits the clinical usefulness of escalating beta-agonist doses in prolonged inotropic support and is one reason alternative agents such as milrinone, which act independently of the beta receptor, may be added or substituted. It also explains why prolonged high-dose catecholamine infusions can become progressively less effective over time.
  91. The correct answer is C (It stabilises the cardiac membrane and suppresses early afterdepolarisations): Magnesium suppresses the early afterdepolarisations that trigger torsades de pointes, largely by reducing calcium influx through L-type calcium channels and stabilising the cardiac cell membrane, even though it does not substantially shorten the QT interval itself. This makes intravenous magnesium sulfate effective first-line treatment for torsades de pointes regardless of the underlying cause or the measured serum magnesium concentration. Correcting any underlying electrolyte disturbance and withdrawing causative QT-prolonging drugs remain important concurrent measures.
  92. The correct answer is A (Excessive inhibition of the sodium-potassium ATPase pump increasing intracellular calcium): Digoxin inhibits the sodium-potassium ATPase pump, and at toxic concentrations this excessive inhibition raises intracellular sodium and, via the sodium-calcium exchanger, intracellular calcium, promoting delayed afterdepolarisations and triggered arrhythmias. Digoxin also increases vagal tone, which combined with calcium overload can produce a wide range of arrhythmias including bradycardia, heart block and ventricular ectopy. Hypokalaemia increases the risk of toxicity because potassium competes with digoxin for the same binding site on the pump.
  93. The correct answer is E (Class II beta-blockade combined with class III potassium channel blockade): Sotalol is unusual among antiarrhythmics in combining non-selective beta-adrenoceptor blockade, a Vaughan Williams class II action, with class III potassium channel blockade that prolongs the cardiac action potential and refractory period. This combination provides both rate control and antiarrhythmic activity, but the class III action also prolongs the QT interval and carries a recognised risk of torsades de pointes, particularly at higher doses or with electrolyte disturbance. Regular ECG monitoring is recommended during initiation and dose titration.
  94. The correct answer is B (Activation of alpha-1 adrenoceptors causing Gq-mediated vascular smooth muscle contraction): Phenylephrine is a selective alpha-1 adrenoceptor agonist with negligible beta activity. Alpha-1 receptor stimulation activates the Gq protein-phospholipase C pathway, raising intracellular calcium and causing arteriolar smooth muscle contraction. This increases systemic vascular resistance and blood pressure, often with a reflex bradycardia from baroreceptor activation. It has minimal direct effect on myocardial contractility, unlike beta-1 agonists.
  95. The correct answer is D (Activation of beta-2 adrenoceptors increasing cyclic AMP and causing smooth muscle relaxation): Salbutamol is a selective beta-2 adrenoceptor agonist. Receptor activation stimulates adenylate cyclase via a Gs protein, raising intracellular cyclic AMP, which activates protein kinase A and reduces intracellular calcium in airway smooth muscle, producing relaxation and bronchodilation. It also has minor effects reducing mediator release from mast cells but this is not its primary mechanism. Beta-2 selectivity limits but does not eliminate beta-1 mediated tachycardia at higher doses.
  96. The correct answer is A (Noradrenaline has predominant alpha-1 activity with modest beta-1 effect, while adrenaline has significant beta-2 activity): Noradrenaline acts mainly on alpha-1 adrenoceptors producing vasoconstriction, with some beta-1 inotropic and chronotropic effect but negligible beta-2 activity. Adrenaline activates alpha-1, beta-1 and beta-2 receptors, and at lower infusion rates beta-2 mediated vasodilation can predominate, sometimes reducing diastolic blood pressure. This distinction underlies why noradrenaline is preferred as first-line vasopressor in septic shock, as it raises systemic vascular resistance more reliably. Adrenaline's mixed beta activity also increases the risk of tachyarrhythmia and lactic acidosis.
  97. The correct answer is E (Direct alpha-1 agonism with additional indirect noradrenaline release from nerve terminals): Metaraminol acts predominantly as a direct alpha-1 adrenoceptor agonist, causing vasoconstriction and raising blood pressure, but it also has an indirect sympathomimetic action by displacing noradrenaline from presynaptic nerve terminals. This mixed mechanism makes it effective for obstetric hypotension, with maintained uteroplacental perfusion at clinical doses. Unlike ephedrine, its predominantly direct action makes it less prone to tachyphylaxis with repeated dosing. Reflex bradycardia can occur due to baroreceptor-mediated vagal activation.
  98. The correct answer is C (Depletion of presynaptic noradrenaline stores available for indirect release by ephedrine): Ephedrine acts largely as an indirect sympathomimetic, displacing noradrenaline from presynaptic vesicles to act on postsynaptic adrenoceptors, with a smaller direct agonist component. With repeated dosing, presynaptic noradrenaline stores become progressively depleted, reducing the amount available for release and producing tachyphylaxis. This differs from phenylephrine and metaraminol, which act predominantly directly and are less susceptible to this effect. Switching to a direct-acting agent is a reasonable strategy when tachyphylaxis to ephedrine develops.
  99. The correct answer is A (Noradrenaline): Noradrenaline is a potent alpha-1 agonist with additional beta-1 activity, producing a steep rise in mean arterial pressure through progressive vasoconstriction as the infusion rate increases. Dobutamine is a predominantly beta-1 agonist inotrope that increases cardiac output but has comparatively little effect on systemic vascular resistance and therefore produces only a modest rise in mean arterial pressure. The curve depicted therefore best matches noradrenaline's dose-dependent vasopressor profile. This distinction underlies the choice of noradrenaline as first-line vasopressor in septic shock.
  100. The correct answer is D (V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle activating the Gq-phospholipase C pathway): Vasopressin acts on V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle, coupled to Gq proteins, which activate phospholipase C and raise intracellular calcium, causing vasoconstriction independent of adrenoceptors. This makes it effective as an adjunct in catecholamine-resistant vasodilatory shock, since it works even when adrenoceptors are downregulated. Its V2 receptor activity in the renal collecting duct produces the separate antidiuretic effect used in diabetes insipidus. Unlike catecholamines, vasopressin does not directly increase myocardial contractility.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Increased myocardial contractility and cardiac output via predominant beta-1 agonism): Dobutamine is a synthetic catecholamine with predominant beta-1 agonist activity, increasing myocardial contractility and cardiac output with a comparatively modest effect on heart rate at low to moderate doses. It has some beta-2 activity that can cause mild peripheral vasodilation and a fall in systemic vascular resistance, distinguishing it from purely vasopressor agents. This makes it useful in cardiogenic shock where improving cardiac output is the priority rather than raising afterload. Tachyarrhythmia can occur at higher infusion rates.
  2. The correct answer is E (Inhibition of phosphodiesterase-3 increasing intracellular cyclic AMP in cardiac and vascular tissue): Milrinone is a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor that prevents breakdown of cyclic AMP, increasing intracellular calcium in cardiac myocytes to enhance contractility, and reducing calcium in vascular smooth muscle to cause vasodilation. Because its action is independent of adrenoceptors, it remains effective in patients on chronic beta-blocker therapy. Its combined inotropic and vasodilator (inodilator) effect can cause significant hypotension, particularly with a loading dose, and it has a comparatively long half-life that is prolonged further in renal impairment.
  3. The correct answer is C (Continue bisoprolol perioperatively, including on the morning of surgery, at the usual dose): Established beta-blocker therapy should be continued perioperatively, as abrupt withdrawal can precipitate rebound tachycardia, hypertension and myocardial ischaemia due to upregulated beta-receptor sensitivity. Continuing the usual dose maintains haemodynamic stability and avoids the increased risk of adverse cardiac events associated with discontinuation. Newly starting beta-blockade immediately before surgery is generally avoided as it has been associated with an increased risk of stroke and hypotension. Dose should not be arbitrarily altered without a clear indication.
  4. The correct answer is B (Concurrent intravenous beta-blocker therapy, due to the risk of severe bradycardia and cardiac arrest): Verapamil is a non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker with significant negative chronotropic and negative inotropic effects through blockade of L-type calcium channels in the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes. Combining it intravenously with a beta-blocker produces profound, potentially fatal bradycardia, atrioventricular block or asystole due to synergistic depression of nodal conduction and myocardial contractility. Oral combinations are used with caution but intravenous co-administration is avoided. Verapamil is also contraindicated in decompensated heart failure and Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome with atrial fibrillation.
  5. The correct answer is A (Hypothyroidism or hyperthyroidism due to the drug's high iodine content and effects on thyroid hormone metabolism): Amiodarone contains a substantial amount of iodine per tablet and structurally resembles thyroxine, which can cause either hypothyroidism through a Wolff-Chaikoff-like effect or hyperthyroidism through iodine-induced excess hormone synthesis or thyroiditis. Thyroid function should be checked before elective surgery in patients on long-term amiodarone, as untreated thyroid dysfunction increases perioperative cardiovascular risk. Corneal deposits, pulmonary fibrosis and hepatotoxicity are also recognised amiodarone toxicities but are not related to its iodine content specifically. Amiodarone's long half-life means these effects can persist for months after discontinuation.
  6. The correct answer is D (An extremely short plasma half-life of only a few seconds due to rapid cellular uptake): Adenosine is rapidly taken up by erythrocytes and endothelial cells and metabolised, giving it a plasma half-life of only a few seconds. A rapid bolus followed immediately by a saline flush ensures an adequate concentration reaches the atrioventricular node before degradation, transiently blocking conduction and interrupting re-entrant supraventricular tachycardia. Its ultra-short duration of action also means side effects such as flushing, chest tightness and transient asystole are brief. Adenosine should be used cautiously in asthma as it can precipitate bronchospasm.
  7. The correct answer is C (Glycopyrrolate is a quaternary ammonium compound that does not readily cross the placenta): Glycopyrrolate is a quaternary ammonium antimuscarinic that is poorly lipid soluble and does not readily cross the placenta or the blood-brain barrier, reducing fetal exposure and maternal central effects. Atropine is a tertiary amine that crosses both barriers more readily, potentially causing fetal tachycardia and maternal central anticholinergic effects such as sedation or confusion. Glycopyrrolate is also a more potent and longer-acting antisialogogue than atropine, which is useful before airway procedures. Neither drug is metabolised by plasma cholinesterase.
  8. The correct answer is E (Muscarinic (M2) receptor stimulation by accumulated acetylcholine slowing sinoatrial node discharge): Neostigmine inhibits acetylcholinesterase, increasing acetylcholine at both nicotinic neuromuscular junctions and cardiac muscarinic M2 receptors. Unopposed muscarinic stimulation at the sinoatrial node reduces the rate of spontaneous depolarisation, producing bradycardia as shown on the rhythm strip. This is why neostigmine is co-administered with an antimuscarinic such as glycopyrrolate or atropine to block the cardiac muscarinic effect. Without this cover, profound bradycardia or even asystole can occur.
  9. The correct answer is A (Hyoscine crosses the blood-brain barrier and can cause sedation or a central anticholinergic syndrome): Hyoscine is a tertiary amine antimuscarinic that readily crosses the blood-brain barrier, producing sedation, amnesia and occasionally confusion or a central anticholinergic syndrome, particularly in elderly patients. This central effect is undesirable when a cooperative, communicative patient is required for an awake procedure. Glycopyrrolate, being a quaternary ammonium compound, lacks significant central nervous system penetration and is therefore preferred as an antisialogogue in this context. Hyoscine can also cause a paradoxical bradycardia at low doses through a central vagal effect.
  10. The correct answer is B (Beta-2 mediated stimulation of the sodium-potassium ATPase pump driving potassium into cells): Beta-2 adrenoceptor activation stimulates the sodium-potassium ATPase pump in skeletal muscle and other tissues, driving potassium intracellularly and lowering serum potassium concentration without any net change in total body potassium. This effect is dose-dependent and more pronounced with high-dose or repeated nebulised salbutamol. Tremor and tachycardia are additional recognised beta-2 and beta-1 mediated side effects respectively. The hypokalaemia is usually transient and resolves once treatment is reduced, but potassium should be monitored in patients receiving high cumulative doses.
  11. The correct answer is D (Competitive antagonism of muscarinic receptors reducing vagally mediated bronchoconstriction): Ipratropium bromide is a quaternary ammonium antimuscarinic that competitively blocks muscarinic receptors, mainly M3, on airway smooth muscle, reducing vagally mediated bronchoconstriction and mucus secretion. Its quaternary structure limits systemic absorption when given by inhalation, minimising systemic anticholinergic side effects. It has an onset slower than salbutamol but a complementary mechanism, which is why the two are combined in acute exacerbations of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease and severe asthma. It has no direct effect on cyclic AMP or leukotriene pathways.
  12. The correct answer is C (Continue the usual dose and give supplementary intravenous hydrocortisone to cover the physiological stress response): Long-term corticosteroid therapy suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, impairing the normal stress-induced cortisol response to surgery. The usual maintenance dose should be continued and supplemented with intravenous hydrocortisone perioperatively, with the dose and duration tailored to the magnitude of surgical stress, to prevent an Addisonian crisis manifesting as hypotension refractory to fluids and vasopressors. Abruptly stopping corticosteroids is dangerous and inappropriate. Dexamethasone is sometimes used for antiemetic prophylaxis but does not replace formal steroid cover for major surgery.
  13. The correct answer is E (Dexamethasone has a delayed onset of antiemetic action, requiring several hours to become maximally effective): Dexamethasone's antiemetic mechanism is not fully understood but is thought to involve central prostaglandin inhibition and reduced tryptophan availability for serotonin synthesis, an effect that takes several hours to become maximally effective. It is therefore given at induction rather than at the end of surgery, unlike ondansetron, which has a rapid onset and is often given towards the end of the procedure closer to expected emergence. This timing strategy maximises antiemetic cover during the highest-risk postoperative period. Dexamethasone also has a long plasma half-life supporting early administration.
  14. The correct answer is D (Ondansetron can prolong the QT interval through hERG potassium channel blockade and is contraindicated in congenital long QT syndrome): Ondansetron, a 5-HT3 receptor antagonist, blocks the rapid component of the delayed rectifier potassium current encoded by the hERG gene, which can prolong the QT interval in a dose-dependent manner. In patients with known congenital long QT syndrome, current UK prescribing information formally lists ondansetron as contraindicated rather than merely requiring caution, so an alternative antiemetic should be used. Other antiemetics such as cyclizine or dexamethasone are preferred in this population, and any coexisting electrolyte abnormality such as hypokalaemia or hypomagnesaemia should still be corrected as part of routine care. This is distinct from acquired or borderline QT prolongation, where a cautious risk-benefit approach with ECG monitoring may still be reasonable.
  15. The correct answer is B (Cyclizine has anticholinergic and antihistaminic properties that can cause tachycardia and increased myocardial oxygen demand): Cyclizine is a histamine H1 receptor antagonist with additional anticholinergic activity, which can cause tachycardia and, in susceptible patients, increased myocardial oxygen demand and reduced diastolic filling time. In a patient with severe ischaemic heart disease and recent infarction, this makes it relatively less desirable than agents such as ondansetron, which lack significant cardiovascular anticholinergic effects. Cyclizine can also cause drowsiness and, at high doses, has been associated with haemodynamic instability when given rapidly intravenously. Its antihistaminic action is unrelated to serotonin or dopamine receptor pathways.
  16. The correct answer is A (QT interval prolongation and extrapyramidal symptoms through dopamine D2 receptor antagonism): Droperidol is a butyrophenone that antagonises central dopamine D2 receptors in the chemoreceptor trigger zone, providing antiemetic efficacy, but the same dopaminergic blockade can cause extrapyramidal symptoms such as dystonia and akathisia. Droperidol also blocks the hERG potassium channel and can prolong the QT interval in a dose-dependent manner, which led to a black box warning restricting its use and requiring lower doses with ECG monitoring in higher risk patients. It has no relevant beta-adrenoceptor or muscarinic activity. Low-dose droperidol at antiemetic doses is generally considered to carry a low absolute arrhythmic risk in patients without other risk factors.
  17. The correct answer is E (Vasopressin, because V1 receptor effects plateau once receptors approach maximal occupancy): Vasopressin acts on V1 receptors on vascular smooth muscle via a Gq-coupled pathway independent of adrenoceptors, and its pressor effect reaches a ceiling once receptor occupancy approaches saturation, so further dose increases produce little additional rise in mean arterial pressure. Noradrenaline acts on alpha-1 and beta-1 adrenoceptors and its dose-response relationship remains comparatively linear over the clinically used range, producing the continuously rising curve. This ceiling effect is one reason vasopressin is used as an adjunct rather than sole agent in catecholamine-resistant septic shock. It also allows a catecholamine-sparing strategy that may reduce arrhythmia risk.
  18. The correct answer is C (Agonism at central and peripheral alpha-2 adrenoceptors reducing noradrenaline release): Dexmedetomidine is a highly selective alpha-2 adrenoceptor agonist that acts centrally, particularly at the locus coeruleus, to reduce noradrenaline release, producing sedation resembling natural sleep, anxiolysis and analgesia with relative preservation of respiratory drive. Peripheral alpha-2 activation contributes to a reduction in heart rate and blood pressure, which is useful in an awake craniotomy where haemodynamic stability and a cooperative, arousable patient are both required. Clonidine shares this alpha-2 mechanism but has lower receptor selectivity and a longer duration of action. Bradycardia and hypotension are recognised dose-dependent side effects.
  19. The correct answer is D (Increased heart rate, contractility and atrioventricular conduction velocity through raised intracellular cyclic AMP): Beta-1 adrenoceptors are coupled to Gs proteins, and their activation stimulates adenylate cyclase, raising cyclic AMP and activating protein kinase A. This increases calcium influx through L-type calcium channels, producing positive chronotropic, inotropic and dromotropic effects, namely increased heart rate, contractility and atrioventricular conduction velocity respectively. These effects underlie the use of beta-1 agonists such as dobutamine in low cardiac output states. Beta-1 blockade, conversely, is used therapeutically to reduce myocardial oxygen demand in ischaemic heart disease.
  20. The correct answer is E (Baroreceptor-mediated reflex increase in vagal tone secondary to the rise in blood pressure): Phenylephrine is a pure alpha-1 agonist with no direct cardiac chronotropic activity. The rapid rise in blood pressure it produces is detected by carotid sinus and aortic arch baroreceptors, triggering a reflex increase in vagal (parasympathetic) tone and reduction in sympathetic outflow to the sinoatrial node, causing reflex bradycardia. This is a physiological compensatory response rather than a direct pharmacological effect of the drug on the heart. This reflex bradycardia can be pronounced enough to require an antimuscarinic if excessive.
  21. The correct answer is B (500 micrograms intramuscular adrenaline, repeated after five minutes if there is no improvement): Current UK Resuscitation Council guidance recommends 500 micrograms of adrenaline given intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh as first-line treatment for anaphylaxis in an adult, repeated after five minutes if there is no clinical improvement. Intramuscular administration provides a wide margin of safety and reliable absorption without the need for immediate intravenous access. Intravenous adrenaline is reserved for refractory anaphylaxis or cardiac arrest and should only be given by those experienced in its use, in small titrated boluses, due to the risk of arrhythmia and severe hypertension. Adrenaline should never be delayed while awaiting intravenous access.
  22. The correct answer is D (Stop the infusion, aspirate residual drug from the cannula, and infiltrate the area with phentolamine): Noradrenaline extravasation causes intense local vasoconstriction that can lead to tissue ischaemia and necrosis if untreated. The infusion should be stopped immediately, as much residual drug as possible aspirated from the cannula before removal, and the affected area infiltrated with phentolamine, an alpha-adrenoceptor antagonist that reverses the vasoconstriction and limits tissue damage. A warm compress can be used as an adjunct to promote vasodilation but is not sufficient alone. Vigorous flushing risks spreading the extravasated drug further into surrounding tissue and is not recommended.
  23. The correct answer is A (Binding to and potentiating antithrombin, accelerating inhibition of thrombin and factor Xa): Unfractionated heparin binds to antithrombin via a specific pentasaccharide sequence, inducing a conformational change that markedly accelerates antithrombin's inhibition of thrombin (factor IIa) and factor Xa, among other clotting factors. Its anticoagulant effect is monitored using the activated clotting time during cardiopulmonary bypass because of the very high doses required, and its short half-life allows reversal with protamine at the end of bypass. Low molecular weight heparins act mainly through antithrombin-mediated factor Xa inhibition with relatively less effect on thrombin. Direct thrombin inhibitors such as bivalirudin work independently of antithrombin.
  24. The correct answer is B (Stop warfarin five days preoperatively and bridge with therapeutic dose low molecular weight heparin): Warfarin should typically be stopped around five days before elective surgery to allow the international normalised ratio to normalise, since its effect on vitamin K-dependent clotting factors takes several days to resolve. Patients at high risk of thromboembolism, such as those with a mechanical mitral valve, require bridging anticoagulation with therapeutic dose low molecular weight heparin, which is then stopped at an appropriate interval before surgery and restarted postoperatively until warfarin is therapeutic again. Protamine reverses heparin, not warfarin; vitamin K or prothrombin complex concentrate are used for warfarin reversal if urgently needed. Direct oral anticoagulants are not a substitute for a mechanical valve indication.
  25. The correct answer is D (A minimum of eighteen hours after the last dose, at prophylactic (not treatment) dosing): Current UK regional anaesthesia guidance recommends withholding rivaroxaban for at least eighteen hours before spinal or epidural needle insertion when used at prophylactic doses in a patient with normal renal function. Treatment-dose rivaroxaban requires a substantially longer interval, commonly around forty eight hours, reflecting the higher drug level that must clear before the procedure. Longer intervals still apply in renal impairment because rivaroxaban has significant renal clearance. The same interval principle applies to catheter removal, which should also be timed to a trough drug level. Direct oral anticoagulants should generally be withheld rather than bridged around regional techniques given their predictable pharmacokinetics.
  26. The correct answer is B (Blockade of cardiac fast sodium channels by bupivacaine widening the QRS complex and precipitating ventricular tachycardia): Bupivacaine has high affinity for cardiac fast sodium channels and dissociates from them slowly, so systemic toxicity causes marked slowing of phase 0 depolarisation, QRS widening and re-entrant ventricular arrhythmias including ventricular tachycardia. This cardiotoxicity is disproportionate to its local anaesthetic potency compared with agents such as lidocaine. Management includes cessation of the local anaesthetic, airway support and intravenous lipid emulsion therapy in addition to standard advanced life support. Lidocaine and other class I antiarrhythmics should be avoided as they can worsen sodium channel blockade, whereas amiodarone remains the recommended antiarrhythmic if one is required during management of local anaesthetic systemic toxicity.
  27. The correct answer is E (Intravenous amiodarone 300 mg loading dose given over 10 to 60 minutes): For a haemodynamically stable patient with broad complex tachycardia, current resuscitation guidance recommends intravenous amiodarone 300 mg given as a loading dose over 10 to 60 minutes through a large vein, followed by a maintenance infusion if the rhythm responds. Amiodarone is a class III antiarrhythmic that prolongs the cardiac action potential and refractory period through potassium channel blockade, with additional sodium and calcium channel effects, making it effective across a broad range of arrhythmias. Adenosine and verapamil are not appropriate first-line agents for broad complex tachycardia of uncertain origin as they can cause deterioration if the rhythm is ventricular in origin. Synchronised cardioversion is reserved for haemodynamically unstable patients or those failing pharmacological treatment.
  28. The correct answer is C (Hypokalaemia, which increases digoxin binding to the sodium-potassium ATPase pump and enhances its effect): Digoxin exerts its effect by inhibiting the myocardial sodium-potassium ATPase pump, and potassium competes with digoxin for the same binding site on this pump. Hypokalaemia, commonly caused by diuretic therapy, increases digoxin binding and potentiates its inhibitory effect, precipitating toxicity even at therapeutic digoxin concentrations, manifesting as nausea, visual disturbance and a range of arrhythmias including ventricular ectopy. Hypomagnesaemia and hypercalcaemia also increase the risk of digoxin toxicity but the potassium interaction is the most clinically significant and well-established mechanism. Management includes correcting potassium, treating arrhythmias, and considering digoxin-specific antibody fragments in severe toxicity.
  29. The correct answer is D (Intravenous glucagon, which increases intracellular cyclic AMP independently of the beta-adrenoceptor): Glucagon activates its own Gs-coupled receptor on cardiac myocytes, increasing intracellular cyclic AMP through a pathway independent of the beta-adrenoceptor, thereby increasing heart rate and contractility even when beta-receptors are blocked. This makes it a useful specific antidote in severe beta-blocker overdose refractory to atropine and standard resuscitation. High-dose insulin-euglycaemia therapy and intravenous lipid emulsion are also used in severe cases, particularly with combined calcium channel blocker toxicity or lipophilic agents. Naloxone and flumazenil have no role in reversing beta-blocker toxicity.
  30. The correct answer is A (Vasopressin is not recommended as a routine substitute for or addition to adrenaline during cardiac arrest): Although vasopressin acts on V1 receptors to produce vasoconstriction independent of adrenoceptors and has theoretical advantages in acidotic or catecholamine-depleted states, current UK Resuscitation Council advanced life support guidance does not recommend its routine use as a substitute for, or in addition to, adrenaline during cardiac arrest, as trials have not demonstrated a clear outcome benefit. Adrenaline therefore remains the standard vasopressor used during resuscitation according to the algorithm. Vasopressin does retain an established role as an adjunct vasopressor in catecholamine-resistant septic shock outside the cardiac arrest setting. Atropine is no longer recommended for asystole or pulseless electrical activity in current guidance.
  31. The correct answer is B (A minimum of twelve hours after the last prophylactic dose): Current UK regional anaesthesia guidance recommends a minimum interval of twelve hours between the last prophylactic dose of low molecular weight heparin and epidural catheter removal, to reduce the risk of epidural haematoma from residual anticoagulant effect. A therapeutic dose of low molecular weight heparin requires a longer interval, typically twenty-four hours, before catheter removal. The next dose of low molecular weight heparin should similarly be delayed for a defined interval, usually at least four hours, after catheter removal. These timing rules apply equally to insertion and removal, since both carry a risk of bleeding into the epidural space.
  32. The correct answer is B (Residual volume): Residual volume. Total lung capacity is the sum of tidal volume, inspiratory reserve volume, expiratory reserve volume and residual volume. Residual volume is the only one of these four subdivisions that cannot be exhaled voluntarily, so a simple spirometer trace can never measure it directly. It must instead be derived from helium dilution, nitrogen washout or whole-body plethysmography, and functional residual capacity (expiratory reserve volume plus residual volume) is calculated indirectly using one of these same techniques.
  33. The correct answer is D (0.28): 0.28. The Bohr equation states that the dead space to tidal volume ratio equals (PaCO2 minus mixed expired PCO2) divided by PaCO2, which here gives (5.3 minus 3.8) divided by 5.3, equal to approximately 0.28. This reflects the proportion of each tidal breath that does not participate in gas exchange, since dead space gas dilutes the carbon dioxide concentration of mixed expired gas relative to arterial blood. A rising Bohr dead space fraction in a ventilated patient can indicate increased alveolar dead space from conditions such as pulmonary embolism or reduced cardiac output.
  34. The correct answer is A (It acts as an oxygen reservoir that buffers arterial oxygen tension during apnoea): It acts as an oxygen reservoir that buffers arterial oxygen tension during apnoea. Functional residual capacity holds the alveolar oxygen store that continues to supply the pulmonary circulation once ventilation ceases, so a larger functional residual capacity delays the fall in arterial oxygen tension during apnoea. Obesity reduces functional residual capacity through reduced chest wall compliance and cephalad diaphragmatic displacement, shrinking this reservoir well before induction even begins.
