Combined with increased oxygen consumption in obese patients, this markedly shortens the time available for intubation before desaturation occurs.
  35. The correct answer is E (Rightward shift, reducing haemoglobin affinity for oxygen and enhancing tissue unloading): Rightward shift, reducing haemoglobin affinity for oxygen and enhancing tissue unloading. Increased hydrogen ion concentration, raised temperature and increased 2,3-diphosphoglycerate concentration, all present in exercising muscle, each independently shift the curve to the right as shown, raising the P50. This reduced haemoglobin affinity favours release of oxygen from haemoglobin exactly where it is needed most, at the low partial pressures of oxygen found in metabolically active tissue. This coordinated response is an important physiological adaptation that increases oxygen delivery during exercise without requiring any increase in cardiac output alone.
  36. The correct answer is C (The V/Q ratio is higher at the apex than at the base): The V/Q ratio is higher at the apex than at the base. Although both ventilation and perfusion increase down the upright lung due to gravity, perfusion increases proportionally far more than ventilation, so the V/Q ratio actually falls progressively from apex to base. At the apex, relatively high ventilation combined with relatively low perfusion produces a high V/Q ratio, approaching the physiological dead space end of the spectrum. This apical West zone 1 physiology explains why apical alveolar dead space is greater than basal dead space in the upright, spontaneously breathing lung.
  37. The correct answer is B (Leftward shift, reducing P50 and impairing oxygen release to the tissues): Leftward shift, reducing P50 and impairing oxygen release to the tissues. Depleted 2,3-diphosphoglycerate in stored red cells increases haemoglobin's affinity for oxygen, shifting the dissociation curve to the left and lowering the P50. This means transfused cells bind oxygen more tightly in the lungs but release it less readily at the tissues immediately after transfusion. Red cell 2,3-diphosphoglycerate concentration is gradually restored over hours to days once the transfused cells are back in circulation, so this effect is usually transient but can be clinically relevant in a critically ill patient receiving a large transfusion.
  38. The correct answer is D (Chronic elevation of cerebrospinal fluid bicarbonate normalises CSF pH despite persistent hypercapnia): Chronic elevation of cerebrospinal fluid bicarbonate normalises CSF pH despite persistent hypercapnia. In chronic hypercapnia, the choroid plexus increases active transport of bicarbonate into the cerebrospinal fluid, buffering the fall in pH that carbon dioxide would otherwise cause. Because central chemoreceptors respond to CSF pH rather than to carbon dioxide tension directly, this renal-like renal compensation blunts their sensitivity to further rises in carbon dioxide. This is precisely why some patients with chronic type 2 respiratory failure become more dependent on hypoxic drive from peripheral chemoreceptors to maintain ventilation.
  39. The correct answer is A (Carotid body peripheral chemoreceptors responding to reduced arterial oxygen tension): Carotid body peripheral chemoreceptors responding to reduced arterial oxygen tension. The carotid bodies, located at the bifurcation of the common carotid arteries, are the principal peripheral chemoreceptors responsible for detecting hypoxaemia and driving compensatory hyperventilation. In patients with chronic hypercapnia whose central chemoreceptor sensitivity to carbon dioxide has been blunted, this hypoxic drive via the carotid bodies can become the dominant stimulus maintaining ventilation. This is the physiological basis for cautious, titrated oxygen therapy in such patients, since abolishing hypoxaemia can remove their principal remaining ventilatory drive.
  40. The correct answer is E (Plateauing (flattening) of both the inspiratory and expiratory flow limbs): Plateauing (flattening) of both the inspiratory and expiratory flow limbs. A fixed upper airway obstruction, such as tracheal stenosis, limits flow equally throughout both inspiration and expiration because the obstruction does not vary with intrathoracic pressure changes. This produces the characteristic box-like flattening of both limbs of the flow-volume loop shown, in contrast to variable extrathoracic or intrathoracic obstructions, which selectively flatten only the inspiratory or only the expiratory limb respectively. Recognising this pattern preoperatively is important, as it warns the anaesthetist of a difficult airway with limited ability to compensate for further narrowing.
  41. The correct answer is B (Alveolar dead space, since ventilation continues without perfusion): Alveolar dead space, since ventilation continues without perfusion. When a pulmonary embolus obstructs blood flow to an area that continues to be ventilated normally, that lung unit is ventilated but not perfused, which by definition constitutes alveolar dead space. This differs from anatomical dead space, which is fixed and confined to the conducting airways regardless of perfusion. A sudden rise in alveolar dead space, reflected as a widened arterial to end-tidal carbon dioxide gradient, is a classic and clinically useful sign of acute pulmonary embolism under anaesthesia.
  42. The correct answer is C (Isovolumetric contraction): Isovolumetric contraction. Once the mitral valve closes, the left ventricle is a sealed chamber, and because the aortic valve has not yet opened, ventricular pressure rises steeply while volume remains fixed, defining isovolumetric contraction. Ejection begins only once left ventricular pressure exceeds aortic diastolic pressure, at which point the aortic valve opens and volume starts to fall. This phase is brief but generates the rapid rate of pressure rise, sometimes measured as dP/dt max, that is used as one index of myocardial contractility.
  43. The correct answer is A (Mitral and tricuspid valve closure): Mitral and tricuspid valve closure. The first heart sound arises from the near-simultaneous closure of the atrioventricular valves, the mitral and tricuspid valves, as ventricular pressure exceeds atrial pressure at the start of systole. This is distinct from the second heart sound, which is produced by closure of the semilunar aortic and pulmonary valves at the end of systole. Splitting of either heart sound can provide clinically useful information about relative timing differences between the left and right sides of the heart.
  44. The correct answer is D (Preload, due to reduced ventricular end-diastolic filling): Preload, due to reduced ventricular end-diastolic filling. Spinal anaesthesia blocks sympathetic outflow to the venous capacitance vessels, causing venodilation and pooling of blood peripherally, which reduces venous return to the heart. By the Frank-Starling mechanism, reduced end-diastolic ventricular filling, that is reduced preload, directly reduces stroke volume and hence cardiac output. This is the principal mechanism of spinal-induced hypotension, and it is why fluid loading or a vasopressor with venoconstrictor activity is often used to counteract it.
  45. The correct answer is B (Afterload): Afterload. Afterload is the resistance or load the ventricle must overcome to eject blood, and in aortic stenosis the fixed, narrowed valve orifice creates a markedly increased afterload for the left ventricle. Over time this leads to compensatory concentric left ventricular hypertrophy as the ventricle adapts to generate higher systolic pressures. Anaesthetic management in severe aortic stenosis must avoid sudden reductions in systemic vascular resistance, since the fixed obstruction prevents the usual compensatory increase in cardiac output.
  46. The correct answer is E (The loop widens, with increased end-diastolic volume and increased stroke volume): The loop widens, with increased end-diastolic volume and increased stroke volume. A fluid bolus increases venous return and therefore left ventricular end-diastolic volume, shifting the bottom right corner of the pressure-volume loop further to the right as shown. Provided the ventricle remains on the ascending limb of the Frank-Starling curve, this increased preload produces a wider loop and a greater stroke volume for a similar end-systolic volume. This graphical relationship is the direct physiological expression of the Frank-Starling law of the heart at the level of the individual cardiac cycle.
  47. The correct answer is C (Contractility): Contractility. Contractility describes the intrinsic force of ventricular contraction at any given preload and afterload, and inotropic agents such as dobutamine increase contractility by enhancing intracellular calcium availability during systole. This shifts the entire Frank-Starling curve upwards and to the left, producing a greater stroke volume for any given end-diastolic volume, distinct from simply increasing preload or reducing afterload. This makes contractility a key therapeutic target in cardiogenic shock, where the heart is failing to generate adequate output despite adequate filling.
  48. The correct answer is A (Sinoatrial node): Sinoatrial node. The sinoatrial node contains specialised pacemaker cells with the fastest intrinsic rate of spontaneous phase 4 depolarisation in the heart, making it the normal primary pacemaker regardless of autonomic innervation. This is why a transplanted, fully denervated heart continues to beat rhythmically at its own intrinsic rate, since the sinoatrial node's automaticity does not depend on nervous input to function, only to modulate its rate. Should the sinoatrial node fail, subsidiary pacemakers such as the atrioventricular node or Purkinje fibres can take over, but at progressively slower intrinsic rates.
  49. The correct answer is D (Atrioventricular node): Atrioventricular node. The atrioventricular node conducts impulses slowly compared with the rest of the conduction system, producing the deliberate delay seen as the PR interval on the surface ECG. This delay allows atrial systole to complete ventricular filling before ventricular depolarisation and contraction begin, optimising end-diastolic volume and hence stroke volume. Disease or drugs that further slow atrioventricular nodal conduction, such as excessive beta-blockade, can prolong this delay into first-degree or higher-grade atrioventricular block.
  50. The correct answer is B (Large fibre diameter and abundant gap junctions permitting rapid impulse spread): Large fibre diameter and abundant gap junctions permitting rapid impulse spread. Purkinje fibres have a large diameter, which reduces internal resistance to current flow, combined with numerous gap junctions at intercalated discs that allow rapid electrical coupling between adjacent cells. This combination gives Purkinje fibres the fastest conduction velocity in the heart, considerably faster than ordinary ventricular myocardium. Rapid, near-simultaneous activation of the ventricles via this network is essential for coordinated, mechanically efficient ventricular contraction rather than a slow, disorganised wave of depolarisation.
  51. The correct answer is C (Diastole, because myocardial compression of intramural vessels during systole impedes flow): Diastole, because myocardial compression of intramural vessels during systole impedes flow. During systole, contraction of the left ventricular myocardium compresses the intramural coronary vessels, particularly in the subendocardium, sharply reducing flow despite high aortic pressure at that time. Once the ventricle relaxes in diastole, this extravascular compression is released and coronary flow rises accordingly, meaning that most left ventricular perfusion occurs during diastole. This is why tachycardia, which shortens diastole disproportionately, can precipitate myocardial ischaemia even without a fall in mean arterial pressure.
  52. The correct answer is A (Absent P waves with an irregularly irregular ventricular response and chaotic baseline): Absent P waves with an irregularly irregular ventricular response and chaotic baseline. Atrial fibrillation is characterised by disorganised, chaotic atrial electrical activity with no discrete, organised P waves, and conduction through the atrioventricular node occurs at irregular intervals, producing an irregularly irregular ventricular rhythm as shown. The absent, coordinated atrial contraction also explains the loss of a palpable atrial contribution to the venous pulsation on examination. This distinguishes atrial fibrillation from atrial flutter, which shows regular sawtooth flutter waves, and from second-degree heart block, which shows a fixed relationship between P waves and QRS complexes before a dropped beat.
  53. The correct answer is E (Local metabolic factors, particularly adenosine released during myocardial hypoxia): Local metabolic factors, particularly adenosine released during myocardial hypoxia. Coronary autoregulation is predominantly a local, metabolic phenomenon, with adenosine released from hypoxic myocardium acting as a potent local vasodilator that matches coronary flow closely to metabolic demand. This local control operates largely independently of systemic autonomic reflexes across a wide range of perfusion pressures, allowing flow to remain relatively stable despite moderate changes in coronary perfusion pressure. Beyond the limits of autoregulation, however, coronary flow becomes pressure dependent, which is why severe hypotension risks myocardial ischaemia regardless of local metabolic demand.
  54. The correct answer is D (Decreased parasympathetic outflow together with increased sympathetic outflow): Decreased parasympathetic outflow together with increased sympathetic outflow. A fall in arterial pressure reduces baroreceptor afferent firing to the medullary vasomotor and cardioinhibitory centres, which reflexively reduces parasympathetic (vagal) outflow to the heart while increasing sympathetic outflow to the heart and peripheral vasculature. The resulting tachycardia, increased contractility and peripheral vasoconstriction act together to raise cardiac output and systemic vascular resistance, helping to restore arterial pressure towards normal. This baroreceptor reflex is the fastest-acting compensatory mechanism following acute haemorrhage, occurring within seconds.
  55. The correct answer is B (Tachycardia and peripheral vasoconstriction redistributing flow to vital organs): Tachycardia and peripheral vasoconstriction redistributing flow to vital organs. Early compensation for moderate haemorrhage is driven predominantly by baroreceptor-mediated sympathetic activation, producing tachycardia to help maintain cardiac output and selective peripheral vasoconstriction that redirects blood flow away from skin, muscle and splanchnic circulation towards the brain and heart. This redistribution allows arterial pressure and vital organ perfusion to be maintained despite a significant reduction in circulating volume. As blood loss progresses beyond this compensatory range, these mechanisms become overwhelmed and hypotension develops.
  56. The correct answer is C (Class II without hypotension): Class II without hypotension. ATLS classifies haemorrhagic shock using blood loss, heart rate, pulse pressure and mental state, and this presentation, with blood loss of 750 to 1500 mL, tachycardia, a narrowed pulse pressure and mild anxiety but a preserved systolic blood pressure, is classic for class II haemorrhage. Systolic blood pressure typically remains normal in class II shock because compensatory tachycardia and vasoconstriction maintain perfusion pressure despite ongoing volume loss. Recognising these early compensated signs is important, since waiting for hypotension to develop, which characterises class III and IV shock, represents a delayed and more dangerous presentation.
  57. The correct answer is A (Helium dilution or whole-body plethysmography): Helium dilution or whole-body plethysmography. Residual volume, and the functional residual capacity and total lung capacity that incorporate it, cannot be measured by simple spirometry because residual volume by definition cannot be exhaled. Helium dilution allows calculation of lung volume from the dilution of a known quantity of an inert, non-absorbed gas, while whole-body plethysmography uses Boyle's law relating pressure and volume changes within a sealed chamber. Both techniques are used clinically to characterise restrictive and obstructive lung disease, where residual volume and functional residual capacity are often abnormal.
  58. The correct answer is D (Mobitz type II second-degree block): Mobitz type II second-degree block. Mobitz type II block is characterised by a constant PR interval on conducted beats with sudden, unpredictable failure of atrioventricular conduction producing a dropped QRS complex, without the progressive PR prolongation seen in Wenckebach block. This pattern indicates disease below the atrioventricular node, typically within the His-Purkinje system, and carries a significant risk of sudden progression to complete heart block. For this reason, Mobitz type II block generally warrants permanent pacemaker insertion even in asymptomatic patients, unlike the usually benign Mobitz type I pattern.
  59. The correct answer is E (Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, a local response to alveolar hypoxia): Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, a local response to alveolar hypoxia. Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction is a local reflex in which reduced alveolar oxygen tension causes constriction of the pulmonary arterioles supplying that alveolar unit, diverting blood flow away from poorly ventilated or collapsed lung towards better-ventilated regions. This response is unique to the pulmonary circulation, where hypoxia causes vasoconstriction rather than the vasodilatation seen in most systemic vascular beds. During one-lung ventilation, this reflex helps limit shunt through the non-ventilated lung, although volatile anaesthetic agents can partially attenuate the response.
  60. The correct answer is B (Dorsal respiratory group in the medulla oblongata): Dorsal respiratory group in the medulla oblongata. The dorsal respiratory group within the medulla oblongata contains the intrinsic pacemaker-like neurons that generate the basic automatic rhythm of inspiration, with expiration normally occurring passively as these neurons fall silent. Pontine centres, including the pneumotaxic and apneustic centres, modulate the rate and depth of this basic rhythm rather than generating it themselves. Peripheral and central chemoreceptor input then further adjusts ventilation according to arterial blood gas status, layered on top of this fundamental medullary rhythm.
  61. The correct answer is C (Leftward shift, impairing oxygen release to the tissues): Leftward shift, impairing oxygen release to the tissues. Carbon monoxide not only reduces the oxygen-carrying capacity of haemoglobin by occupying binding sites but also increases the oxygen affinity of the remaining haem groups, shifting the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve of the unaffected haemoglobin to the left. This combination severely impairs oxygen release at the tissues even when arterial oxygen saturation as measured by conventional pulse oximetry may appear falsely reassuring. This is one reason carbon monoxide poisoning can cause severe tissue hypoxia disproportionate to the measured oxygen saturation, and why co-oximetry is required for accurate assessment.
  62. The correct answer is A (Atrial contraction): Atrial contraction. The 'a' wave of the jugular venous pulse corresponds to the rise in right atrial pressure produced by atrial systole immediately before ventricular contraction begins. It is followed by the 'c' wave, reflecting tricuspid valve bulging during early ventricular isovolumetric contraction, and the 'v' wave, reflecting atrial filling against a closed tricuspid valve during ventricular systole. An absent 'a' wave, as seen in atrial fibrillation, or giant 'a' waves, as seen in tricuspid stenosis or complete heart block, are clinically useful examination findings.
  63. The correct answer is D (Frank-Starling law of the heart): Frank-Starling law of the heart. The Frank-Starling law states that, within physiological limits, increasing ventricular end-diastolic volume increases the force of contraction and hence stroke volume, due to more optimal overlap of actin and myosin filaments at greater sarcomere stretch. This intrinsic mechanism allows the heart to automatically match output to venous return without requiring changes in heart rate or contractility. It is distinct from the Bainbridge reflex, which describes a heart rate increase in response to atrial stretch, and from the Bowditch effect, which describes rate-dependent changes in contractility.
  64. The correct answer is B (Marked hypotension combined with elevated left ventricular filling pressure): Marked hypotension combined with elevated left ventricular filling pressure. Coronary perfusion pressure equals aortic diastolic pressure minus left ventricular end-diastolic pressure, so any combination of falling diastolic pressure and rising ventricular filling pressure directly narrows this gradient. In a patient with ischaemic heart disease and limited coronary flow reserve, this combination can precipitate subendocardial ischaemia even without overt tachycardia or a fall in mean arterial pressure alone. Maintaining adequate diastolic blood pressure is therefore a key anaesthetic priority in patients with significant coronary artery disease.
  65. The correct answer is E (Atrioventricular node): Atrioventricular node. Progressive prolongation of the PR interval followed by eventual complete failure of conduction is characteristic of disease affecting the atrioventricular node itself, as shown by the block indicated on the pathway. Because the atrioventricular node is the sole normal electrical connection between atria and ventricles, progressive dysfunction here produces exactly this pattern of worsening delay culminating in complete heart block. This differs from disease below the node, in the bundle of His or bundle branches, which more typically produces sudden, unheralded conduction failure rather than gradual prolongation.
  66. The correct answer is C (Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system activation): Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system activation. Reduced renal perfusion pressure and reduced sodium delivery to the macula densa after haemorrhage stimulate renin release from the juxtaglomerular apparatus, generating angiotensin II and subsequently aldosterone. Angiotensin II causes systemic vasoconstriction while aldosterone promotes renal sodium and water retention, together supporting circulating volume and blood pressure over hours to days following the initial insult. This hormonal response acts more slowly than the immediate baroreceptor reflex but is important for sustained compensation after significant blood loss.
  67. The correct answer is A (Closing capacity rises with age and can come to exceed functional residual capacity): Closing capacity rises with age and can come to exceed functional residual capacity. Closing capacity, the lung volume at which small airways in dependent regions begin to close during expiration, increases progressively with age due to loss of lung elastic recoil. When closing capacity rises to exceed functional residual capacity, which occurs earlier in the supine and anaesthetised state, airway closure occurs during normal tidal breathing, promoting atelectasis, ventilation-perfusion mismatch and hypoxaemia. This explains why elderly and obese patients are at particular risk of desaturation under general anaesthesia even without any additional respiratory pathology.
  68. The correct answer is D (Rightward shift, reducing haemoglobin affinity and enhancing tissue oxygen release): Rightward shift, reducing haemoglobin affinity and enhancing tissue oxygen release. Increased temperature reduces the affinity of haemoglobin for oxygen, shifting the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve to the right and increasing the P50. This physiological response is advantageous during fever or exercise, as it facilitates greater oxygen unloading at the tissues, which often have increased local temperature and metabolic demand. Conversely, hypothermia shifts the curve to the left, which can impair tissue oxygen delivery despite an apparently adequate arterial oxygen saturation reading.
  69. The correct answer is B (Carbon dioxide diffuses into CSF, forms carbonic acid, lowering CSF pH and stimulating chemoreceptors): Carbon dioxide diffuses into CSF, forms carbonic acid, lowering CSF pH and stimulating chemoreceptors. Carbon dioxide crosses the blood-brain barrier freely, unlike hydrogen ions, and once in the cerebrospinal fluid it combines with water to form carbonic acid, which dissociates to release hydrogen ions and lower CSF pH. Central chemoreceptors respond directly to this fall in CSF pH rather than to arterial carbon dioxide tension itself, providing an indirect but highly sensitive mechanism for detecting hypercapnia. This explains the characteristically rapid ventilatory response to acute rises in arterial carbon dioxide compared with the much slower renal compensation for a primary metabolic acidosis.
  70. The correct answer is C (Dominant resting parasympathetic (vagal) tone slowing sinoatrial node discharge): Dominant resting parasympathetic (vagal) tone slowing sinoatrial node discharge. At rest, the autonomic nervous system exerts a net inhibitory effect on the sinoatrial node, with parasympathetic (vagal) tone normally dominating over sympathetic tone, slowing the observed heart rate well below the node's intrinsic firing rate of approximately 100 beats per minute. This vagal predominance is particularly marked in fit, well-trained individuals, who often show pronounced resting bradycardia as a result. Pharmacological or surgical vagal blockade, as with atropine or cardiac transplantation respectively, characteristically unmasks a heart rate closer to this higher intrinsic sinoatrial rate.
  71. The correct answer is C (Functional residual capacity): functional residual capacity equals expiratory reserve volume plus residual volume and represents the lung volume remaining at the end of a normal tidal expiration. It is markedly increased in COPD due to gas trapping and loss of elastic recoil, contributing to hyperinflation. This raised FRC increases the work of breathing and is the classic spirometric finding in COPD. Vital capacity and total lung capacity are calculated differently and do not isolate this specific relationship.
  72. The correct answer is E (Affinity decreases, curve shifts to the right): acidosis and hypercapnia produce the Bohr effect, reducing haemoglobin's affinity for oxygen and shifting the dissociation curve to the right. This facilitates oxygen unloading at the tissues, where raised carbon dioxide and hydrogen ion concentrations reflect increased metabolic demand. The shift is an appropriate physiological response that matches oxygen delivery to tissue requirements. A leftward shift, by contrast, would impair oxygen release at the tissues, which is not what occurs with acidosis.
  73. The correct answer is A (Dead space ventilation, V/Q ratio approaching infinity): a pulmonary embolus abolishes perfusion to a segment while ventilation continues, producing alveolar dead space with a V/Q ratio tending toward infinity. This increases physiological dead space and widens the gradient between arterial and end-tidal carbon dioxide, a useful bedside clue to embolism. Shunt is the opposite abnormality, where perfusion continues but ventilation is lost, giving a V/Q ratio approaching zero. Normal matching would not explain the clinical deterioration described.
  74. The correct answer is D (Peripheral chemoreceptor hypoxic drive is blunted by hyperoxia): in chronic type 2 respiratory failure, central chemoreceptors become relatively desensitised to carbon dioxide because cerebrospinal fluid bicarbonate has been buffered over time. Ventilatory drive then depends more heavily on hypoxic stimulation of the peripheral chemoreceptors in the carotid bodies. Administering supplemental oxygen removes this hypoxic drive and can precipitate hypoventilation, alongside a lesser contribution from worsening V/Q mismatch. Central chemoreceptors do not become suddenly more sensitive in this scenario.
  75. The correct answer is B (Curve shifts left, P50 decreases, haemoglobin holds oxygen more tightly): hypothermia increases haemoglobin's affinity for oxygen, shifting the dissociation curve to the left and reducing the P50, the partial pressure of oxygen at which haemoglobin is 50% saturated. This means haemoglobin binds oxygen more tightly and releases it less readily at the tissues for any given tissue oxygen tension. The curve shown demonstrates this leftward shift compared with the normal 37°C curve. A rightward shift would instead reflect factors such as acidosis, hypercapnia or fever.
  76. The correct answer is A (Isovolumetric contraction): isovolumetric contraction occurs after the mitral valve closes and before the aortic valve opens, with both atrioventricular and semilunar valves closed. Ventricular pressure rises steeply while ventricular volume remains fixed, since no blood can enter or leave the chamber. This phase ends once ventricular pressure exceeds aortic pressure, opening the aortic valve and beginning ejection. Isovolumetric relaxation is the analogous phase occurring after ejection, not before it.
  77. The correct answer is C (Increased venous return raises preload, lengthening sarcomeres via Frank-Starling): the Frank-Starling mechanism describes how increased venous return raises ventricular end-diastolic volume, stretching cardiac sarcomeres toward a more optimal length for force generation. This intrinsic property increases stroke volume and cardiac output without requiring any change in heart rate or contractility. The small rise in central venous pressure reflects the modest increase in preload needed to produce a disproportionately large gain in stroke volume in a previously underfilled heart. Afterload and sympathetic withdrawal are not the primary mechanisms described here.
  78. The correct answer is B (Small fibre diameter and slow calcium-dependent action potentials): atrioventricular node cells are small in diameter, have relatively few gap junctions, and generate action potentials predominantly through slow calcium channel activity rather than fast sodium channels. These features together produce the characteristic conduction delay through the node. This delay is physiologically important, allowing atrial contraction to complete ventricular filling before ventricular depolarisation and contraction begin. The AV node does not have the fastest intrinsic pacemaker rate, which belongs to the sinoatrial node.
  79. The correct answer is E (Systolic myocardial compression of intramural vessels obstructs flow): during ventricular systole, the contracting myocardium compresses the intramural coronary vessels running through it, sharply raising extravascular resistance and reducing flow, an effect most pronounced in the subendocardium. Consequently, the majority of left ventricular coronary blood flow occurs during diastole, when the myocardium relaxes and this compression is relieved. Autoregulation is present in the coronary circulation, and systolic aortic pressure is not too low to perfuse the myocardium under normal conditions.
  80. The correct answer is D (Flattening of both inspiratory and expiratory limbs, plateau pattern): a fixed upper airway obstruction, whether intrathoracic or extrathoracic, restricts airflow throughout the entire respiratory cycle because the degree of narrowing does not vary with intrathoracic pressure changes. This produces the characteristic plateau flattening of both the inspiratory and expiratory limbs of the flow-volume loop, as shown. A variable extrathoracic obstruction instead selectively flattens only the inspiratory limb, while a scooped concave expiratory limb is typical of obstructive small airway disease rather than a fixed central obstruction.
  81. The correct answer is C (Baroreceptor-mediated sympathetic activation increases heart rate and vasoconstriction): the earliest compensatory response to moderate haemorrhage is baroreceptor-mediated, as falling arterial pressure reduces baroreceptor afferent firing from the carotid sinus and aortic arch, triggering reflex sympathetic activation. This produces tachycardia and peripheral vasoconstriction that help maintain mean arterial pressure before overt hypotension develops. Mechanisms such as the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone axis, antidiuretic hormone release and transcapillary refill act over a much slower timescale of hours rather than minutes.
  82. The correct answer is A (Reduced oxygen reserve leads to more rapid desaturation): functional residual capacity acts as the body's principal oxygen reservoir during periods of apnoea. In term pregnancy, diaphragmatic splinting by the gravid uterus reduces functional residual capacity while oxygen consumption simultaneously rises due to increased metabolic demand. This combination substantially shortens the safe apnoea time before desaturation occurs, which is why thorough preoxygenation is particularly important before rapid sequence induction in obstetric patients. An increased oxygen reserve would have the opposite, protective effect.
  83. The correct answer is E (Gamma chains in fetal haemoglobin bind 2,3-DPG less avidly): fetal haemoglobin is composed of two alpha and two gamma globin chains, and these gamma chains bind 2,3-diphosphoglycerate less avidly than the beta chains found in adult haemoglobin. Reduced 2,3-DPG binding increases haemoglobin's oxygen affinity, shifting the dissociation curve to the left. This higher affinity facilitates the transfer of oxygen from the maternal to the fetal circulation across the placenta, where oxygen tensions are relatively low. Fetal haemoglobin does not contain four beta chains, which would instead describe an abnormal haemoglobin variant.
  84. The correct answer is B (Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction diverting flow to ventilated lung): alveolar hypoxia within the collapsed, non-ventilated lung triggers hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, a local response that constricts pulmonary vasculature in poorly ventilated regions. This diverts blood flow away from the shunted lung toward the well-ventilated dependent lung, reducing the overall shunt fraction and limiting the fall in arterial oxygenation. This protective mechanism is attenuated by volatile anaesthetic agents and vasodilators, which can worsen oxygenation during one-lung ventilation. Increased bronchial artery flow does not meaningfully compensate for this shunt.
  85. The correct answer is D (Absent discrete P waves with an irregularly irregular ventricular rate): atrial fibrillation is characterised by chaotic, disorganised atrial electrical activity that replaces discrete P waves with an undulating fibrillatory baseline. The atrioventricular node conducts these impulses irregularly, producing the classic irregularly irregular ventricular rhythm seen on the rhythm strip and felt at the pulse. This distinguishes atrial fibrillation from atrial flutter, which shows a regular sawtooth pattern, and from heart block, which shows fixed or progressively changing relationships between P waves and QRS complexes.
  86. The correct answer is A (Central chemoreceptors in the medulla responding to cerebrospinal fluid pH): central chemoreceptors located near the ventral surface of the medulla respond to changes in hydrogen ion concentration within the cerebrospinal fluid. Carbon dioxide diffuses freely across the blood brain barrier and is hydrated to carbonic acid, lowering CSF pH and stimulating these chemoreceptors. This mechanism provides the dominant drive to breathe under normal day-to-day conditions, well before peripheral hypoxic drive becomes significant. Peripheral chemoreceptors in the carotid bodies respond mainly to hypoxaemia rather than to modest rises in carbon dioxide.
  87. The correct answer is B (Closure of the aortic valve at the end of ventricular ejection): the dicrotic notch on the arterial pressure waveform reflects a transient rebound in pressure caused by brief backflow of blood toward the closing aortic valve. This occurs as ventricular pressure falls below aortic pressure at the end of ejection, marking the transition from systole into diastole. It is a useful landmark for identifying end-systole on invasive arterial waveforms during anaesthesia. Opening of the mitral valve occurs later, during early diastolic filling, and does not produce this feature.
  88. The correct answer is C (A fixed outflow gradient limits the compensatory increase in stroke volume): in severe aortic stenosis, the fixed valvular obstruction restricts the ventricle's ability to increase stroke volume in response to a fall in systemic vascular resistance, unlike a structurally normal heart where afterload reduction readily augments ejection. A sudden fall in systemic vascular resistance can therefore precipitate profound hypotension, because the ventricle cannot compensate by increasing flow across the stenotic orifice. Preload remains relevant in this condition and heart rate compensation does not reliably restore cardiac output without risk of further compromising diastolic filling.
  89. The correct answer is D (Purkinje fibres): Purkinje fibres have the fastest conduction velocity of any tissue within the cardiac conduction system, owing to their large fibre diameter and abundant gap junctions. This rapid conduction allows near-simultaneous depolarisation of the ventricular myocardium, which is essential for coordinated and efficient ventricular contraction. The sinoatrial node instead has the fastest intrinsic rate of spontaneous depolarisation, which is a distinct property from conduction velocity. The atrioventricular node, by contrast, has the slowest conduction velocity of the system.
  90. The correct answer is E (Progressive PR prolongation with a dropped beat, warranting cardiology review): the trace demonstrates Mobitz type 1, or Wenckebach, second degree heart block, in which the PR interval progressively lengthens with each successive beat until a P wave fails to conduct and a QRS complex is dropped. This pattern generally carries a relatively benign prognosis compared with Mobitz type 2 block, but preoperative cardiology review remains sensible to exclude progression and to guide perioperative monitoring. It should not be mistaken for complete heart block, in which there is no consistent relationship between P waves and QRS complexes at all.
  91. The correct answer is A (Subendocardium experiences the highest extravascular compressive forces during systole): the subendocardial layer of the myocardium lies closest to the high-pressure ventricular cavity and therefore experiences the greatest intramural compressive force during systole, which impedes perfusion of this layer specifically. In left ventricular hypertrophy, diastolic filling time is often reduced and wall tension is increased, further narrowing the pressure gradient that drives subendocardial blood flow. This combination makes the subendocardium the most vulnerable layer to ischaemia, even when the epicardial coronary arteries themselves are angiographically normal.
  92. The correct answer is C (Peripheral vasoconstriction redistributing blood flow to vital organs): compensated haemorrhagic shock is characterised by intense sympathetically mediated peripheral vasoconstriction, which redistributes blood flow away from skin, muscle and splanchnic circulation toward the heart and brain. This preserves systolic blood pressure in the earlier stages of shock but produces the clinical signs described, including cool peripheries, narrowed pulse pressure and delayed capillary refill. Recognising these peripheral signs is clinically important because blood pressure alone can be falsely reassuring in a patient who is still actively compensating for ongoing blood loss.
  93. The correct answer is B (Vital capacity): vital capacity is defined as the maximum volume of air that can be forcibly exhaled following a maximal inspiration, and it comprises tidal volume, inspiratory reserve volume and expiratory reserve volume combined. It specifically excludes residual volume, the air that remains in the lungs after maximal expiration and cannot be voluntarily expelled. Functional residual capacity and total lung capacity are calculated differently and include residual volume within their definitions, which distinguishes them from vital capacity.
  94. The correct answer is E (Right shift, decreasing haemoglobin affinity, facilitating oxygen unloading): pyrexia shifts the oxyhaemoglobin dissociation curve to the right, reducing haemoglobin's affinity for oxygen at any given partial pressure of oxygen. This facilitates oxygen unloading at the tissue level, where increased metabolic activity and heat production during fever raise local oxygen demand. This response mirrors the rightward shifts produced by acidosis and hypercapnia, all of which act to better match oxygen delivery to increased tissue requirements. A leftward shift would instead impair oxygen release at a time when demand is increased.
  95. The correct answer is D (Atrioventricular node): first degree heart block reflects delayed but ultimately complete conduction through the atrioventricular node, so every atrial impulse still reaches the ventricles and a QRS complex follows every P wave. The PR interval is prolonged beyond 0.2 seconds because conduction is slowed specifically at this site within the pathway shown, rather than at the sinoatrial node or within the more distal conduction system. Delay at the bundle branches or Purkinje fibres would instead be expected to widen the QRS complex rather than prolong the PR interval alone.
  96. The correct answer is B (Gravity increases both ventilation and perfusion toward dependent regions, but perfusion more): in the upright lung, gravity increases both ventilation and blood flow toward the dependent, lower regions, but the gradient is considerably steeper for perfusion than for ventilation. This produces a relatively high V/Q ratio at the apex and a relatively low V/Q ratio at the dependent base, a pattern classically described using West's zones. Positioning under anaesthesia, including the lateral decubitus position, alters this normal distribution further and contributes to intraoperative V/Q mismatch.
  97. The correct answer is A (Medullary respiratory centres, including the dorsal and ventral respiratory groups): the fundamental rhythm of breathing is generated by neuronal groups located within the medulla, with the dorsal respiratory group primarily driving inspiration and the ventral respiratory group contributing to both inspiration and active expiration. This intrinsic rhythm persists even in the absence of higher cortical input, although it is modulated by pontine centres and by chemoreceptor feedback regarding oxygen and carbon dioxide levels. The cerebellum and hypothalamus are not responsible for generating the basic respiratory rhythm itself.
  98. The correct answer is D (Atrial contraction against a closed or resistant tricuspid valve pathway): the a wave on the central venous pressure trace corresponds to right atrial contraction, which raises atrial pressure transiently as blood is forced toward the right ventricle across the tricuspid valve. In tricuspid stenosis this wave becomes exaggerated because the atrium must generate higher pressure to overcome the narrowed valve orifice. The a wave is characteristically absent in atrial fibrillation, since coordinated atrial contraction is lost, which helps distinguish these two conditions on the trace.
  99. The correct answer is C (Increased intracellular calcium availability enhancing cross-bridge cycling directly): dobutamine acts predominantly as a positive inotrope through beta-1 adrenoceptor stimulation, which increases intracellular calcium availability within cardiac myocytes and enhances the strength of actin-myosin cross-bridge cycling. This increases contractility and stroke volume independently of the Frank-Starling mechanism, allowing greater ejection to occur at the same end-diastolic volume rather than requiring further ventricular stretch. This is distinct from mechanisms that improve stroke volume through increased preload or reduced afterload alone.
  100. The correct answer is E (End-systolic volume decreases, the loop widens and stroke volume increases): reducing afterload lowers the pressure the ventricle must generate to open the aortic valve and eject blood, allowing the ventricle to reach a lower end-systolic volume for the same preload and contractility. On the pressure-volume loop shown, this is seen as the loop widening along the volume axis, reflecting an increased stroke volume compared with the baseline loop. This principle underlies the use of vasodilator therapy to improve forward flow in patients with impaired ventricular function.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (An accessory pathway bypassing the normal AV nodal delay): Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome results from an additional accessory conduction pathway, often referred to as the bundle of Kent, that connects the atria directly to the ventricles and bypasses the physiological delay normally imposed by the atrioventricular node. This allows part of the ventricular myocardium to be pre-excited early, producing the characteristic delta wave and shortened PR interval seen on the ECG. This accessory pathway also predisposes affected patients to re-entrant supraventricular tachyarrhythmias.
  2. The correct answer is A (Tachycardia shortens diastole, reducing the time available for coronary perfusion): the majority of left ventricular coronary blood flow occurs during diastole, and tachycardia disproportionately shortens diastolic time relative to systolic time as heart rate increases. When this reduced perfusion time is combined with a fixed critical stenosis that limits maximal achievable flow, together with the increased myocardial oxygen demand produced by both tachycardia and hypertension, the balance between oxygen supply and demand is tipped toward ischaemia. Coronary autoregulation, while impaired distal to a critical stenosis, is not entirely abolished under normal resting conditions.
  3. The correct answer is D (Transcapillary refill drawing interstitial fluid into the vascular space): over a timescale of minutes to hours following haemorrhage, reduced capillary hydrostatic pressure alters the balance of Starling forces in favour of movement of protein-poor interstitial fluid into the vascular compartment. This process, known as transcapillary refill, helps restore circulating volume but simultaneously dilutes the remaining red cell mass, producing a gradual fall in haematocrit that becomes evident over this delayed timescale rather than immediately after the bleeding episode. Increased erythropoietin production would take days to weeks to have any measurable effect.
  4. The correct answer is C (Functional residual capacity falls, sometimes below closing capacity): obesity reduces both chest wall and lung compliance, and the increased abdominal mass load on the diaphragm, particularly pronounced in the supine position, substantially reduces functional residual capacity. When functional residual capacity falls below closing capacity, small airways close during normal tidal breathing, producing ventilation-perfusion mismatch and predisposing the patient to rapid oxygen desaturation during induction of anaesthesia. Total lung capacity and vital capacity are affected to a lesser degree than functional residual capacity in this context.
  5. The correct answer is E (Chronic anaemia increases red cell 2,3-DPG, reducing haemoglobin affinity): the chronic anaemia characteristic of sickle cell disease stimulates increased production of 2,3-diphosphoglycerate within red cells. This compound binds preferentially to deoxygenated haemoglobin, reducing its affinity for oxygen and shifting the dissociation curve to the right. This compensatory adaptation improves oxygen unloading and delivery to the tissues, helping to offset the reduced oxygen-carrying capacity resulting from the lower circulating haemoglobin concentration seen in chronic sickle cell disease.
  6. The correct answer is A (Increased alveolar dead space, widening the arterial to end-tidal CO2 gradient): temporarily clamping the pulmonary artery abolishes perfusion to a lung segment that continues to be ventilated, creating alveolar dead space with a V/Q ratio approaching infinity in that segment. Ventilated but unperfused alveoli do not participate in gas exchange, which widens the normal difference between arterial and end-tidal carbon dioxide as measured by capnography. This is the opposite abnormality to a shunt, in which perfusion continues to alveoli that are not ventilated.
  7. The correct answer is B (Rising arterial carbon dioxide tension detected mainly via central chemoreceptors): during voluntary breath-holding, arterial carbon dioxide tension rises progressively and is the dominant stimulus that eventually terminates the breath-hold. This rise is sensed predominantly by central chemoreceptors responding to the resulting fall in cerebrospinal fluid pH, rather than by peripheral chemoreceptors. Hypoxaemia contributes comparatively little at this early stage in a healthy individual, since oxygen stores are initially well preserved and the peripheral hypoxic drive is a relatively weak stimulus by comparison.
  8. The correct answer is C (The Frank-Starling curve is shifted downward and to the right): in chronic heart failure the Frank-Starling relationship is shifted downward and to the right, meaning that for any given preload, whether expressed as end-diastolic volume or filling pressure, the failing ventricle generates a lower stroke volume than a healthy heart. This reflects impaired intrinsic contractility of the failing myocardium. Because the curve also tends to plateau, further increases in preload beyond the optimal point may fail to improve, or can even worsen, stroke volume due to overstretching of the already dilated ventricle.
  9. The correct answer is B (Myogenic and tubuloglomerular feedback mechanisms adjust afferent arteriolar tone): Renal blood flow and GFR remain relatively constant between mean arterial pressures of approximately 80 and 180 mmHg through intrinsic autoregulation. The myogenic mechanism responds to stretch in the afferent arteriolar wall, contracting smooth muscle when pressure rises and relaxing it when pressure falls, while tubuloglomerular feedback via the macula densa senses distal tubular sodium chloride delivery and adjusts afferent arteriolar resistance accordingly. Below this autoregulatory range, as may occur during spinal-induced hypotension, GFR becomes pressure-dependent and falls. Angiotensin II acts predominantly on the efferent arteriole to preserve GFR when renal perfusion pressure falls, which is a separate protective mechanism from intrinsic autoregulation itself.
  10. The correct answer is D (Filtered glucose load exceeds the proximal tubular transport maximum for reabsorption): Glucose is freely filtered at the glomerulus and almost entirely reabsorbed in the proximal convoluted tubule by sodium-glucose cotransporters, principally SGLT2. This reabsorption has a finite transport maximum, and once the filtered load exceeds this capacity, typically around a plasma glucose of 10 mmol/L, glucose appears in the urine. The glomerular filtration barrier itself does not become abnormally permeable to glucose in hyperglycaemia. Aldosterone acts on sodium and potassium handling in the distal nephron and has no direct effect on proximal tubular glucose transporters.
  11. The correct answer is A (Direct reduction in renal vascular resistance with impaired autoregulation): Volatile anaesthetic agents such as sevoflurane reduce renal vascular resistance directly and blunt the intrinsic autoregulatory response, making renal blood flow more dependent on systemic arterial pressure than in the awake state. This effect is separate from, though compounded by, the reduction in cardiac output and mean arterial pressure that volatile agents also produce. Volatile agents do not cause clinically significant nephrotoxicity at the concentrations used in routine anaesthesia, and modern agents such as sevoflurane are not associated with meaningful antidiuretic hormone release. Efferent arteriolar constriction is a compensatory response to hypoperfusion rather than a direct volatile agent effect.
  12. The correct answer is E (Reduced venous return lowers cardiac output and renal perfusion pressure): High positive end-expiratory pressure raises intrathoracic pressure, which reduces venous return to the right heart and consequently lowers cardiac output. This reduction in cardiac output decreases renal perfusion pressure and activates the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and antidiuretic hormone release as a secondary consequence, not a direct effect of the ventilator itself. Renal sympathetic outflow actually increases rather than decreases in response to reduced venous return, promoting sodium and water retention rather than diuresis. Hypercapnia, if present, tends to cause renal vasoconstriction rather than vasodilation.
  13. The correct answer is C (Acute respiratory acidosis with minimal renal compensation): The panel shown demonstrates a markedly low pH with a very high PaCO2, reflecting acute alveolar hypoventilation from the low ventilator rate in a patient with severe airflow obstruction. The bicarbonate concentration remains only mildly elevated, which is typical of the acute rather than chronic phase of respiratory acidosis, since renal compensation through increased hydrogen ion excretion and bicarbonate reabsorption takes several days to become fully effective. A chronic compensated picture would show a much higher bicarbonate with a less severely deranged pH for the same PaCO2. The near-zero base excess confirms that no significant metabolic component has yet developed.
  14. The correct answer is A (pH depends on the ratio of bicarbonate to dissolved carbon dioxide, not their absolute values): The Henderson-Hasselbalch equation states that pH equals the pKa of the bicarbonate buffer system plus the logarithm of the ratio of bicarbonate concentration to dissolved carbon dioxide. This means that pH is preserved as long as the ratio of these two components remains normal, even if both are elevated or reduced together, which is exactly what occurs during effective metabolic or respiratory compensation. A change in either component alone, without a proportionate change in the other, will shift this ratio and alter pH. The pKa itself is a constant for the bicarbonate system and does not vary with clinical state.
  15. The correct answer is D (An anion gap of 28 mmol/L, indicating a raised anion gap acidosis): The anion gap is calculated as plasma sodium minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate, giving 138 minus (96 plus 14), which equals 28 mmol/L, well above the normal range of approximately 8 to 16 mmol/L. This raised anion gap reflects the accumulation of an unmeasured anion, in this case lactate generated by tissue hypoperfusion in sepsis. A normal anion gap acidosis would instead reflect bicarbonate loss balanced by a rise in chloride, such as in diarrhoea or renal tubular acidosis. The markedly reduced bicarbonate here confirms a significant metabolic acidosis is present, ruling out the low anion gap options.
  16. The correct answer is B (Increased renal bicarbonate reabsorption and increased hydrogen ion excretion): In chronic respiratory acidosis, the kidney compensates over several days by increasing proximal tubular bicarbonate reabsorption and enhancing distal hydrogen ion secretion, partly through increased ammoniagenesis, which generates new bicarbonate that is returned to the plasma. This raises plasma bicarbonate towards a level that partially normalises pH despite the persistently elevated PaCO2. Increased urinary bicarbonate excretion would worsen rather than correct the acidosis and is the opposite of what actually occurs. Aldosterone influences sodium and potassium handling but is not the primary driver of the bicarbonate rise seen in chronic respiratory acidosis compensation.
  17. The correct answer is E (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion causing dilutional hyponatraemia): Subarachnoid haemorrhage is a recognised cause of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion, leading to water retention, dilutional hyponatraemia, inappropriately concentrated urine, and low plasma osmolality in a clinically euvolaemic patient. This distinguishes it from cerebral salt wasting, another recognised complication of subarachnoid haemorrhage, which instead produces clinical hypovolaemia due to genuine renal sodium loss. Diabetes insipidus produces the opposite picture of dilute urine and hypernatraemia from reduced antidiuretic hormone effect. Addisonian crisis would typically also feature hyperkalaemia and hypotension, which are not described here.
  18. The correct answer is C (Tall tented T waves with widening of the QRS complex): Severe hyperkalaemia progressively alters cardiac membrane excitability, initially producing tall tented T waves, and as potassium rises further, widening of the QRS complex, flattening of P waves, and eventually a sine wave pattern preceding cardiac arrest. Intravenous calcium gluconate is given urgently in this situation to stabilise the cardiac membrane and reduce the risk of malignant arrhythmia while other measures lower plasma potassium. Torsades de pointes and prominent U waves are classically associated with hypokalaemia rather than hyperkalaemia. Delta waves reflect an accessory conduction pathway and are unrelated to potassium disturbance.
  19. The correct answer is D (Inadvertent parathyroid gland damage causing reduced parathyroid hormone secretion): The parathyroid glands lie close to the thyroid gland and are at risk of direct injury, devascularisation, or inadvertent removal during total thyroidectomy. Reduced parathyroid hormone secretion leads to hypocalcaemia through decreased bone resorption, decreased renal calcium reabsorption, and decreased activation of vitamin D, producing the neuromuscular irritability seen as Trousseau's sign. Excessive parathyroid hormone would cause hypercalcaemia rather than the hypocalcaemia described here. Calcitonin has only a minor physiological role in calcium homeostasis in humans and does not typically cause clinically significant hypocalcaemia even with excess secretion.
  20. The correct answer is A (High anion gap metabolic acidosis with partial respiratory compensation): The panel shown demonstrates a severely low pH with a markedly reduced bicarbonate, consistent with a significant metabolic acidosis, in this case from accumulation of ketoacids in diabetic ketoacidosis. The low PaCO2 reflects the patient's compensatory hyperventilation, classically described as Kussmaul breathing, which partially offsets the fall in pH by reducing carbon dioxide tension. Calculating the anion gap from the sodium, chloride, and bicarbonate values in this scenario would confirm a markedly raised gap due to the unmeasured ketoacid anions. A normal anion gap acidosis would instead show a proportionate rise in chloride rather than accumulation of ketones.
  21. The correct answer is E (Hypomagnesaemia, since magnesium deficiency impairs renal potassium conservation): Magnesium is required for normal function of the renal outer medullary potassium channel, and magnesium depletion promotes ongoing renal potassium wasting that makes hypokalaemia resistant to potassium replacement alone. Correcting the underlying magnesium deficit is therefore necessary before hypokalaemia can be reliably corrected. Prolonged parenteral nutrition without adequate magnesium supplementation is a recognised cause of this combined deficiency. Hypercalcaemia and hypernatraemia do not typically produce this pattern of refractory hypokalaemia, and hyperphosphataemia has no direct antagonistic effect on potassium channels.
  22. The correct answer is B (Extensive first-pass hepatic metabolism before reaching the systemic circulation): Orally administered morphine is absorbed from the gut and passes via the portal vein through the liver before reaching the systemic circulation, where a substantial proportion is metabolised on this first pass, principally by glucuronidation, before it can exert a systemic effect. This first-pass effect substantially reduces the oral bioavailability of morphine compared with intravenous administration, which bypasses the portal circulation entirely. Gastric absorption of morphine itself is not the limiting step in its reduced oral potency. Renal clearance of unchanged drug and enterohepatic recirculation are real pharmacokinetic phenomena but do not account for the marked difference in oral versus intravenous potency described here.
  23. The correct answer is C (The portal vein supplies most flow while the artery supplies half the oxygen): The portal vein contributes approximately 70 to 75 percent of total hepatic blood flow but, because this blood has already passed through the splanchnic circulation, it delivers only around half of the liver's oxygen supply. The hepatic artery supplies the remaining smaller proportion of total flow but, carrying fully oxygenated blood, contributes the other half of hepatic oxygen delivery. This dual supply, along with the hepatic arterial buffer response that increases arterial flow when portal flow falls, helps protect the liver from ischaemia. The portal vein does not carry fully oxygenated blood, since it has already passed through the gastrointestinal capillary beds.
  24. The correct answer is A (Phase I oxidation followed by phase II conjugation reactions): Phase I hepatic metabolism, predominantly mediated by cytochrome P450 enzymes, introduces or exposes a reactive chemical group through oxidation, reduction, or hydrolysis, often producing a more polar but still potentially active metabolite. Phase II reactions then conjugate this metabolite with a hydrophilic molecule such as glucuronic acid, sulfate, or glycine, greatly increasing water solubility to facilitate renal or biliary excretion. Not all drugs require both phases, and some undergo phase II conjugation directly without prior phase I oxidation, but the sequence described in this stem, oxidation followed by glucuronide conjugation, is the classic phase I then phase II pathway. There is no established phase III hepatic reaction category analogous to phase I and II.
  25. The correct answer is E (Cortisol promotes gluconeogenesis and induces peripheral insulin resistance): Cortisol released during the surgical stress response stimulates hepatic gluconeogenesis by increasing the availability of gluconeogenic substrates from protein and fat breakdown, while simultaneously reducing peripheral glucose uptake by inducing insulin resistance in skeletal muscle and adipose tissue. Together these effects raise circulating glucose concentrations, contributing alongside catecholamines and glucagon to the well recognised phenomenon of postoperative hyperglycaemia even in patients without pre-existing diabetes. Cortisol does not directly stimulate insulin secretion and does not increase insulin receptor sensitivity, both of which would tend to lower rather than raise glucose. Suppression of gluconeogenesis would also be the opposite of cortisol's actual physiological effect.
  26. The correct answer is D (Tachycardia, increased myocardial contractility, and enhanced glycogenolysis): Adrenaline and noradrenaline released during the surgical stress response act on beta-1 adrenergic receptors in the heart to increase heart rate and contractility, and on hepatic and muscle beta-2 receptors to stimulate glycogenolysis and lipolysis, mobilising energy substrates to meet increased metabolic demand. These effects work alongside cortisol and glucagon to produce the characteristic hyperdynamic, hypermetabolic, hyperglycaemic state of the surgical stress response. Catecholamines cause vasoconstriction in most vascular beds rather than generalised vasodilation, and they raise rather than lower blood glucose. Bradycardia and suppressed glycogenolysis are the opposite of the expected catecholamine effect.
  27. The correct answer is B (Hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis with respiratory compensation): Persistent vomiting of gastric contents in pyloric stenosis causes loss of hydrogen and chloride ions, generating a hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis, while volume depletion drives aldosterone-mediated renal potassium wasting that produces the accompanying hypokalaemia. The panel shown demonstrates a raised pH with a markedly elevated bicarbonate and positive base excess, alongside low chloride and low potassium, all consistent with this classic biochemical picture. The mildly raised PaCO2 reflects a degree of respiratory compensation, since hypoventilation partially offsets the alkalosis by retaining carbon dioxide. This pattern is distinct from a metabolic acidosis, which would show a low rather than high bicarbonate and pH.
  28. The correct answer is C (Propylthiouracil and propranolol to reduce hormone synthesis and conversion): Thyroid storm is a life-threatening exacerbation of thyrotoxicosis that can be precipitated by the physiological stress of surgery and anaesthesia in an unoptimised patient. Propylthiouracil blocks new thyroid hormone synthesis and, uniquely among antithyroid drugs, also inhibits peripheral conversion of thyroxine to the more active triiodothyronine, while propranolol controls the adrenergic features such as tachycardia and provides some additional inhibition of peripheral conversion. Levothyroxine would worsen the crisis by adding more thyroid hormone, and radioactive iodine has no role in acute management given its delayed onset of action. Calcium gluconate and insulin-dextrose are treatments for hyperkalaemia and are not indicated in thyroid storm.
  29. The correct answer is E (Immediate intravenous hydrocortisone with aggressive fluid resuscitation): Adrenal crisis from abrupt steroid withdrawal produces glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid deficiency, causing hypotension, hyponatraemia, and hyperkalaemia through loss of cortisol's permissive effect on vascular tone and aldosterone's role in renal sodium retention and potassium excretion. Immediate treatment requires intravenous hydrocortisone to replace both glucocorticoid and, at high dose, some mineralocorticoid activity, combined with aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation to correct hypovolaemia and hyponatraemia. Treatment should never be delayed to await formal short synacthen test confirmation, since the crisis is life-threatening. Oral fludrocortisone acts too slowly and fluid restriction would worsen the haemodynamic instability rather than improve it.
  30. The correct answer is A (Continue usual dose and give supplementary intravenous hydrocortisone at induction and postoperatively): Patients on long-term exogenous corticosteroids have suppressed hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis function and cannot mount an adequate endogenous cortisol response to the physiological stress of major surgery. Current UK practice is to continue the patient's usual maintenance steroid dose and supplement this with additional intravenous hydrocortisone, typically at induction and continuing for major surgery, scaled to the magnitude of surgical stress. Stopping steroids abruptly risks precipitating adrenal crisis rather than reducing risk. Simply doubling the oral dose without intravenous cover, or waiting for hypotension to develop before treating, both risk inadequate cortisol replacement during the critical perioperative period.
  31. The correct answer is D (Hepatic and renal gluconeogenesis using amino acids, lactate, and glycerol): Hepatic glycogen stores are limited and become exhausted within roughly 24 hours of fasting, so beyond this point glucose availability depends increasingly on gluconeogenesis, using amino acids released from muscle protein breakdown, lactate, and glycerol released from adipose tissue lipolysis as substrates. The kidney also contributes an increasing share of gluconeogenesis as starvation continues. Insulin secretion falls rather than rises during starvation, since low glucose availability does not stimulate insulin release, and peripheral tissues increasingly shift towards using ketone bodies and fatty acids for fuel rather than increasing glucose uptake. Ketone bodies cannot be converted back into glucose, since the enzymatic step linking acetyl-CoA to glucose synthesis does not exist in human metabolism.
  32. The correct answer is C (Insulin-driven intracellular shift of phosphate during carbohydrate refeeding): Reintroducing carbohydrate after prolonged starvation triggers a surge in insulin secretion, which drives glucose, phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells to support glycolysis and cellular anabolism. In a patient already depleted of total body phosphate from prolonged starvation, this sudden intracellular shift can produce profound extracellular hypophosphataemia, contributing to muscle weakness, respiratory failure, and cardiac arrhythmia characteristic of refeeding syndrome. This is a redistribution phenomenon rather than a primary renal excretion or absorption problem, and it is not caused by phosphate binding by feed proteins. Prevention relies on identifying at-risk patients and introducing feed slowly with electrolyte monitoring and supplementation before and during refeeding.
  33. The correct answer is B (NSAIDs inhibit prostaglandin-mediated afferent arteriolar vasodilation reducing renal blood flow): In states of reduced effective renal perfusion, locally produced prostaglandins maintain afferent arteriolar dilation to preserve renal blood flow and glomerular filtration. NSAIDs inhibit cyclo-oxygenase and therefore prostaglandin synthesis, removing this compensatory vasodilator mechanism and allowing unopposed vasoconstriction of the afferent arteriole, which can precipitate acute kidney injury in patients who are already dependent on prostaglandin-mediated renal protection, such as those with chronic kidney disease, hypovolaemia, or heart failure. NSAIDs do not act primarily on the efferent arteriole or directly damage the glomerular basement membrane. Their effect on aldosterone secretion is not the principal mechanism of NSAID-associated renal injury.
  34. The correct answer is A (Combined respiratory and metabolic acidosis from hypoventilation and hypoperfusion): The panel shown demonstrates a severely low pH with both a markedly elevated PaCO2, reflecting inadequate ventilation during the arrest, and a markedly reduced bicarbonate with a strongly negative base excess, reflecting the lactic acidosis generated by prolonged tissue hypoperfusion. The presence of both an elevated PaCO2 and a reduced bicarbonate together, rather than one compensating appropriately for the other, indicates a combined rather than a single compensated disturbance. This mixed picture is typical immediately after cardiac arrest and return of spontaneous circulation, before ventilation and perfusion have normalised. A purely respiratory or purely metabolic process would not account for both the very high carbon dioxide and the very low bicarbonate seen together here.
  35. The correct answer is E (Renin cleaves angiotensinogen to angiotensin I, converted to angiotensin II in the lungs): Reduced renal perfusion pressure is sensed by the juxtaglomerular apparatus, which releases renin, an enzyme that cleaves circulating angiotensinogen, produced by the liver, into angiotensin I. Angiotensin I is then converted to angiotensin II predominantly by angiotensin-converting enzyme located in the pulmonary vascular endothelium. Angiotensin II causes systemic and efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, helping to maintain glomerular filtration pressure, and stimulates aldosterone release from the adrenal cortex, which acts on the distal nephron to increase sodium reabsorption and potassium excretion, not reabsorption. The macula densa contributes to renin release regulation but does not itself release angiotensin-converting enzyme.
  36. The correct answer is C (Inhibition of the Na-K-2Cl cotransporter in the thick ascending limb): Loop diuretics such as furosemide act on the Na-K-2Cl cotransporter in the thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle, blocking reabsorption of sodium, potassium, and chloride at this site. The resulting increased delivery of sodium to the distal nephron enhances sodium-potassium exchange in the collecting duct, promoting potassium loss, while the associated volume contraction activates the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system, further worsening potassium and hydrogen ion losses and producing the characteristic contraction alkalosis. Thiazide diuretics act on the sodium-chloride cotransporter in the distal convoluted tubule, a different site with a similar but distinct electrolyte pattern. Carbonic anhydrase inhibitors and aldosterone receptor blockade act by different mechanisms not primarily responsible for the picture described here.
  37. The correct answer is D (Glomerular filtration rate falls in proportion to reduced perfusion pressure): Renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate are actively maintained across a defined autoregulatory range of arterial pressure, but once mean arterial pressure falls below the lower limit of this range, typically around 80 mmHg, autoregulatory mechanisms are overwhelmed and glomerular filtration becomes directly dependent on perfusion pressure, falling as pressure falls. This is why prolonged intraoperative hypotension below the autoregulatory threshold carries a real risk of acute kidney injury. Compensatory efferent arteriolar dilation or increased renal blood flow would not occur under these circumstances, since the underlying problem is inadequate perfusion pressure itself. Tubular creatinine secretion does not meaningfully increase to compensate for a falling filtration rate.
  38. The correct answer is A (Systemic absorption of hypotonic irrigation fluid via open venous sinuses): During transurethral resection of the prostate, opened venous sinuses in the prostatic bed allow direct systemic absorption of the hypotonic glycine irrigation fluid used to maintain a clear operative field. Large volumes of absorbed hypotonic fluid dilute plasma sodium acutely, producing the syndrome historically known as TURP syndrome, which can also cause glycine-related visual disturbance and confusion independent of the hyponatraemia itself. This is a mechanical and dilutional process related directly to the surgical technique rather than a hormonal or adrenal mechanism. Antidiuretic hormone excess and adrenal insufficiency are recognised causes of hyponatraemia in other clinical contexts but are not the mechanism specific to this intraoperative presentation.
  39. The correct answer is A (Elective surgery should generally be postponed to allow gradual potassium correction): Although mild asymptomatic hypokalaemia is common and the evidence for a sharp cancellation threshold is limited, significant hypokalaemia below approximately 3.0 to 3.2 mmol/L is generally regarded as a reason to postpone elective surgery, since it increases the risk of perioperative arrhythmia, particularly in patients with cardiac disease or those receiving digoxin, and allows time for safer gradual correction rather than rapid intravenous replacement. Rapid potassium boluses risk dangerous hyperkalaemia and arrhythmia and are inappropriate as a routine preoperative measure. There is no universally fixed absolute cutoff that makes any degree of hypokalaemia an absolute contraindication, but proceeding with clinically significant hypokalaemia purely because the patient is asymptomatic is not appropriate practice.
  40. The correct answer is E (Aggressive intravenous isotonic saline rehydration to restore renal calcium clearance): Severe hypercalcaemia of malignancy typically presents with significant dehydration, since hypercalcaemia impairs renal concentrating ability and causes polyuria, and this volume depletion further reduces renal calcium clearance, worsening the hypercalcaemia in a vicious cycle. The first priority is therefore aggressive rehydration with intravenous isotonic saline to restore circulating volume and improve renal calcium excretion, before adding bisphosphonate therapy for more sustained calcium lowering. Fluid restriction would worsen rather than improve renal calcium handling in this context. Calcium supplementation is clearly inappropriate, and loop diuretics should only be considered cautiously after adequate rehydration has been achieved, never as monotherapy beforehand.
  41. The correct answer is B (Respiratory alkalosis with partial renal compensation): The panel shown demonstrates a raised pH with a low PaCO2, reflecting hyperventilation driven by early sepsis, which is a common and often early finding before overt haemodynamic instability develops. The mildly reduced bicarbonate represents the beginning of renal compensation, whereby the kidney reduces hydrogen ion excretion and bicarbonate reabsorption in an attempt to normalise pH, though this compensation is only partial given the still elevated pH. A fully compensated chronic respiratory acidosis would show a low rather than high pH with an elevated rather than reduced PaCO2, which is the opposite of what is shown here. The absence of a significantly negative base excess argues against a primary metabolic acidosis being the dominant process.
  42. The correct answer is D (Rifampicin induces cytochrome P450 enzymes increasing warfarin metabolism): Rifampicin is a potent inducer of hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, including those responsible for metabolising warfarin, which increases the rate of warfarin clearance and reduces its anticoagulant effect at any given dose. Patients on concurrent rifampicin therefore typically require a substantially higher warfarin dose to achieve the same degree of anticoagulation, and this interaction must be anticipated and monitored closely with regular INR checks. Rifampicin does not act by displacing warfarin from protein binding sites, inhibiting vitamin K epoxide reductase, or reducing intestinal absorption of warfarin, all of which would tend to increase rather than decrease anticoagulant effect. Enzyme induction develops over one to two weeks and similarly resolves gradually once rifampicin is stopped.
  43.
