The correct answer is C (Cortisol peaks in the early morning and is lowest around midnight): Cortisol secretion from the adrenal cortex follows a well established circadian rhythm driven by pulsatile hypothalamic corticotrophin-releasing hormone and pituitary adrenocorticotrophic hormone release, with plasma cortisol typically peaking shortly after waking in the early morning and declining to its lowest point around midnight. This rhythm is clinically relevant when interpreting single random cortisol measurements and when timing dynamic endocrine tests such as the overnight dexamethasone suppression test. Cortisol secretion is entirely dependent on adrenocorticotrophic hormone stimulation from the pituitary rather than independent of it. The rhythm is a normal and consistently demonstrable feature of healthy adult physiology rather than being absent or reversed.
  44. The correct answer is E (Excess catecholamine release from chromaffin tissue stimulating adrenergic receptors): Phaeochromocytoma is a catecholamine-secreting tumour of chromaffin cells, most commonly arising in the adrenal medulla, and the physical and pharmacological stimulation associated with induction of anaesthesia can provoke a sudden massive release of stored adrenaline and noradrenaline. This surge acts on alpha and beta adrenergic receptors to produce severe hypertension, tachyarrhythmia, and potentially myocardial ischaemia or pulmonary oedema if unrecognised. This mechanism is distinct from excess cortisol, aldosterone, parathyroid hormone, or thyroid hormone, each of which produces a different characteristic clinical crisis rather than the acute hypertensive and arrhythmic picture described here. Preoperative recognition and alpha then beta blockade are essential to prevent this presentation when phaeochromocytoma is known or suspected.
  45. The correct answer is A (Chronic cortisol excess suppresses the contralateral adrenal gland and pituitary ACTH): Sustained excess cortisol secretion from an adrenal adenoma exerts negative feedback on the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, suppressing pituitary adrenocorticotrophic hormone release, which in turn causes atrophy and reduced function of the contralateral, otherwise normal, adrenal gland. Following removal of the adenoma, the remaining suppressed adrenal gland cannot immediately resume normal cortisol production, leaving the patient acutely cortisol deficient despite having had endogenous hypercortisolism only shortly before, and requiring perioperative glucocorticoid replacement that is gradually weaned as adrenal function recovers. The contralateral gland is deliberately left in place during surgery for this exact reason, rather than being routinely removed. This suppression is a well recognised and expected physiological consequence rather than a rare occurrence.
  46. The correct answer is B (Check and correct phosphate, potassium, and magnesium before slowly introducing feed): Refeeding syndrome is prevented primarily by identifying patients at high risk, such as those with prolonged starvation or chronic alcohol excess, checking baseline phosphate, potassium, and magnesium concentrations, correcting any existing deficits, and then introducing nutritional support cautiously at a reduced calorie intake with gradual escalation while these electrolytes are monitored closely and supplemented proactively. Thiamine should also be given before or with the start of feeding to prevent Wernicke's encephalopathy, not withheld. Starting full-calorie feeding immediately, or giving high-dose glucose without addressing electrolyte status first, are exactly the practices that precipitate refeeding syndrome rather than prevent it. Restricting electrolyte supplementation during feeding removes the key protective measure against the syndrome.
  47. The correct answer is D (Surgical and anaesthetic stress precipitating decompensation in unoptimised thyrotoxicosis): Thyroid storm is a rare but life-threatening decompensation of underlying thyrotoxicosis, typically precipitated by a significant physiological stressor such as infection, trauma, or surgery, particularly when the underlying hyperthyroidism has not been adequately controlled beforehand with antithyroid medication and beta-blockade. Emergency surgery performed without the opportunity for proper preoperative optimisation therefore carries a recognised risk of triggering this presentation in a patient with poorly controlled Graves' disease. Adequate rather than excessive beta-blockade is protective, and intraoperative hypothermia or excess fluid administration are not established precipitants of thyroid storm. Postoperative hypoglycaemia is unrelated to thyroid status and would not itself explain the combination of fever, severe tachycardia, and confusion described.
  48. The correct answer is C (Net filtration pressure across the glomerular capillary membrane): GFR is determined by the sum of Starling forces acting across the glomerular capillary wall, namely glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure, Bowman's capsule hydrostatic pressure and glomerular capillary oncotic pressure. Afferent and efferent arteriolar tone alter this net pressure by changing glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure, which is why they influence GFR indirectly rather than being the primary determinant themselves. Renal blood flow and tubuloglomerular feedback modulate GFR but act by changing this same net filtration pressure rather than determining it independently.
  49. The correct answer is A (Myogenic constriction of afferent arteriolar smooth muscle in response to stretch): Renal autoregulation over the range 80 to 180 mmHg is maintained mainly by the myogenic response, in which increased wall stretch causes afferent arteriolar smooth muscle to constrict, limiting the rise in glomerular capillary pressure. Tubuloglomerular feedback contributes a smaller, slower component by sensing distal tubular sodium chloride delivery at the macula densa. Sympathetic tone and angiotensin II become dominant only outside the autoregulatory range or during haemodynamic stress, and prostaglandins act mainly to preserve flow when vasoconstrictor tone is high.
  50. The correct answer is D (Glucose): Glucose is freely filtered at the glomerulus and almost entirely reabsorbed in the proximal convoluted tubule via sodium-glucose cotransporters SGLT2 and SGLT1, so urinary glucose is normally undetectable below the renal threshold of around 10 mmol/L. Urea is passively reabsorbed to a variable degree depending on flow rate, potassium reabsorption in the proximal tubule is incomplete and secretion occurs more distally, and creatinine is filtered but not reabsorbed, which is why it is used to estimate GFR. Hydrogen ion is actively secreted rather than reabsorbed in this segment.
  51. The correct answer is B (Reduced renal perfusion pressure activating the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and antidiuretic hormone): Positive pressure ventilation raises intrathoracic pressure, reducing venous return and cardiac output, which lowers renal perfusion pressure and effective circulating volume. Baroreceptor-mediated activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and non-osmotic release of antidiuretic hormone follow, promoting sodium and water retention and reducing urine output. Atrial natriuretic peptide release is typically reduced rather than increased because atrial stretch falls as venous return decreases. This mechanism, not direct parenchymal compression, is the principal cause of oliguria during high-pressure ventilation.
  52. The correct answer is E (Acute respiratory acidosis with minimal metabolic compensation): The low pH with markedly elevated PaCO2 confirms a respiratory acidosis, caused here by opioid-induced hypoventilation. The bicarbonate and base excess are only marginally raised, which is typical of an acute process, since renal compensation through increased bicarbonate reabsorption and ammoniagenesis takes twenty-four to seventy-two hours to develop fully. A chronic pattern would show a much higher bicarbonate and near-normal pH for the degree of hypercapnia. Naloxone and improved ventilation, rather than bicarbonate administration, are the appropriate treatment.
  53. The correct answer is A (pH is proportional to the ratio of bicarbonate concentration to dissolved carbon dioxide): The Henderson-Hasselbalch equation states that pH equals the pKa of the bicarbonate system plus the logarithm of the ratio of bicarbonate concentration to dissolved carbon dioxide, the latter being proportional to PaCO2. Because pH depends on this ratio rather than on either value alone, a rise in both bicarbonate and PaCO2 can leave pH relatively unchanged, as occurs in compensated respiratory acidosis. Options describing pH as dependent on a single variable in isolation ignore the buffering relationship the equation describes.
  54. The correct answer is C (28 mmol/L, consistent with a raised anion gap acidosis): The anion gap is calculated as sodium minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate, giving 140 minus 112, which equals 28 mmol/L, well above the normal range of approximately 8 to 16 mmol/L. A raised anion gap indicates accumulation of unmeasured anions such as lactate, ketoacids or exogenous toxins, rather than the simple loss of bicarbonate seen in normal anion gap acidosis. This calculation is a routine first step in classifying a metabolic acidosis and directing further investigation. Chloride and bicarbonate levels here do not support a respiratory process.
  55. The correct answer is D (Expected PaCO2 is approximately 2.7 kPa, reflecting appropriate hyperventilation): Winter's formula predicts expected PaCO2 in mmHg as 1.5 times bicarbonate plus 8, plus or minus 2, which for a bicarbonate of 8 mmol/L gives approximately 20 mmHg (range 18 to 22 mmHg), equivalent to approximately 2.4 to 2.9 kPa. This confirms that vigorous compensatory hyperventilation, driving PaCO2 well below the normal 4.7 to 6.0 kPa range, is the physiologically appropriate response to this severe metabolic acidosis. A measured PaCO2 within this predicted range indicates appropriate respiratory compensation rather than a separate primary respiratory disorder. A value outside this range would suggest a mixed acid-base disturbance.
  56. The correct answer is B (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion): SIADH is a recognised complication of intracranial surgery, characterised by euvolaemic hyponatraemia with inappropriately concentrated urine despite low plasma osmolality, reflecting continued antidiuretic hormone release despite normal or expanded volume status. Cerebral salt wasting produces a similar biochemical picture but with volume depletion and renal sodium loss rather than euvolaemia. Diabetes insipidus produces hypernatraemia with dilute urine, the opposite pattern. Fluid restriction, rather than sodium replacement alone, is the mainstay of managing SIADH once the diagnosis is confirmed.
  57. The correct answer is E (Osmotic demyelination syndrome affecting the pons): Correcting chronic hyponatraemia too rapidly, generally more than 8 to 10 mmol/L in 24 hours, risks osmotic demyelination syndrome, in which rapid osmotic shifts damage myelinated neurons, classically in the pons, causing delayed quadriparesis, dysarthria and altered consciousness. This risk is highest when correction exceeds recommended limits in chronic hyponatraemia, as described here. Cerebral oedema is the risk of correcting hyponatraemia too slowly or of untreated acute hyponatraemia, not of overcorrection. Sodium correction should proceed in small, monitored increments with the option to relower sodium using desmopressin and dextrose if overcorrection occurs.
  58. The correct answer is D (Raised anion gap metabolic acidosis with respiratory compensation): The markedly low pH and bicarbonate with a large negative base excess confirm a severe metabolic acidosis, here due to accumulation of ketoacids in diabetic ketoacidosis, which would produce a raised anion gap once electrolytes are measured. The low PaCO2 reflects appropriate compensatory hyperventilation, known clinically as Kussmaul breathing, which partially limits the fall in pH. A normal anion gap acidosis would typically show a less severe base deficit for a comparable bicarbonate and would suggest bicarbonate loss such as diarrhoea rather than ketoacid accumulation. Treatment requires fluid resuscitation and intravenous insulin rather than bicarbonate.
  59. The correct answer is A (Intravenous calcium gluconate to stabilise the cardiac membrane): In hyperkalaemia with ECG changes, calcium gluconate should be given first because it stabilises the cardiac cell membrane and reduces the risk of arrhythmia within minutes, without lowering plasma potassium itself. Insulin with dextrose and salbutamol both shift potassium intracellularly and lower plasma levels over the following thirty to sixty minutes, but neither acts fast enough to be the initial priority when ECG changes are present. Calcium resonium acts over hours by removing potassium from the body and has no role in acute stabilisation. Bicarbonate is reserved for cases with significant concurrent acidosis.
  60. The correct answer is B (Succinylcholine should be avoided due to the risk of exaggerated hyperkalaemia): Following burns, denervation injury or prolonged immobility, extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors proliferate across the muscle membrane, and succinylcholine-induced depolarisation of these receptors can cause a massive, life-threatening efflux of potassium. This risk typically begins around 24 to 72 hours post-injury and persists for many months, so a burn ten days old falls squarely within the danger period regardless of total body surface area. A non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking agent should be used instead for rapid sequence induction in this context. The risk is not confined to larger burns and does not require ninety days to develop.
  61. The correct answer is C (Citrate anticoagulant chelating ionised calcium in the circulation): Blood products are anticoagulated with citrate, which binds ionised calcium to prevent clotting in the storage bag. During rapid, large-volume transfusion, citrate is infused faster than the liver can metabolise it, leading to transient chelation of circulating ionised calcium and clinically significant hypocalcaemia, which can impair cardiac contractility and coagulation. This effect is compounded by hepatic hypoperfusion or hypothermia, which slow citrate clearance further. Regular monitoring of ionised calcium and administration of calcium chloride or gluconate is standard practice during massive transfusion.
  62. The correct answer is E (Refractory hypokalaemia and increased risk of cardiac arrhythmia): Magnesium is required for the normal function of the sodium-potassium ATPase pump and for renal potassium conservation, so hypomagnesaemia commonly causes hypokalaemia that resists correction until magnesium is also replaced. Magnesium deficiency also predisposes to ventricular arrhythmias, including torsades de pointes, by prolonging the QT interval rather than shortening it. Hypomagnesaemia is typically associated with hypocalcaemia due to impaired parathyroid hormone secretion and end-organ resistance, not hypercalcaemia. Neuromuscular blockade is potentiated, not diminished, by low magnesium, which is a relevant consideration during anaesthesia.
  63. The correct answer is A (A large proportion of the absorbed dose is metabolised by the liver before reaching the systemic circulation): Drugs absorbed from the gastrointestinal tract enter the portal venous system and pass directly through the liver before reaching the systemic circulation, allowing hepatic enzymes to metabolise a substantial fraction before it can act. Intravenous administration bypasses this first pass entirely, delivering the full dose directly into the systemic circulation, which is why oral and intravenous doses of high-extraction drugs differ so markedly. Gastric acid degradation and intestinal bacterial metabolism can contribute to reduced bioavailability for some drugs but are not the defining feature of the first-pass effect. Oral drugs do pass through, rather than bypass, the liver.
  64. The correct answer is D (The hepatic artery buffer response increases arterial flow when portal flow decreases): The liver receives about 75% of its blood flow from the portal vein and 25% from the hepatic artery, but the artery supplies roughly half of hepatic oxygen delivery because portal blood is partially deoxygenated. The hepatic artery buffer response is an intrinsic mechanism, mediated by washout of locally produced adenosine, whereby a fall in portal venous flow triggers compensatory hepatic arterial vasodilation to help preserve total hepatic perfusion. This reciprocal relationship does not work in the opposite direction, since portal flow is largely determined by splanchnic venous return rather than hepatic regulation.
  65. The correct answer is B (Phenytoin induces hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, increasing drug metabolism and clearance): Phenytoin is a potent inducer of hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, which increases the metabolic clearance of many co-administered drugs, including several non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking agents that are metabolised or conjugated by these pathways. This enzyme induction, along with possible chronic upregulation of postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors seen with anticonvulsant therapy, contributes to the relative resistance to neuromuscular blockade often observed in patients on long-term phenytoin. Phenytoin does not act directly at the neuromuscular junction receptor itself, nor does it significantly inhibit plasma cholinesterase, which is more relevant to succinylcholine metabolism.
  66. The correct answer is C (Metabolic alkalosis with partial respiratory compensation): Loss of hydrochloric acid from prolonged vomiting produces a hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis, reflected here by the raised pH, elevated bicarbonate and strongly positive base excess. The modestly elevated PaCO2 represents appropriate but limited respiratory compensation, since hypoventilation to retain carbon dioxide is constrained by the resulting risk of hypoxaemia. Volume depletion further perpetuates the alkalosis by driving renal bicarbonate reabsorption and paradoxical aciduria despite systemic alkalosis. Correction requires intravenous fluid and chloride replacement, typically with sodium chloride, rather than acetazolamide or respiratory manipulation.
  67. The correct answer is E (Cortisol promotes gluconeogenesis and potentiates the action of catecholamines): Surgical stress activates the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, causing a marked rise in cortisol that promotes hepatic gluconeogenesis, mobilises amino acids and free fatty acids as metabolic substrates, and potentiates the vascular and metabolic effects of circulating catecholamines, a phenomenon known as a permissive effect. This coordinated response supports the increased metabolic demand of surgery and tissue repair rather than suppressing catecholamine release. Cortisol raises, rather than lowers, blood glucose, partly through reduced peripheral glucose uptake, and its effects extend well beyond electrolyte regulation during the stress response.
  68. The correct answer is B (Sympathoadrenal release of adrenaline and noradrenaline in response to nociceptive stimulus): Surgical stimulation activates afferent nociceptive pathways that trigger sympathoadrenal discharge, releasing adrenaline and noradrenaline from the adrenal medulla and sympathetic nerve terminals within seconds, producing the characteristic rise in heart rate and blood pressure seen at incision. This response can occur even under adequate anaesthetic depth if analgesia is insufficient, which is why intraoperative haemodynamic surges are often treated by increasing opioid analgesia rather than volatile agent concentration alone. Cortisol contributes to the stress response but acts over minutes to hours, too slowly to explain an immediate haemodynamic surge. The renin-angiotensin system responds to volume and pressure changes rather than acute nociception.
  69. The correct answer is D (Hyperpyrexia, marked tachycardia, agitation and gastrointestinal disturbance together): Thyroid storm is a clinical diagnosis based on the combination of hyperpyrexia, severe tachycardia or arrhythmia, central nervous system disturbance such as agitation or confusion, and gastrointestinal features including vomiting, often precipitated by the physiological stress of surgery or infection in a patient with untreated or poorly controlled hyperthyroidism. No single biochemical test confirms the diagnosis, so recognising this constellation of features is essential for prompt treatment with beta-blockade, antithyroid drugs, iodine and steroids. Isolated tachycardia or hypertension alone would be a much less specific and less severe presentation. Bradycardia and hypothermia would point away from thyroid storm entirely.
  70. The correct answer is A (Chronic exogenous steroid use suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, risking adrenal insufficiency under stress): Long-term exogenous corticosteroid therapy suppresses endogenous corticotrophin-releasing hormone and adrenocorticotrophic hormone secretion through negative feedback, leading to adrenal cortical atrophy and an impaired ability to mount the normal cortisol surge required during the physiological stress of surgery. Without supplemental steroid cover, such patients are at risk of an Addisonian-type crisis, presenting with hypotension refractory to fluids and vasopressors. This suppression is generally reversible over weeks to months after stopping steroids rather than permanent destruction of the gland. The rationale is haemodynamic and metabolic support, not primarily prevention of nausea or mineralocorticoid effects.
  71. The correct answer is E (A shift towards ketone body utilisation by the brain, sparing protein): After the first day or so of fasting, hepatic glycogen stores are exhausted, and the body increasingly relies on lipolysis and hepatic ketogenesis to supply ketone bodies, which the brain can use as an alternative fuel to glucose. This adaptation significantly reduces the rate of muscle protein breakdown that would otherwise be needed to provide gluconeogenic substrate, helping preserve lean body mass during prolonged starvation. In the first 24 hours, glycogenolysis is the dominant fuel source, but glycogen stores are limited and quickly depleted. Insulin secretion falls, rather than rises, as glucose availability declines during starvation.
  72. The correct answer is C (Insulin-driven intracellular shift of phosphate following reintroduction of carbohydrate): Refeeding after prolonged starvation triggers a surge in insulin secretion in response to carbohydrate intake, which drives glucose, phosphate, potassium and magnesium into cells for glycolysis and cellular repair processes. In a chronically depleted patient, this intracellular shift can produce severe hypophosphataemia, which impairs ATP production and can cause muscle weakness, respiratory failure and cardiac dysfunction if unrecognised. This mechanism, rather than renal wasting or malabsorption, is the defining feature of refeeding syndrome and is why feeding should be reintroduced slowly with proactive electrolyte monitoring and supplementation in at-risk patients.
  73. The correct answer is D (Acute respiratory alkalosis with minimal metabolic compensation): The raised pH with markedly reduced PaCO2 confirms a respiratory alkalosis, here driven by acute anxiety-related hyperventilation. Bicarbonate and base excess remain essentially normal, which is expected in an acute process, since renal compensation by reducing bicarbonate reabsorption takes hours to days to develop fully. The perioral tingling and carpopedal spasm reflect a fall in ionised calcium caused by hypocapnia-induced alkalosis increasing calcium binding to albumin. A chronic pattern would show a clearly reduced bicarbonate to compensate for the persistently low PaCO2. Reassurance and controlled breathing, rather than bicarbonate or oxygen therapy, are the appropriate management here.
  74. The correct answer is B (The juxtaglomerular apparatus of the afferent arteriole): Granular juxtaglomerular cells within the wall of the afferent arteriole act as baroreceptors sensing renal perfusion pressure, and release renin in response to reduced pressure, initiating the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone cascade that ultimately increases sodium and water retention and vasoconstriction. The macula densa, part of the distal tubule adjacent to the juxtaglomerular apparatus, senses sodium chloride delivery rather than pressure directly and modulates renin release through this separate chemosensing pathway. The vasa recta and collecting duct are involved in the medullary concentrating mechanism and final urine composition rather than renin release.
  75. The correct answer is A (Cranial diabetes insipidus due to reduced antidiuretic hormone secretion): Damage to the posterior pituitary or hypothalamic-pituitary stalk during pituitary surgery can reduce antidiuretic hormone secretion, causing cranial diabetes insipidus, characterised by large volumes of dilute urine and rising plasma sodium as free water is lost in excess of solute. This is the direct physiological opposite of SIADH, in which excess antidiuretic hormone causes water retention and dilutional hyponatraemia. Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus produces a similar biochemical picture but results from renal resistance to antidiuretic hormone rather than reduced secretion, and is not typically caused by pituitary surgery. Management involves careful fluid balance and desmopressin replacement guided by serial sodium measurement.
  76. The correct answer is E (Volatile agents tend to reduce renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate dose-dependently): Volatile anaesthetic agents generally cause a dose-dependent reduction in renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate, mediated partly by reduced cardiac output and mean arterial pressure and partly by increased renal vascular resistance and altered autoregulation, even when systemic blood pressure is relatively preserved. These effects are usually reversible once the anaesthetic is discontinued and adequate perfusion is restored, and modern volatile agents are not considered directly nephrotoxic at standard clinical concentrations, unlike older agents such as methoxyflurane with fluoride-related toxicity concerns. Maintaining adequate intravascular volume and perfusion pressure intraoperatively helps minimise this effect.
  77. The correct answer is C (Increased antidiuretic hormone release and reduced atrial natriuretic peptide release): Positive pressure ventilation reduces venous return and right atrial stretch, which is sensed by atrial stretch receptors as a fall in effective circulating volume. This triggers a non-osmotic increase in antidiuretic hormone release from the posterior pituitary and a reduction in atrial natriuretic peptide secretion from atrial myocytes, both of which promote sodium and water retention to help restore intravascular volume. This combined hormonal response contributes to the reduced urine output commonly observed during mechanical ventilation with high intrathoracic pressures. Both hormones move in a direction that opposes diuresis, not independently or unpredictably.
  78. The correct answer is A (Chronic alcohol use induces hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, increasing drug metabolism): Chronic heavy alcohol consumption induces hepatic cytochrome P450 enzymes, particularly CYP2E1 and related pathways, increasing the metabolic clearance of many anaesthetic drugs and contributing to the cross-tolerance often observed with induction agents and volatile anaesthetics. This pharmacokinetic tolerance is compounded by pharmacodynamic changes in central nervous system receptor sensitivity, but the enzyme induction effect is the mechanism most directly explaining an increased dose requirement rather than reduced hepatic function, which would be expected to decrease clearance if cirrhosis were established. In advanced liver disease, the picture may reverse as synthetic and metabolic capacity declines.
  79. The correct answer is D (Prolonged prothrombin time with low serum albumin): Prothrombin time and serum albumin both reflect the liver's capacity to synthesise clotting factors and plasma proteins, so a prolonged prothrombin time together with hypoalbuminaemia is a specific marker of impaired hepatic synthetic function, which is directly relevant to perioperative bleeding risk and drug protein binding. Aminotransferases such as alanine aminotransferase indicate hepatocyte injury rather than synthetic capacity, and alkaline phosphatase and gamma-glutamyl transferase are markers of cholestasis or biliary obstruction. Bilirubin can be elevated from several causes, including haemolysis or biliary obstruction, without necessarily indicating poor synthetic reserve.
  80. The correct answer is E (Chronic respiratory acidosis with a superimposed acute metabolic acidosis): The elevated bicarbonate of 27 mmol/L is consistent with renal compensation for this patient's known chronic hypercapnia from severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. However, the pH is lower than would be expected for this degree of compensation, and the base excess is negative rather than the strongly positive value expected in pure chronic respiratory acidosis, indicating an additional acute metabolic acidosis, most likely from lactate accumulation in sepsis. Recognising this mixed pattern is important because it signals a new acute process superimposed on the patient's baseline, requiring treatment of the underlying sepsis rather than assuming the abnormal gas is simply the patient's chronic norm. A single interpretation of either pure disorder would miss the acute deterioration.
  81. The correct answer is B (Mechanical release of stored catecholamines from tumour chromaffin cells into the circulation): Phaeochromocytomas are catecholamine-secreting tumours arising from chromaffin cells, and physical manipulation during surgery can cause a sudden, massive release of stored adrenaline and noradrenaline into the circulation, producing severe hypertension and tachycardia. This is why alpha and beta adrenergic blockade are established preoperatively and short-acting vasodilators and beta-blockers are kept immediately available intraoperatively. The crisis is caused by direct catecholamine release rather than a reflex autonomic or renin-angiotensin mechanism, and the adrenal cortex, which secretes cortisol, is not the source of these tumours. Ligation of the tumour's venous drainage before further handling can help limit further catecholamine release.
  82. The correct answer is A (Reduced renal 1-alpha-hydroxylase activity lowers active vitamin D, reducing gut calcium absorption and stimulating parathyroid hormone): In chronic kidney disease, reduced renal mass and function impair 1-alpha-hydroxylase activity, decreasing conversion of 25-hydroxyvitamin D to active calcitriol. This reduces intestinal calcium absorption and, combined with reduced phosphate excretion causing hyperphosphataemia, lowers plasma ionised calcium, which stimulates sustained parathyroid hormone secretion in an attempt to restore calcium homeostasis. Over time this leads to secondary hyperparathyroidism and, if prolonged, renal osteodystrophy. Renal failure impairs phosphate excretion, raising rather than lowering phosphate, and does not directly destroy parathyroid tissue.
  83. The correct answer is D (Loss of deep tendon reflexes with a falling respiratory rate): Magnesium toxicity produces a predictable, dose-dependent sequence of clinical signs as plasma levels rise, with loss of deep tendon reflexes typically preceding respiratory depression and, at higher levels, cardiac conduction abnormalities and arrest. Monitoring deep tendon reflexes, respiratory rate and urine output at the bedside allows early detection of accumulating toxicity, particularly in patients with reduced renal clearance of magnesium. Brisk reflexes and a normal respiratory rate are reassuring rather than concerning findings. Calcium gluconate should be available to reverse severe magnesium toxicity if it occurs.
  84. The correct answer is A (This pattern most likely represents sick euthyroid syndrome related to critical illness): Sick euthyroid syndrome, also called non-thyroidal illness syndrome, is common in critically unwell patients and is characterised by reduced peripheral conversion of thyroxine to triiodothyronine, low or low-normal thyroid hormone levels, and a thyroid stimulating hormone that remains within the normal range rather than rising appropriately. This pattern reflects adaptive changes in thyroid hormone metabolism during severe illness rather than true primary or central hypothyroidism, and thyroid hormone replacement has not been shown to improve outcomes in this setting. Repeating thyroid function tests after recovery from the acute illness is the appropriate way to distinguish this from genuine thyroid dysfunction, rather than treating empirically.
  85. The correct answer is E (Start feeding at a reduced calorie rate with proactive thiamine and electrolyte supplementation): Current guidance for patients at high risk of refeeding syndrome recommends starting nutritional support at a reduced calorie rate, typically around 5 to 10 kilocalories per kilogram per day initially, together with prophylactic thiamine and correction or proactive supplementation of potassium, phosphate and magnesium before and during feeding, since these shifts occur rapidly once carbohydrate intake resumes. Waiting for phosphate to fall before acting, or delaying feeding altogether, exposes the patient unnecessarily to prolonged malnutrition and does not prevent the syndrome, since the risk arises specifically at the point feeding is reintroduced. Full calorie feeding from day one is the single highest-risk approach in such patients.
  86. The correct answer is B (60 to 160 mmHg, with flow remaining relatively constant across this plateau): cerebral blood flow autoregulation maintains a relatively constant cerebral blood flow of around 50 mL per 100 g of brain tissue per minute across a mean arterial pressure range of roughly 60 to 160 mmHg in the normotensive adult. Below the lower limit, flow becomes pressure-dependent and falls, risking ischaemia. Above the upper limit, autoregulation is overwhelmed and flow rises passively with pressure, risking cerebral oedema. In chronic hypertension this plateau shifts rightward, so a pressure within the quoted normal range may already represent relative hypoperfusion.
  87. The correct answer is D (Flow changes by roughly one to two millilitres per 100 grams per minute per mmHg change): within the normal physiological range, approximately 20 to 80 mmHg arterial carbon dioxide tension, cerebral blood flow changes by around one to two millilitres per 100 grams of brain tissue per minute for every mmHg change in carbon dioxide tension. This reactivity is mediated by carbon dioxide-induced changes in perivascular pH altering cerebral arteriolar tone, with hypocapnia causing vasoconstriction and hypercapnia causing vasodilation. This is the physiological basis for using hyperventilation to reduce cerebral blood volume and intracranial pressure during neurosurgery. Reactivity plateaus at extremes of carbon dioxide tension outside this range.
  88. The correct answer is A (Displacement of cerebrospinal fluid from the cranial vault into the spinal theca): the Monro-Kellie doctrine states that the skull is a rigid, non-expansile compartment containing brain, blood and cerebrospinal fluid, so any increase in the volume of one component must be offset by a reduction in another to keep intracranial pressure stable. As a mass slowly enlarges, cerebrospinal fluid is displaced out of the cranial vault into the spinal subarachnoid space, which is the earliest and most efficient compensatory mechanism. Once this reserve is exhausted, compensation shifts to reducing intracranial venous blood volume. Only after both mechanisms are exhausted does intracranial pressure begin to rise steeply.
  89. The correct answer is E (Cushing's triad, indicating critically raised intracranial pressure with impending herniation): Cushing's triad describes hypertension, bradycardia and an irregular breathing pattern occurring together as a late and ominous sign of critically raised intracranial pressure. The hypertension is a reflex response aiming to maintain cerebral perfusion pressure against the rising intracranial pressure, and the accompanying bradycardia is a baroreceptor-mediated vagal response to this hypertension. Irregular respiration reflects brainstem compression as herniation becomes imminent. This is a medical emergency requiring immediate measures to reduce intracranial pressure.
  90. The correct answer is C (Myelinated fibres conduct via saltatory conduction, jumping between nodes of Ranvier): large myelinated A-alpha fibres are insulated by a myelin sheath interrupted at regular intervals by nodes of Ranvier, where voltage-gated sodium channels are concentrated. The action potential effectively jumps from node to node, a process called saltatory conduction, which is considerably faster than the continuous, point-by-point depolarisation seen in unmyelinated C fibres. Fibre diameter also contributes to conduction velocity, but the myelination and saltatory mechanism is the dominant factor. This is why C fibres, which carry dull, poorly localised pain, conduct far more slowly than large myelinated motor and proprioceptive fibres.
  91. The correct answer is A (They competitively antagonise acetylcholine at the postsynaptic nicotinic receptor): non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking drugs such as rocuronium bind reversibly to the alpha subunits of the postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptor at the neuromuscular junction, competitively preventing acetylcholine from binding and triggering depolarisation. Because they do not themselves activate the receptor, there is no initial fasciculation, unlike with depolarising agents. Recovery occurs as acetylcholine concentration rises relative to the blocking drug, or is accelerated pharmacologically with an anticholinesterase or sugammadex. This competitive, non-depolarising mechanism distinguishes them fundamentally from suxamethonium.
  92. The correct answer is D (The block has developed features resembling a non-depolarising block, termed phase two): repeated or prolonged exposure to suxamethonium can cause the initial depolarising phase one block to evolve into a phase two block, which shares features with a non-depolarising block including train-of-four fade and post-tetanic potentiation. The exact mechanism is not fully understood but is thought to involve desensitisation of the postsynaptic receptor and possible presynaptic effects. Clinically this manifests as unexpectedly prolonged neuromuscular blockade after suxamethonium. Recognising phase two block is important because management differs from a straightforward depolarising block, and anticholinesterase reversal may sometimes be considered.
  93. The correct answer is B (Lower segment Caesarean section requiring block to the nipple line): the T4 dermatome corresponds to the nipple line, and a sensory block extending to this level is the widely accepted minimum height required for surgical anaesthesia during a lower segment Caesarean section, ensuring adequate coverage of visceral peritoneal stimulation during uterine exteriorisation and manipulation. Lower abdominal or lower limb procedures such as transurethral prostate resection, amputation or hernia repair require considerably lower block heights than T4. Confirming block height at the nipple line before starting a Caesarean section is a standard safety check performed by the anaesthetist.
  94. The correct answer is E (Ankle plantarflexion, mediated by the tibial nerve root supply): the S1 dermatome corresponds to the lateral foot and heel, and sensory blockade extending to this level following a spinal anaesthetic implies motor blockade of the lumbosacral nerve roots supplying the lower limb, including S1, which mediates ankle plantarflexion via the tibial nerve and is also the root tested by the ankle jerk reflex. Hip flexion, knee extension, elbow flexion and wrist extension are supplied by more proximal or upper limb nerve roots and are not relevant to a lower limb spinal block reaching this level. This illustrates why motor assessment alongside sensory testing is useful when confirming adequacy of a spinal block for lower limb surgery.
  95. The correct answer is C (Roots, trunks, divisions, cords, branches, proceeding proximal to distal): the brachial plexus is organised, from proximal to distal, into roots (C5 to T1), trunks (upper, middle and lower), divisions (anterior and posterior), cords (lateral, posterior and medial, named for their relationship to the axillary artery) and finally terminal branches. This sequence is commonly remembered using a mnemonic taught throughout anaesthetic and surgical training. Understanding this organisation underpins the rationale for different regional anaesthetic approaches, since a block performed more proximally, such as interscalene, targets roots and trunks, while a more distal block, such as axillary, targets terminal branches around the cords.
  96. The correct answer is D (The medial forearm and hand, supplied by the lower trunk): an interscalene block targets the plexus at the level of the roots and upper trunk, formed by C5 and C6, and reliably anaesthetises the shoulder and lateral arm but characteristically spares the lower trunk contribution, formed by C8 and T1, which supplies the medial forearm and hand via the ulnar nerve. This sparing is a well-recognised limitation of interscalene block and is why it is not typically used as the sole technique for hand surgery. The shoulder, deltoid region and acromioclavicular joint are all well covered because they are supplied predominantly by upper trunk-derived fibres.
  97. The correct answer is A (The musculocutaneous nerve, which has already left the sheath within coracobrachialis): by the level of the axilla, the musculocutaneous nerve has typically already left the neurovascular sheath, having pierced or passed through coracobrachialis more proximally, so it lies outside the sheath surrounding the axillary artery and the other terminal branches. This is clinically important because a standard perivascular axillary block injection may fail to reliably anaesthetise the musculocutaneous nerve, often requiring a separate targeted injection into coracobrachialis. The median, ulnar and radial nerves remain within or closely applied to the sheath at this level, arranged anterior, medial and posterior to the artery respectively.
  98. The correct answer is B (Weak elbow flexion with sensory loss over the lateral forearm): the musculocutaneous nerve arises from the lateral cord and supplies motor innervation to the biceps brachii, brachialis and coracobrachialis muscles, all flexors of the elbow, before continuing as the lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm to supply sensation over the lateral forearm. An incompletely blocked musculocutaneous nerve therefore presents with preserved sensation and strength in this specific distribution despite an otherwise successful brachial plexus block. This is distinct from the distributions of the radial, ulnar, median and axillary nerves, which supply different muscle groups and skin territories.
  99. The correct answer is E (The cricoid cartilage, the only complete cartilaginous ring of the airway): the cricoid cartilage is unique among the laryngeal and tracheal cartilages in forming a complete, signet-ring-shaped ring of cartilage, unlike the thyroid cartilage, which is incomplete posteriorly, or the tracheal rings, which are C-shaped and deficient posteriorly where they abut the trachealis muscle. This complete ring structure is the anatomical basis for cricoid pressure, historically used during rapid sequence induction to occlude the oesophagus. The arytenoid, corniculate and epiglottic cartilages are paired or single structures that do not form complete rings.
  100. The correct answer is C (The cricothyroid membrane, located between the thyroid and cricoid cartilages): the cricothyroid membrane is a thin fibroelastic membrane spanning the gap between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage and the superior border of the cricoid cartilage, and it is the standard site for emergency surgical or needle access to the airway during a can't-intubate, can't-oxygenate scenario. It lies relatively superficially in the midline anterior neck and is largely avascular in its central portion, making it a safer entry point than surrounding structures. The hyoid bone, thyroid notch, first tracheal ring and sternal notch are useful surrounding landmarks but are not themselves the site of incision.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Mucosa from the epiglottis down to the level of the vocal cords): the internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve is purely sensory and supplies mucosal sensation from the epiglottis down to, and including, the superior surface of the vocal cords, making it the target for a superior laryngeal nerve block during awake airway techniques to obtund the gag and cough reflex triggered by laryngoscope or airway device contact in this region. The area below the vocal cords is instead supplied by the recurrent laryngeal nerve. The external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve is motor, supplying cricothyroid, and the tongue is supplied by other cranial nerves.
  2. The correct answer is D (Sensory below the cords and motor to all intrinsic muscles except cricothyroid): the recurrent laryngeal nerve provides sensory innervation to the laryngeal mucosa below the level of the vocal cords, an area not covered by the superior laryngeal nerve, and provides motor innervation to all of the intrinsic muscles of the larynx except cricothyroid, which is supplied by the external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve. Topicalisation of the subglottic airway, for example by transtracheal injection of local anaesthetic through the cricothyroid membrane, is required to anaesthetise this recurrent laryngeal nerve territory during awake intubation. Bilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury is a recognised cause of airway obstruction due to unopposed vocal cord adduction.
  3. The correct answer is C (A sudden loss of resistance as the needle passes through ligamentum flavum): the loss of resistance technique relies on the dense, fibrous ligamentum flavum offering firm resistance to injection through a syringe of saline or air attached to the Tuohy needle, followed by a sudden loss of that resistance as the needle tip passes through the ligament into the epidural space, where the potential space offers minimal resistance to injection. Free aspiration of cerebrospinal fluid would instead indicate inadvertent dural puncture, not correct epidural placement. A palpable click and increased resistance are not the accepted signs relied upon, and blood on aspiration suggests placement within an epidural vein rather than the epidural space itself.
  4. The correct answer is B (L1 to L2, the accepted average level in adults): in the average adult, the spinal cord tapers to end as the conus medullaris at approximately the L1 to L2 vertebral level, although some anatomical variation exists. Performing a spinal or epidural needle insertion at or below the L3/4 interspace, well below this level, avoids direct trauma to the spinal cord itself, since only the cauda equina, a bundle of freely mobile nerve roots, occupies the subarachnoid space at this lower level. This is why L3/4 or L4/5 is generally preferred over higher lumbar interspaces for neuraxial techniques. The dural sac extends further caudally than the cord itself, to around S2.
  5. The correct answer is E (S2, corresponding to a line joining the posterior superior iliac spines): the dural sac, and therefore the subarachnoid space, typically terminates at approximately the S2 vertebral level in adults, considerably lower than the spinal cord itself, which ends around L1/2. A line joining the two posterior superior iliac spines is a useful surface landmark that approximates this S2 level. This anatomical relationship is relevant when performing a caudal block, since the needle must avoid advancing too far cephalad and risking dural puncture. It also explains why cerebrospinal fluid can still be obtained via a lumbar puncture performed well below the level of cord termination.
  6. The correct answer is A (Between the sternal and clavicular heads of sternocleidomastoid, lateral to the carotid pulse): the classic landmark technique for right internal jugular vein cannulation identifies the apex of the triangle formed by the sternal and clavicular heads of sternocleidomastoid and the clavicle, with the needle directed towards the ipsilateral nipple, lateral to the palpated carotid artery pulse. The internal jugular vein lies within the carotid sheath, anterolateral to the carotid artery at this level. This landmark-based approach has now largely been superseded by ultrasound guidance, which directly visualises the vein and reduces the risk of inadvertent carotid puncture.
  7. The correct answer is D (The apex of the lung and pleura lie in close proximity posteriorly): the subclavian vein passes over the first rib in close relation to the apex of the lung and the pleural dome lying just posterior and inferior to the vessel, meaning that a needle directed too deeply or at an incorrect angle during subclavian cannulation risks puncturing the pleura and causing a pneumothorax. This anatomical relationship is a key reason the subclavian approach carries a higher recognised pneumothorax risk than the internal jugular approach, although it offers advantages including lower infection rates for long-term lines and easier landmark identification in an anticoagulated or obese patient. The phrenic nerve, trachea, subclavian artery and thoracic duct are all nearby structures but are not the principal explanation for this specific risk.
  8. The correct answer is B (The autoregulatory curve shifts to the right, requiring a higher pressure to maintain flow): chronic hypertension causes structural remodelling of cerebral resistance vessels, shifting the entire cerebral autoregulatory curve to the right so that both the lower and upper limits of autoregulation occur at higher mean arterial pressures than in a normotensive individual. This means a mean arterial pressure that would be entirely adequate in a normotensive patient may fall below the shifted lower limit in a chronically hypertensive patient, risking cerebral hypoperfusion. This is clinically important when managing blood pressure during anaesthesia in hypertensive patients, since allowing pressure to fall towards a conventionally normal target may still compromise cerebral perfusion.
  9. The correct answer is C (Displacement of intracranial venous blood volume out of the cranial vault): once the compensatory capacity of cerebrospinal fluid displacement into the spinal theca is exhausted, the next compensatory mechanism recruited under the Monro-Kellie doctrine is a reduction in intracranial venous blood volume, as venous structures are compliant and can be compressed to accommodate a growing mass. Arterial blood volume is comparatively resistant to this compensatory reduction because it is required to maintain cerebral perfusion. Once both cerebrospinal fluid and venous blood compensatory reserves are exhausted, the intracranial pressure-volume curve becomes very steep, and small further increases in volume produce large rises in intracranial pressure, risking herniation.
  10. The correct answer is E (Saltatory conduction, in which depolarisation jumps between nodes of Ranvier): saltatory conduction describes the mechanism by which, in a myelinated axon, the action potential effectively jumps from one node of Ranvier to the next, since the myelinated internodal segments are electrically insulated and voltage-gated sodium channels are concentrated only at the nodes. This dramatically increases conduction velocity compared with continuous conduction along an unmyelinated fibre, while also being metabolically efficient because active depolarisation only needs to occur at the nodes rather than along the entire axon length. Wallerian degeneration is an unrelated pathological process following axonal injury, not a normal conduction mechanism.
  11. The correct answer is D (Autoantibodies directed against postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors): myasthenia gravis is an autoimmune condition in which autoantibodies are directed against the postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptor at the neuromuscular junction, reducing the number of functional receptors available and impairing neuromuscular transmission, producing characteristic fatigable weakness. This underlying receptor deficit explains the heightened sensitivity of myasthenic patients to non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking drugs, which compete for the same reduced pool of receptors, and their relative resistance to suxamethonium. Anti-MuSK antibodies represent a less common variant affecting a different postsynaptic protein, but the classical and most common form involves receptor-directed autoantibodies.
  12. The correct answer is A (C6, the dermatome most commonly used to test this block clinically): the thumb and the lateral aspect of the hand correspond to the C6 dermatome, making it a convenient and commonly used site for clinically testing sensory onset following a brachial plexus block targeting the upper roots and trunks of the plexus. The little finger corresponds instead to C8, the medial forearm to T1, the shoulder tip to C4, and the nipple line to T4, none of which are useful for assessing a block intended primarily for hand surgery. Testing a dermatome appropriate to the surgical site and the roots targeted by the chosen block technique is standard practice before proceeding to surgery.
  13. The correct answer is C (T10, the dermatome level corresponding to the umbilicus): the umbilicus corresponds to the T10 dermatome, which is why the visceral, poorly localised pain of early labour, arising from uterine contraction and cervical dilation transmitted via sympathetic afferents entering the spinal cord at T10 to L1, is classically referred to this level. As labour progresses and somatic pain from perineal and vaginal stretching develops via the pudendal nerve, S2 to S4, the pain becomes better localised to the perineum. Understanding these referred pain patterns helps explain why an epidural for labour must eventually cover a wide dermatomal range from T10 down to S4.
  14. The correct answer is B (The median nerve, formed from contributions of both highlighted cords): the median nerve is formed by the union of a lateral root arising from the lateral cord and a medial root arising from the medial cord, which join anterior to the axillary artery to form the median nerve proper. The radial and axillary nerves arise solely from the posterior cord, the ulnar nerve arises solely from the medial cord, and the musculocutaneous nerve arises solely from the lateral cord, so none of these other terminal nerves receive a contribution from both of the highlighted cords. This dual-cord origin of the median nerve is a frequently tested point in brachial plexus anatomy.
  15. The correct answer is E (The long thoracic nerve, supplying serratus anterior): the long thoracic nerve arises directly from the roots of C5, C6 and C7 and runs a long, superficial course along the lateral chest wall to supply serratus anterior, which stabilises and rotates the scapula against the chest wall. Injury to this nerve, which is vulnerable during axillary surgery including axillary node clearance, produces winging of the scapula, in which the medial border protrudes posteriorly, most evident when pushing against resistance. The axillary, suprascapular, thoracodorsal and dorsal scapular nerves supply different muscles and do not produce this characteristic winging pattern when injured in isolation.
  16. The correct answer is D (It attaches inferiorly to the thyroid cartilage via the thyroepiglottic ligament): the epiglottic cartilage is anchored inferiorly to the inner surface of the thyroid cartilage, just below the thyroid notch, by the thyroepiglottic ligament, while its superior free edge projects upward behind the tongue base and hyoid bone. It is also connected to the hyoid bone by the hyoepiglottic ligament, but this is a secondary attachment rather than its principal anchor point. The epiglottis has no direct attachment to the cricoid cartilage or the arytenoid cartilages, which are instead connected to the vocal ligaments and the cricoarytenoid joints respectively.
  17. The correct answer is A (Between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage and the cricoid cartilage): the cricothyroid membrane spans the interval between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage superiorly and the superior border of the cricoid cartilage inferiorly, lying in the midline of the anterior neck. This location makes it readily palpable as a soft depression below the more prominent thyroid cartilage and above the firm cricoid ring, and it is this reliable, relatively superficial and largely avascular position that makes it the preferred site for emergency front-of-neck access. The hyoid bone, first tracheal ring and posterior arytenoid region are all distinct landmarks that do not correspond to this membrane's location.
  18. The correct answer is C (The glossopharyngeal nerve, supplying the posterior third of the tongue and oropharynx): the glossopharyngeal nerve carries general sensation, and taste, from the posterior third of the tongue and the oropharyngeal mucosa, and forms the afferent limb of the gag reflex, with the vagus nerve providing the efferent motor limb via pharyngeal muscle contraction. The facial nerve supplies taste, not general sensation, to the anterior two-thirds of the tongue, which is otherwise supplied by the lingual branch of the trigeminal nerve. The hypoglossal nerve is purely motor to the tongue musculature and carries no sensory fibres relevant to the gag reflex.
  19. The correct answer is B (A steeper cephalad needle angle, since spinous processes overlap markedly here): in the mid-thoracic spine, the spinous processes are angled steeply downward and overlap one another considerably more than in the lumbar spine, where they are nearly horizontal. This anatomical difference means that a midline approach to a mid-thoracic epidural typically requires a steeper cephalad angulation of the needle to pass between the overlapping spinous processes and reach the interlaminar space. A paramedian approach is a useful alternative technique at this level but is not mandatory, and a caudal approach through the sacral hiatus is an entirely different technique used for sacral or low lumbar analgesia, not for thoracic epidurals.
  20. The correct answer is E (Tuffier's line, a line joining the iliac crests, marking approximately L4): Tuffier's line, also called the intercristal line, is drawn joining the highest points of the two iliac crests and conventionally intersects the spine at approximately the L4 vertebral level or the L4/5 interspace, providing a useful surface landmark for estimating lumbar interspace level before performing a spinal anaesthetic or lumbar puncture. Because the spinal cord terminates around L1/2, using this landmark helps the anaesthetist select an interspace safely below cord termination. The sacral hiatus, scapular angle, iliac tubercle and posterior superior iliac spine are all recognised landmarks for other purposes but do not reliably estimate this specific lumbar level.
  21. The correct answer is D (Medial to the femoral artery, within the femoral sheath): within the femoral triangle, the femoral neurovascular structures are arranged from lateral to medial as nerve, artery, vein, with the femoral vein lying medial to the femoral artery and both contained within the femoral sheath, medial to the pulsatile femoral artery which serves as the palpable landmark for cannulation. This lateral-to-medial arrangement is commonly remembered using the mnemonic NAVEL, standing for nerve, artery, vein, empty space and lymphatics. Correctly identifying this relationship reduces the risk of inadvertent arterial puncture during femoral venous cannulation.
  22. The correct answer is A (Hypercapnia dilates healthy vessels, diverting blood away from the ischaemic territory): intracerebral steal occurs when a region of brain is supplied by a vessel with a fixed, maximally dilated stenosis that can no longer respond to carbon dioxide, while surrounding healthy vessels retain normal carbon dioxide reactivity. During hypercapnia, the healthy vessels dilate further and their vascular resistance falls, diverting blood flow preferentially towards these already well-perfused regions and away from the fixed, already maximally dilated stenotic territory, worsening ischaemia in the vulnerable area. This is the rationale for avoiding hypercapnia, and sometimes deliberately inducing mild hypocapnia, in patients with known fixed cerebrovascular stenoses undergoing anaesthesia.
  23. The correct answer is C (Above 20 to 25 mmHg, the generally accepted treatment threshold): normal intracranial pressure in a supine adult is generally considered to be below 10 to 15 mmHg, and standard neurocritical care guidance recommends initiating active treatment to lower intracranial pressure once it sustainedly exceeds approximately 20 to 25 mmHg, since pressures above this threshold are associated with reduced cerebral perfusion pressure and an increased risk of secondary brain injury and herniation. Values below 20 mmHg do not typically warrant escalation of specific intracranial pressure-directed therapy, while waiting until pressures reach 40 or 60 mmHg would represent a dangerous delay in treatment.
  24. The correct answer is B (Five subunits arranged around a central ion channel pore): the mature postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptor at the adult neuromuscular junction is a pentameric structure composed of five subunits, specifically two alpha subunits along with one each of beta, delta and epsilon subunits, arranged around a central ion channel pore. Acetylcholine, or a depolarising neuromuscular blocking drug, must bind to both alpha subunits to open the channel and allow cation flux, producing depolarisation. This pentameric structure differs from the fetal isoform of the receptor, which contains a gamma subunit in place of the epsilon subunit, and understanding this structure underlies the mechanism of action of both depolarising and non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking drugs.
  25. The correct answer is A (cerebral blood flow falls below the pressure-dependent threshold, risking cerebral ischaemia): Chronic hypertension causes structural and functional adaptation of cerebral resistance vessels, shifting the entire autoregulatory curve to the right so that both the lower and upper limits sit at higher pressures. A mean arterial pressure of 65 mmHg, which would be well tolerated in a normotensive patient, now falls below this patient's raised lower limit of autoregulation. Below the lower limit, cerebral blood flow becomes pressure-passive and falls linearly with pressure, risking watershed ischaemia. This is why hypertensive patients require higher perioperative blood pressure targets than normotensive patients.
  26. The correct answer is C (displacement of cerebrospinal fluid into the spinal subarachnoid space): The Monro-Kellie doctrine describes the cranium as a rigid compartment containing brain, blood and cerebrospinal fluid in fixed total volume, so an expanding mass such as a chronic subdural haematoma is initially compensated for by displacement of cerebrospinal fluid out of the cranial vault into the spinal subarachnoid space, along with reduction in cerebral venous blood volume. This compensation allows intracranial pressure and clinical status to remain relatively stable while the reserve capacity persists. Once these compensatory volumes are exhausted, further expansion produces a steep and often rapid rise in intracranial pressure and neurological deterioration. Neuronal regeneration and skull expansion do not occur in this timeframe in an adult.
  27. The correct answer is E (C fibres have a small diameter and short critical axonal length needing block): Local anaesthetic must block a minimum length of axon, the critical length, to prevent action potential propagation, and this length is shorter in small-diameter unmyelinated C fibres than in large myelinated A-beta fibres. Because the drug diffuses along the nerve as a concentration gradient, the short critical length of C fibres is exposed to blocking concentrations first, producing early loss of dull pain sensation. A-beta fibres, being larger with longer internodal distances, require a greater length of exposure before conduction fails. Options A to D describe properties that are either incorrect or do not determine the order of differential block.
  28. The correct answer is B (the highlighted level corresponds to the nipple, indicating an adequate surgical block height): The T4 dermatome lies at the nipple line, and a sensory block extending to this level is the accepted minimum for comfortable caesarean section under spinal anaesthesia, since it covers the visceral peritoneal stimulation from uterine exteriorisation. The finding that sensation returns two levels below at T6, the xiphisternum, confirms the block extends precisely to T4 rather than higher. A block failing to reach T4 typically produces visceral discomfort during peritoneal traction. This dermatomal landmark is routinely used clinically to confirm adequate block height before surgical incision.
  29. The correct answer is D (cerebral vasoconstriction reduces cerebral blood flow and cerebral blood volume): Hypocapnia causes constriction of cerebral resistance arterioles through alkalinisation of perivascular cerebrospinal fluid, which reduces both cerebral blood flow and cerebral blood volume, the mechanism exploited to reduce brain bulk during craniotomy. This response is roughly linear between a PaCO2 of about 3 and 10 kPa, beyond which the response plateaus. Excessive hyperventilation risks cerebral ischaemia because vasoconstriction can reduce flow to critically low levels despite normal systemic oxygenation. Option A and C describe the opposite response, seen with hypercapnia rather than hypocapnia.
  30. The correct answer is A (compensatory reserve is exhausted, so small volume increases now cause large pressure rises): The intracranial pressure-volume curve is initially flat, as compensatory mechanisms such as displacement of cerebrospinal fluid and venous blood accommodate an expanding mass. Once these reserves are exhausted, the curve becomes steep, so even small further increases in volume, such as oedema or a small haematoma, produce disproportionately large rises in intracranial pressure. This exponential relationship explains why patients can deteriorate rapidly and unpredictably once decompensation begins. The adult skull is rigid and cannot expand, unlike an infant's skull with open sutures and fontanelles.
  31. The correct answer is B (relative resistance to suxamethonium, often requiring a larger dose for effect): In myasthenia gravis, autoantibodies reduce the number of functional postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, so a standard dose of suxamethonium, which acts by depolarising these receptors, produces a smaller clinical effect than expected. Patients with myasthenia gravis characteristically show resistance to depolarising neuromuscular blockers but increased sensitivity to non-depolarising agents, which compete for the already reduced pool of receptors. This distinction is clinically important when planning muscle relaxation for these patients. Suxamethonium acts postsynaptically, not presynaptically, so option D is incorrect.
  32. The correct answer is E (L4, supplying the medial malleolus and medial great toe): The L4 dermatome runs down the anteromedial thigh and leg to the medial malleolus and the medial side of the great toe, a landmark commonly used clinically alongside the knee jerk reflex, which also tests L4. L5 supplies the dorsum of the foot and the middle toes, while S1 covers the lateral foot and little toe with the ankle jerk. Recognising this sequential arrangement allows accurate assessment of block height and level during neuraxial techniques. T12 and L3 supply more proximal regions of the inguinal area and anterior thigh respectively.
  33. The correct answer is C (reduced blood viscosity and hypoxaemia-driven vasodilation increase cerebral flow to maintain oxygen delivery): Severe anaemia reduces blood viscosity, which lowers cerebral vascular resistance, and reduced arterial oxygen content also directly promotes cerebral vasodilation, both acting to increase cerebral blood flow and preserve oxygen delivery to brain tissue. This compensatory response maintains cerebral oxygen delivery close to normal until haemoglobin falls to critically low levels. Cerebral autoregulation for pressure and carbon dioxide reactivity remain intact in isolated anaemia, distinguishing this from pathological states where autoregulation fails. Increased viscosity, as seen in polycythaemia, has the opposite effect of raising resistance and reducing flow.
  34. The correct answer is D (head elevation and a neutral neck promote cerebral venous drainage, lowering intracranial blood volume): Moderate head-up positioning and avoidance of neck rotation or tight cervical collars facilitate unobstructed drainage through the internal jugular veins, reducing cerebral venous blood volume and therefore intracranial pressure, consistent with the Monro-Kellie doctrine. Rotating the head can compress the contralateral internal jugular vein, impairing venous outflow and raising intracranial pressure. Excessive head elevation beyond about thirty degrees can reduce cerebral perfusion pressure by lowering mean arterial pressure at the level of the brain, so a moderate angle balances venous drainage against perfusion. This positioning is a simple, low-risk component of intracranial pressure management.
  35. The correct answer is A (it lies between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage and the superior border of the cricoid cartilage): The cricothyroid membrane is a fibroelastic structure spanning the gap between these two cartilages, and it is the target for emergency surgical or needle airway access because it lies relatively superficially and avoids the thyroid isthmus and major vessels. Option B describes the thyrohyoid membrane, which connects the hyoid bone to the thyroid cartilage, while option C describes the cricotracheal ligament. The membrane lies anteriorly in the midline, not posteriorly or laterally, making options D and E incorrect. Accurate identification of this membrane is a core skill tested in the Primary FRCA OSCE.
  36. The correct answer is E (presynaptic nicotinic autoreceptor blockade reduces mobilisation of acetylcholine with repeated stimulation): Non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking agents also block presynaptic nicotinic autoreceptors that normally facilitate acetylcholine mobilisation during high-frequency stimulation, so with repeated stimuli less acetylcholine is available to compete with the blocking drug at the postsynaptic receptor, producing progressive fade. This train-of-four fade is characteristic of non-depolarising block and is absent in a normal, unblocked phase one depolarising block, which shows no fade. Monitoring fade allows assessment of block depth and adequacy of reversal before extubation. Depolarising blockers cause a different pattern of desensitisation rather than presynaptic-mediated fade.
  37. The correct answer is B (T1 and T2 dermatomes via the intercostobrachial and medial cutaneous nerves of the arm): The skin of the medial upper arm is supplied by the intercostobrachial nerve, a lateral cutaneous branch of the second intercostal nerve, together with the medial cutaneous nerve of the arm from the medial cord, and these fibres are often spared by an axillary brachial plexus block because the intercostobrachial nerve arises outside the plexus proper. This is why supplementary subcutaneous infiltration is sometimes needed if a tourniquet will be applied to the upper arm. The other dermatomes listed correspond to different, more distal regions of the upper limb supplied entirely by brachial plexus branches.
  38. The correct answer is D (C5 and C6 form the upper trunk, C7 alone forms the middle trunk, and C8 with T1 form the lower trunk): The five roots of the brachial plexus, C5 to T1, combine in the posterior triangle of the neck to form three trunks, with C5 and C6 uniting to form the upper trunk, C7 continuing alone as the middle trunk, and C8 and T1 joining to form the lower trunk. An interscalene block is placed at this level and therefore reliably blocks the upper and middle trunks, explaining its excellent coverage of the shoulder but relative sparing of the lower trunk contribution to the hand, notably the ulnar nerve. This root-to-trunk arrangement is fundamental to understanding block distribution at different approaches.
  39. The correct answer is C (cerebral metabolic rate falls while cerebral blood flow increases due to direct vasodilation): Volatile anaesthetic agents reduce cerebral metabolic rate for oxygen but simultaneously cause direct cerebral vasodilation, uncoupling flow from metabolism, so cerebral blood flow can actually increase at higher concentrations despite reduced metabolic demand. This effect is dose dependent and becomes more pronounced above about one minimum alveolar concentration, and it can raise intracranial pressure in patients with reduced intracranial compliance. In contrast, intravenous agents such as propofol tend to preserve coupling between flow and metabolism, which is one reason they are often preferred for neuroanaesthesia. Cerebral autoregulation is also impaired at higher volatile concentrations rather than enhanced.
  40. The correct answer is A (the medial one and a half fingers and hypothenar eminence, supplied by the ulnar nerve): The lower trunk is formed from C8 and T1 roots and continues via the medial cord to become the main contribution to the ulnar nerve, which supplies the medial one and a half fingers and the hypothenar eminence. If the lower trunk were spared during a supraclavicular block, this ulnar distribution would retain sensation while the upper and middle trunk territories, including the thumb, radial forearm and shoulder, would be blocked. This explains why supraclavicular blocks, performed at the trunk level, carry a small but recognised risk of missing ulnar-sided sensation if local anaesthetic spread is uneven around the lower trunk. The median, radial, musculocutaneous and axillary nerves listed in the other options arise mainly from the upper and middle trunk contributions.
  41. The correct answer is E (mannitol creates an osmotic gradient that draws water from brain tissue into plasma, reducing brain volume): Mannitol is a hyperosmolar agent that does not readily cross an intact blood-brain barrier, so it establishes an osmotic gradient across the barrier that draws water out of brain tissue and into the intravascular space, reducing brain water content and intracranial pressure within minutes. A later, additional effect includes reduced blood viscosity improving microcirculatory flow and a subsequent diuresis, but these occur after the initial osmotic effect. If the blood-brain barrier is disrupted, mannitol can cross into brain tissue and worsen oedema through reverse osmosis, which is why its use is more effective in regions with intact barrier function.
  42. The correct answer is B (muscle strength improves with repeated exercise due to calcium accumulation presynaptically): In Eaton-Lambert myasthenic syndrome, antibodies reduce the number of functional presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels, impairing acetylcholine release, but repeated muscle activity causes calcium to accumulate within the nerve terminal, transiently increasing acetylcholine release and producing the characteristic post-exercise facilitation of strength, the opposite pattern to myasthenia gravis. These patients are markedly sensitive to both depolarising and non-depolarising neuromuscular blocking agents because baseline neuromuscular transmission is already impaired, so doses should be reduced and monitored carefully with a nerve stimulator. Anticholinesterase response is typically poor compared with myasthenia gravis because the defect is presynaptic rather than postsynaptic.
  43. The correct answer is D (axillary and radial nerves): The posterior cord of the brachial plexus, formed from the posterior divisions of all three trunks, terminates as the axillary nerve and the radial nerve, having also given off proximal branches including the upper and lower subscapular nerves and the thoracodorsal nerve. The lateral cord terminates as the musculocutaneous nerve and contributes to the median nerve, while the medial cord contributes the ulnar nerve and the other head of the median nerve. Remembering this cord-to-terminal-branch relationship is essential for predicting which movements and sensory areas are affected by a block or injury at a particular level of the plexus.
  44. The correct answer is C (the umbilicus): The T10 dermatome corresponds to the level of the umbilicus, a landmark commonly remembered alongside T4 at the nipple line and T6 at the xiphisternum, and it is frequently used clinically both to assess sensory levels after spinal cord injury and to confirm block height during neuraxial anaesthesia. The inguinal ligament corresponds approximately to the L1 dermatome, and the costal margin lies closer to T7 to T8. Accurate recall of these landmarks allows rapid clinical localisation of a sensory level at the bedside before formal imaging confirms the anatomical extent of any lesion.
  45. The correct answer is A (C6, supplying the lateral forearm and thumb): The C6 dermatome runs along the lateral forearm to the thumb and index finger and is a key landmark for confirming the extent of an interscalene or supraclavicular brachial plexus block, since it is often tested alongside the biceps reflex, which is also predominantly C6. C5 covers the lateral upper arm over the regimental badge area of the deltoid, while C7, C8 and T1 progressively cover the middle finger, little finger and medial forearm respectively as the dermatomes descend around the hand. Testing sensation at this specific level helps distinguish an interscalene block that covers the shoulder but has not yet extended distally to the forearm and hand.
  46.
