The correct answer is B (long thoracic nerve, from the roots of C5, C6 and C7): The long thoracic nerve arises directly from the roots of C5, C6 and C7 before the trunks are formed, and it runs down the medial wall of the axilla to supply serratus anterior, making it vulnerable during axillary dissection or mastectomy surgery. Injury produces winging of the scapula because serratus anterior normally holds the scapula against the chest wall. The suprascapular nerve arises from the upper trunk, the thoracodorsal and axillary nerves arise from the posterior cord, and the dorsal scapular nerve arises from the C5 root rather than the lower trunk, making the other options incorrect.
  47. The correct answer is E (the cricoid cartilage is the only complete ring of cartilage in the airway, shaped like a signet ring): Unlike the C-shaped tracheal rings, the cricoid cartilage forms a complete ring, narrower anteriorly and broader posteriorly, giving it its signet ring shape, and this completeness is exploited clinically when applying cricoid pressure during rapid sequence induction. The epiglottis is a single, unpaired leaf-shaped cartilage, while the arytenoid cartilages are paired and sit posteriorly, articulating with the cricoid cartilage and attaching to the vocal cords. Several laryngeal cartilages, including the thyroid, cricoid and arytenoid cartilages, undergo variable ossification with age, so the thyroid cartilage is not the only one that does. Accurate knowledge of this cartilaginous anatomy underpins safe management of the difficult airway.
  48. The correct answer is D (the thyroid cartilage): The cricothyroid membrane spans the space between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage superiorly and the superior border of the cricoid cartilage inferiorly, so the thyroid cartilage, palpable as the laryngeal prominence or Adam's apple, lies immediately above it. The hyoid bone lies further superior, above the thyrohyoid membrane, and the first tracheal ring lies below the cricoid cartilage rather than directly adjacent to the cricothyroid membrane. The thyroid isthmus typically overlies the second to fourth tracheal rings, below the level relevant to cricothyroidotomy, though anatomical variation exists. Correct identification of these relations avoids injury to the thyroid gland during emergency front-of-neck access.
  49. The correct answer is C (the subclavian artery): At the supraclavicular level, the trunks and divisions of the brachial plexus lie just superior and posterior to the subclavian artery as it crosses the first rib, and this close relationship is why the plexus is sometimes described as being in the corner of a pocket formed by the artery and the first rib. The subclavian artery and the underlying pleura are the key structures to avoid during needle advancement, since inadvertent puncture can cause haematoma or pneumothorax. The internal jugular vein and common carotid artery lie more medially in the neck, while the vertebral artery arises from the subclavian artery but is not the vessel immediately adjacent to the plexus at this level.
  50. The correct answer is B (the recurrent laryngeal nerve and phrenic nerve, causing hoarseness and hemidiaphragmatic paresis): Local anaesthetic injected around the upper and middle trunks at the interscalene level readily spreads to the adjacent phrenic nerve, which runs on the anterior surface of scalenus anterior, producing hemidiaphragmatic paresis and breathlessness, and can also affect the recurrent laryngeal nerve, producing hoarseness from ipsilateral vocal cord paresis. Ipsilateral phrenic nerve palsy occurs in a very high proportion of interscalene blocks and is usually well tolerated in healthy patients but can be significant in those with respiratory compromise. Horner's syndrome from stellate ganglion spread can also occur but presents with ptosis, miosis and anhidrosis rather than voice or breathing changes, distinguishing it from this presentation.
  51. The correct answer is A (the internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve, a branch of the vagus nerve): The internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve pierces the thyrohyoid membrane and supplies sensory innervation to the laryngeal mucosa from the epiglottis down to the level of the vocal cords, and it is commonly blocked bilaterally to facilitate awake fibreoptic intubation. The external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve is purely motor, supplying cricothyroid muscle, while the recurrent laryngeal nerve provides sensory innervation below the vocal cords and motor supply to the remaining intrinsic laryngeal muscles. The glossopharyngeal nerve supplies the posterior third of the tongue and oropharynx, an area anaesthetised separately, often by gargled local anaesthetic or nerve block, before airway instrumentation.
  52. The correct answer is D (the recurrent laryngeal nerve): The recurrent laryngeal nerve runs in close proximity to the inferior thyroid artery and posterior to the thyroid gland before entering the larynx, making it vulnerable during thyroidectomy, and unilateral injury classically produces a weak, hoarse voice with the affected cord fixed in a paramedian position due to unopposed action of the intact cricothyroid muscle. Bilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury is far more dangerous, as both cords can adopt a paramedian position and cause airway obstruction requiring urgent tracheostomy. The external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve is also at risk during thyroid surgery, particularly near the superior pole, but injury produces voice pitch changes rather than this classic hoarse paramedian cord picture.
  53. The correct answer is E (the ligamentum flavum): The ligamentum flavum is a dense, elastic ligament connecting adjacent laminae, and it is the last structure the needle passes through before entering the epidural space, producing the characteristic increase in resistance followed by a sudden loss of resistance as the needle tip exits the ligament. The dura and arachnoid mater lie beyond the epidural space and would only be encountered if the needle were advanced too far, producing a dural tap. The posterior longitudinal ligament lies anteriorly within the vertebral canal, and the lamina is bony rather than ligamentous, both of which are not encountered in the standard midline trajectory through the interlaminar space.
  54. The correct answer is C (the internal jugular vein lies anterolateral to the common carotid artery within the carotid sheath): The internal jugular vein, common carotid artery and vagus nerve are enclosed together within the carotid sheath, with the vein typically lying anterolateral to the artery, and the vagus nerve positioned posteriorly between the two, an arrangement that guides safe needle trajectory during cannulation. This relationship can vary anatomically between individuals, which is why ultrasound guidance is now recommended as the standard of care rather than relying on landmark technique alone. The vein and artery share the same fascial sheath rather than being in separate compartments, making option D incorrect.
  55. The correct answer is A (the vocal cords, marking the narrowest part of the adult glottis): The glottis, at the level of the vocal cords, represents the narrowest fixed part of the adult airway and the final structure the fibrescope must pass before entering the trachea. The vallecula lies anterior to the epiglottis and is a landmark for laryngoscope blade placement rather than a narrowing of the airway itself. The cricoid cartilage lies below the vocal cords and marks the start of the subglottic airway rather than the oesophageal entrance, which lies posteriorly at the pharyngo-oesophageal junction. Correct identification of this sagittal anatomy on the diagram is essential when planning awake fibreoptic intubation.
  56. The correct answer is B (the recurrent laryngeal nerve): Below the level of the vocal cords, sensory innervation of the laryngeal and upper tracheal mucosa is provided by the recurrent laryngeal nerve, which also supplies motor fibres to all intrinsic laryngeal muscles except cricothyroid. Transtracheal injection of local anaesthetic, typically through the cricothyroid membrane, anaesthetises this subglottic mucosa and often provokes a cough that helps spread the drug upward towards the cords. Above the vocal cords, sensation is instead carried by the internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve, which is why bilateral superior laryngeal nerve blocks are performed separately to cover this supraglottic region. The glossopharyngeal and hypoglossal nerves supply the oropharynx and tongue musculature respectively, not the larynx itself.
  57. The correct answer is D (the spinal cord typically ends at L1/2, allowing safe needle insertion below this level): In adults, the spinal cord terminates as the conus medullaris at approximately the L1/2 vertebral level, below which the vertebral canal contains only the cauda equina, a bundle of nerve roots floating in cerebrospinal fluid, so needle insertion at L3/4 or L4/5 carries minimal risk of direct spinal cord injury. The dural sac itself extends further, typically ending around S2, which is why cerebrospinal fluid can still be aspirated at these lower lumbar levels. In neonates, the cord ends more caudally at around L3, an important difference when performing lumbar puncture in this age group. Option E is incorrect as the cord does not extend the full length of the canal in adults.
  58. The correct answer is E (it is superficial, relatively avascular in the midline, and reliably palpable even in obese patients): The cricothyroid membrane is chosen for emergency surgical airway access because it lies close to the skin surface, the midline is relatively avascular compared with surrounding structures, and it remains identifiable by palpation or ultrasound even when anatomical landmarks are distorted by obesity or oedema. It does not lie deep to the thyroid isthmus, which usually overlies lower tracheal rings, and small but clinically significant vessels, such as the cricothyroid artery, can cross the membrane and must be considered during the procedure. It is not the widest part of the airway, since the trachea below is wider, but its accessibility outweighs this in an emergency.
  59. The correct answer is C (the dura mater, allowing cerebrospinal fluid to leak into the epidural space): The dura mater is the tough outer meningeal layer enclosing the arachnoid mater and the cerebrospinal fluid-filled subarachnoid space, and an unintended puncture of this layer by an epidural needle allows cerebrospinal fluid to leak into the epidural space, reducing intracranial cerebrospinal fluid pressure and causing traction on pain-sensitive intracranial structures, producing a post-dural puncture headache. The ligamentum flavum and interspinous ligament are structures the needle passes through before reaching the epidural space and are not themselves associated with this complication. The posterior longitudinal ligament lies anterior within the vertebral canal and is not encountered during a standard posterior approach.
  60. The correct answer is B (the ilioinguinal nerve, supplying the inguinal region at L1): The L1 dermatome covers the inguinal region and upper medial thigh and is supplied predominantly by the ilioinguinal nerve, a branch of the lumbar plexus that runs separately from the femoral nerve, which instead supplies the L2 to L4 dermatomes over the anterior and medial thigh and leg via its cutaneous branches. Because a femoral nerve block does not reliably cover the L1 dermatome, sensation in the groin can remain intact even with a dense femoral block, which is clinically relevant when planning anaesthesia for procedures extending into the groin. The obturator nerve supplies a variable strip of the medial thigh, and the sciatic nerve supplies the posterior thigh and most of the leg below the knee, both distinct from the highlighted L1 territory.
  61. The correct answer is A (the posterior third of the tongue and oropharynx are supplied by the glossopharyngeal nerve): The glossopharyngeal nerve provides general sensory innervation to the posterior third of the tongue, the oropharynx and the tonsillar region, and topical local anaesthetic sprayed onto these structures, or a formal glossopharyngeal nerve block, reduces gag reflex and discomfort during fibreoptic scope passage. The anterior two-thirds of the tongue receive sensory supply from the lingual nerve, a branch of the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve, not the vagus nerve. Nasal mucosa is supplied by branches of the trigeminal nerve rather than the facial nerve, and the soft palate receives sensory fibres mainly from the trigeminal and glossopharyngeal nerves rather than the hypoglossal nerve, which is purely motor to the tongue.
  62. The correct answer is E (the dome of the pleura lies immediately posterior and inferior to the subclavian vein as it crosses the first rib): The subclavian vein passes anterior to the first rib and scalenus anterior, with the pleural dome lying immediately posterior and inferior to it at this point, so a needle directed too steeply or advanced too far during subclavian cannulation risks puncturing the pleura and causing a pneumothorax. The subclavian artery lies posterior to scalenus anterior and is separated from the vein by this muscle, not simply deeper along the same path. The apex of the lung can extend above the level of the first rib in some patients, further increasing risk rather than reducing it, making option B incorrect. The phrenic nerve does cross the anterior surface of scalenus anterior nearby but is not the structure explaining pneumothorax risk.
  63. The correct answer is B (Boyle's law applies, and the pressure will approximately double): Boyle's law states that at constant temperature, the pressure of a fixed mass of gas is inversely proportional to its volume, so pressure multiplied by volume remains constant. Halving the volume at constant temperature therefore requires pressure to approximately double to keep this product unchanged. Charles's law and Gay-Lussac's law both involve temperature changing, which is explicitly excluded here since temperature is held constant. The universal gas law reduces to Boyle's law under these isothermal conditions, so it gives the same doubling prediction, not a quartering.
  64. The correct answer is D (Volume is directly proportional to absolute temperature at constant pressure): Charles's law describes exactly this scenario, stating that at constant pressure the volume of a fixed mass of gas increases in direct proportion to its absolute temperature in kelvin. As the Entonox warms from ambient cold towards room temperature while pressure stays constant, its volume rises proportionally rather than falling or staying fixed. An inverse relationship between volume and temperature at constant pressure would violate Charles's law entirely. A squared relationship is not part of any of the simple gas laws taught at this level.
  65. The correct answer is A (Gay-Lussac's law, since pressure is directly proportional to absolute temperature at fixed volume): Gay-Lussac's law applies specifically when volume is held constant, as it is inside a rigid, sealed steel cylinder, and states that pressure rises in direct proportion to absolute temperature. As sunlight heats the cylinder, the trapped gas cannot expand, so the rising kinetic energy of the gas molecules is expressed entirely as increased pressure. Boyle's law requires constant temperature, which is not the case here, and Charles's law requires constant pressure, which also does not apply. Dalton's and Henry's laws concern partial pressures and gas solubility respectively, neither of which is relevant to a single sealed gas heating at fixed volume.
  66. The correct answer is E (The curve is a rectangular hyperbola consistent with Boyle's law): The chart shown plots volume against pressure at constant temperature for a fixed mass of gas, and the curved shape falling steeply then flattening as pressure rises is the classic rectangular hyperbola produced when pressure multiplied by volume equals a constant. This shape directly demonstrates Boyle's law rather than Charles's law, which would instead show a straight line relationship between volume and absolute temperature at constant pressure. The curve does not indicate an isobaric process, since pressure is clearly changing throughout, and it cannot demonstrate critical temperature because temperature itself is held fixed in this experiment. A linear falling relationship would produce a straight line rather than the curved hyperbola shown.
  67. The correct answer is C (The temperature above which a gas cannot be liquefied by pressure alone): Critical temperature is defined as the temperature above which a substance can no longer be converted to a liquid no matter how much pressure is applied to it. Nitrous oxide has a critical temperature of 36.5 degrees Celsius, so at typical room temperature it remains below this threshold and can exist as a liquid under the pressure inside a sealed cylinder. This is distinct from boiling point, which is the temperature at which a liquid turns to vapour at a specified fixed pressure such as one atmosphere. It is also unrelated to the temperature at which a substance freezes or to how closely a gas obeys the ideal gas law.
  68. The correct answer is B (The pressure gauge stays constant until nearly all liquid has evaporated, then falls): While liquid nitrous oxide remains present in the cylinder, the gauge reads the saturated vapour pressure above the liquid, which stays essentially constant as long as any liquid remains, regardless of how much liquid is left. Only once all the liquid has evaporated does the cylinder contain purely gaseous nitrous oxide, and from that point the pressure falls in proportion to the remaining contents as gas is withdrawn. This means the gauge cannot reliably indicate how much Entonox remains until the liquid phase is exhausted, unlike a purely gaseous cylinder such as oxygen. The cylinder does contain liquid at ordinary room temperature precisely because that temperature sits below the critical temperature, and refrigeration well below freezing is not required for this to occur.
  69. The correct answer is A (The Entonox cylinder body is coloured blue and white in a quartered pattern): Entonox, the premixed nitrous oxide and oxygen analgesic gas, is identified in the United Kingdom by a body painted in a blue and white quartered pattern to distinguish it clearly from single-gas cylinders. Under the current UK cylinder colour standard, pure oxygen cylinders instead have a plain white body throughout, and pure nitrous oxide cylinders have a plain french blue body throughout. This distinct colour scheme reduces the risk of a member of staff mistakenly connecting the wrong gas mixture during patient care. Colour coding is a visual safety aid used alongside, not instead of, the pin index system that mechanically prevents an incorrect physical connection.
  70. The correct answer is D (The pin index system, which uses a unique hole arrangement for each gas): The pin index system uses a specific arrangement of pins on the cylinder yoke that must align with a matching pattern of holes on the cylinder valve block, and each medical gas has its own unique pin configuration. This mechanical safeguard prevents a nitrous oxide cylinder from being physically attached to a yoke designed for oxygen, regardless of any error in visual checking. The Bodok seal is simply the fibre washer that creates a gas-tight joint once the correct cylinder is already correctly connected, and does not itself prevent wrong connections. Colour coding and screw thread systems provide additional layers of safety but are not the mechanism responsible for physically blocking this specific mismatched attachment.
  71. The correct answer is E (Approximately 1.1 kPa): One millimetre of mercury is approximately equal to 0.133 kilopascals, so multiplying 8 mmHg by 0.133 gives a value of approximately 1.1 kPa. This conversion factor arises from the density of mercury and the standard gravitational acceleration used to define the mmHg unit historically used for blood pressure measurement. The other listed values correspond to common conversion errors, such as using the wrong factor or misplacing a decimal point during the calculation. Accurate unit conversion between mmHg and kPa is a core physics skill for interpreting invasive pressure monitoring in the UK, where kPa is the preferred SI unit.
  72. The correct answer is C (Body grey with a black and white quartered shoulder): The table shown lists medical air as having a grey body with a shoulder painted in black and white quarters, distinguishing it from other grey-bodied gases such as carbon dioxide, which has a plain grey shoulder. Under the current UK cylinder colour standard, oxygen instead has a plain white body throughout, and nitrous oxide has a plain french blue body and shoulder throughout with no quartering. Entonox is the only gas in the table with a fully quartered blue and white body as well as a quartered shoulder. Correctly distinguishing these combinations is important because several UK medical gases share the same body colour and rely on the shoulder pattern for safe identification.
  73. The correct answer is B (Approximately 2.0 kPa): One kilopascal is approximately equal to 10.2 centimetres of water, so dividing 20 cmH2O by this factor gives approximately 2.0 kPa. Centimetres of water is a much smaller pressure unit than the kilopascal, which is why a clinically meaningful airway pressure of 20 cmH2O converts to a comparatively small kilopascal value. Confusing this conversion with the mmHg to kPa factor, or failing to convert at all, produces the other incorrect values listed. Understanding this conversion allows accurate comparison of ventilator pressures reported in cmH2O against arterial or venous pressures reported in kPa.
  74. The correct answer is A (Approximately 400 kPa): One bar is approximately equal to 100 kilopascals, so a pipeline working pressure of 4 bar converts to approximately 400 kPa. This relatively high working pressure is typical of piped medical gas supplies, which operate at a much greater pressure than the pressures found within the patient's own airway or circulation. Confusing bar with atmospheres, or misplacing a decimal point during conversion, produces each of the other listed incorrect values. Recognising that pipeline supply pressure is far higher than physiological pressures helps trainees understand why pressure-reducing regulators are essential components of the anaesthetic gas supply system.
  75. The correct answer is D (Flow rate is directly proportional to the pressure gradient): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation describes laminar flow through a tube and states that flow rate is directly proportional to the pressure gradient driving the fluid along the tube, all other factors being held constant. This means doubling the pressure difference across the cannula will approximately double the achievable flow rate under fully laminar conditions. A square root or squared relationship would not match the linear proportionality that the equation actually predicts for pressure gradient specifically. Stating that flow is unaffected by the pressure gradient, or inversely related to it, would contradict the basic physical principle that a greater driving force produces greater flow.
  76. The correct answer is C (Flow is proportional to the fourth power of the radius of the cannula): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation shows that flow rate is proportional to the radius of the tube raised to the fourth power, so even a modest increase in radius produces a dramatic rise in achievable laminar flow. Doubling the internal diameter therefore increases potential flow by a factor of roughly sixteen, far more than any change to length or pressure gradient alone could achieve. This fourth power relationship is the physical basis for why a short, wide-bore intravenous cannula delivers fluid so much faster than a long, narrow one. An inverse or squared relationship with radius would understate the powerful effect that diameter has on flow through a cannula.
  77. The correct answer is E (It is a dimensionless number combining density, velocity, diameter and viscosity): The Reynolds number is a dimensionless value calculated from fluid density, mean flow velocity, tube diameter and fluid viscosity, and it predicts whether flow will remain laminar or become turbulent. A low Reynolds number favours smooth laminar flow, while values above approximately two thousand indicate a transition towards turbulence within a straight tube. It is not itself a pressure or a flow rate, and it is not a fixed physical constant, since its value changes with the fluid and the flow conditions being considered. This makes it a genuinely useful predictive tool for anaesthetists assessing flow behaviour in airways and vascular catheters of differing calibre.
  78. The correct answer is B (A transition from laminar to turbulent flow as the Reynolds number rises): The chart shown demonstrates flow rising in a roughly linear fashion at lower driving pressures, consistent with laminar flow, before the curve begins to flatten as pressure and flow velocity increase further. This flattening reflects the onset of turbulence, since turbulent flow requires a disproportionately greater pressure gradient to achieve the same increase in flow compared with laminar conditions. A steady linear relationship throughout the whole range would not show this change in gradient at higher pressures. Neither a flowmeter fault nor the critical temperature of the gas would produce this characteristic curved transition specifically at higher flow rates.
  79. The correct answer is D (The annular gap around the bobbin widens with height, altering flow characteristics): A rotameter tube is tapered so that the annular gap between the bobbin and the tube wall widens progressively towards the top, changing the resistance to flow through that gap as the bobbin rises. The bobbin settles at the height where the upward force from gas flow through this widening gap balances its own weight, and that height is calibrated to correspond to a specific flow rate for a given gas. The bobbin does not sink with increasing flow, since a greater flow produces a greater upward force that raises it further up the tapered tube. The reading is taken from the correct reference point on the bobbin rather than an arbitrary edge, and the tube widens rather than narrows towards the top.
  80. The correct answer is A (Spinning centres the bobbin in the tube and reduces friction against the glass wall): Rotameter bobbins are deliberately designed with angled slots or vanes that cause the gas flow passing around them to make the bobbin spin, and this rotation helps keep the bobbin centred within the tapered glass tube. Centring the bobbin in this way reduces frictional contact with the tube wall, improving the accuracy and consistency of the flow reading. Spinning is a normal, intended feature of laminar flow through the annular gap and does not itself indicate turbulence or an incorrect gas. It has a genuine functional purpose in the design and is not simply an incidental manufacturing artefact or the result of static electricity.
  81. The correct answer is C (The density and viscosity of the gas at the flow rates used): Rotameter calibration depends on the density and viscosity of the specific gas passing through the tube, because these properties determine the relationship between bobbin height and actual flow rate at both low and high flow settings. Oxygen and nitrous oxide differ in density and viscosity, so a tube calibrated for one gas will give an inaccurate reading if a different gas is passed through it. Colour and odour of the gas have no bearing on the physical flow characteristics that determine bobbin position. Critical temperature and molecular weight measured at absolute zero are not the properties that govern flow-dependent behaviour across the working range of the rotameter.
  82. The correct answer is E (It divides incoming fresh gas between a bypass channel and the vaporising chamber): The splitting ratio describes how incoming fresh gas is divided between a bypass pathway that carries gas straight through the vaporiser unchanged and a separate pathway that passes through the vaporising chamber, where it becomes saturated with volatile agent vapour. Adjusting the concentration dial changes this splitting ratio, altering the proportion of gas exposed to the vaporising chamber and therefore the final agent concentration delivered to the patient circuit. The splitting ratio does not fix total fresh gas flow, which is instead set independently by the anaesthetist using the flow control valves. It also does not directly control oxygen concentration or measure remaining liquid volume, both of which are separate functions of the anaesthetic machine.
  83. The correct answer is B (A bimetallic strip or expanding bellows valve that automatically alters the splitting ratio): Modern variable bypass vaporisers use a temperature-sensitive component, such as a bimetallic strip or an expanding bellows valve, that automatically adjusts the splitting ratio as the vaporising chamber cools during use. As cooling from latent heat of vaporisation would otherwise reduce vapour output, this mechanism increases the proportion of fresh gas diverted through the vaporising chamber to compensate, keeping delivered concentration stable. This automatic compensation removes the need for the anaesthetist to make continuous manual adjustments during a case. Electrical heating elements and pressure relief valves are features of different vaporiser designs and do not describe the standard temperature compensation mechanism used in this type of device.
  84. The correct answer is D (Approximately 101.3 kPa): The table shown confirms that one standard atmosphere is approximately equal to 101.3 kilopascals, which is also equivalent to 760 mmHg or approximately 1033 cmH2O. This value forms the baseline against which many clinical and physiological pressures are compared, including barometric pressure used in blood gas interpretation. The other values listed represent common errors such as misplacing a decimal point or confusing the atmosphere conversion with the bar conversion given elsewhere in the same table. Being able to move confidently between these units is a core physics skill tested in the written examination.
  85. The correct answer is A (This is macroshock, since current entered through intact skin at low resistance sites): Macroshock describes an electric shock in which current passes through intact skin and encounters the relatively high resistance of the outer skin layers, generally requiring a larger current to cause any significant physiological effect. Because this patient's skin was unbroken and the shock produced only a brief tingling sensation, this is a classic example of macroshock rather than the far more dangerous microshock, which requires direct contact with the heart. Capacitive coupling and earth leakage describe different mechanisms of current transmission that are not indicated by the scenario described. Classifying an event as macroshock does not require knowledge of the equipment's specific Type BF rating, since the classification depends on the pathway and magnitude of current involved.
  86. The correct answer is C (Current applied directly to the heart bypasses the high resistance of intact skin): A pacing wire in direct contact with the myocardium provides a low-resistance pathway straight to the heart, bypassing the protective high resistance normally provided by intact skin. This means an extremely small current, far below the threshold that would cause any harm via the skin, can still induce ventricular fibrillation when applied directly to cardiac tissue, a phenomenon known as microshock. Pacing wires do not increase total body resistance, and the wire itself does not generate current but simply conducts any stray current present. Microshock risk depends critically on the current's pathway through the body, and the current thresholds involved are far smaller than the milliamp range associated with macroshock via intact skin.
  87. The correct answer is E (Approximately 100 microamps): A current of approximately 100 microamps applied directly to the myocardium via an intracardiac pathway is generally quoted as sufficient to risk inducing ventricular fibrillation, illustrating how much smaller the dangerous threshold is for microshock compared with macroshock via intact skin. By contrast, currents in the range of many tens to over one hundred milliamps are typically required to cause similar harm when applied across intact skin, since skin resistance dissipates most of the current before it reaches the heart. This large difference in threshold, roughly a thousandfold, is the fundamental reason why microshock precautions are so important around patients with direct cardiac connections. The other values listed either understate or substantially overstate the widely quoted intracardiac threshold.
  88. The correct answer is B (Type CF has a stricter, lower leakage current limit than Type BF): Type CF equipment is designed with the lowest permitted leakage current of the standard classifications specifically because it may be connected directly to the heart, where even a very small stray current poses a microshock risk. Type BF equipment has a floating, isolated patient circuit that reduces shock risk compared with Type B, but its permitted leakage current is still higher than the stricter limit applied to Type CF devices. The two classifications therefore do not share identical limits, and Type BF is not restricted to direct cardiac connection, since that role belongs specifically to Type CF. A floating patient circuit is a feature Type CF equipment retains alongside its lower leakage limit, not a feature it lacks.
  89. The correct answer is A (It provides a low-resistance path so fault current trips the circuit breaker): Earthing connects exposed metal parts of mains-powered equipment to earth via a low-resistance conductor, so that if a fault develops and mains voltage reaches the casing, a large fault current flows to earth rather than through anyone touching the equipment. This large fault current is designed to trip the circuit breaker or blow a fuse rapidly, disconnecting the faulty equipment from the mains supply before serious harm can occur. Earthing does not prevent a fault current from developing in the first place, and it does not isolate the patient circuit from mains, which is instead the function of isolation transformers used in some equipment. It also does not alter the type of current involved or change the patient's own body resistance.
  90. The correct answer is D (Approximately 2000): The chart shown demonstrates the Reynolds number rising steadily with increasing flow velocity through a fixed-diameter tube, and the conventional transition point from laminar to turbulent flow in a straight tube is widely quoted as occurring at a Reynolds number of approximately 2000. Below this critical value, flow tends to remain smooth and laminar, while above it flow becomes increasingly disorganised and turbulent, requiring greater pressure to drive the same flow rate. The other values listed either understate or considerably overstate the generally accepted transition threshold used in respiratory and cardiovascular physics teaching. This transition point is a key reason why airway narrowing, which increases local flow velocity, tends to provoke turbulence and audible noisy breathing.
  91. The correct answer is C (Narrowing increases flow velocity and disrupts smooth parallel layers of gas): When an airway narrows, the same volume of gas must pass through a smaller cross-sectional area, which increases flow velocity at that point according to the principle of continuity. This increase in velocity raises the Reynolds number, making the disorganised, disrupted flow pattern of turbulence more likely to develop rather than the smooth parallel layers characteristic of laminar flow. Narrowing therefore does not reduce velocity or favour laminar flow, and it does not decrease the Reynolds number, both of which are the opposite of what actually occurs. Turbulence can develop in gases as well as liquids whenever the Reynolds number rises sufficiently, so it is not restricted to liquid flow alone.
  92. The correct answer is E (Current density is highest at the small active electrode, generating intense local heating): In monopolar diathermy, the same total current passes through both the small active electrode and the much larger return electrode plate, but current density, which is current per unit area, is far greater at the small active electrode tip. This high current density concentrates electrical energy into intense localised heating sufficient to cut or coagulate tissue precisely at the active electrode, while the same current spread over the much larger return plate produces negligible heating there. The return plate does not carry a higher voltage than the active electrode, and current density is deliberately unequal between the two electrodes by design, not equal. The generator delivers current continuously while activated rather than only when the active electrode contacts metal instruments.
  93. The correct answer is B (Bipolar diathermy confines current flow between the two forceps tips only): Bipolar diathermy passes current from one forceps tip through only the small amount of tissue grasped between the two tips and back to the other tip, without any current passing through the wider body or requiring a separate return electrode. This confines the electrical pathway to a very small, precisely controlled volume of tissue, making it particularly suitable for delicate dissection near structures such as digital nerves where a wider current pathway would be hazardous. Bipolar diathermy still uses alternating current, just as monopolar diathermy does, and does not require a return electrode plate placed elsewhere on the patient. It does not inherently operate at a higher power setting, and while it reduces the risk of collateral thermal spread, it does not eliminate that risk entirely.
  94. The correct answer is D (Current found an alternative path of lower resistance back to earth through the electrode): Even when a diathermy return plate is correctly applied, stray current can occasionally find an alternative, unintended pathway of lower resistance back to earth, such as through a monitoring electrode with a small area of skin contact, concentrating current density at that point and causing a burn. This occurs because current will always follow the path or paths of least overall resistance, which may not be exclusively through the intended return plate. The ECG electrode itself does not generate current independently, and the return plate functioning correctly does not rule out this alternative pathway occurring elsewhere. The pattern described, a burn coinciding with a monitoring electrode site during diathermy use, is far more consistent with stray current pathway than with an unrelated pressure injury or an accidental switch to bipolar mode.
  95. The correct answer is A (Pressure and volume become inversely proportional to one another): When temperature and the number of moles of gas are both held constant in the universal gas equation PV equals nRT, the right-hand side of the equation becomes a fixed constant, meaning pressure multiplied by volume must remain unchanged. This is mathematically identical to Boyle's law, so pressure and volume become inversely proportional to one another under these specific constant conditions. Pressure and volume do not become directly proportional, since that relationship would instead require volume and temperature to vary together at constant pressure. The universal gas constant itself is a fixed physical constant and does not change to compensate for any variable in the equation.
  96. The correct answer is C (Type CF, because it has the lowest permitted leakage current): The table shown confirms that Type CF carries the lowest permitted leakage current of the three classifications, which is precisely why it is the only category suitable for direct connection to the heart, where even minute stray currents pose a microshock risk. Type B has the highest permitted leakage current and an earthed rather than floating enclosure, making it suitable only for equipment without direct patient connection. Type BF sits between the two, offering a floating isolated patient circuit and a lower leakage limit than Type B, but its limit remains higher than that of Type CF. The three classifications therefore do not share an identical leakage current limit, and only Type CF is described in the table as suitable for direct cardiac contact.
  97. The correct answer is E (Nitrous oxide exists partly as liquid because room temperature is near its critical temperature): Nitrous oxide has a critical temperature of only 36.5 degrees Celsius, which sits very close to typical room temperature, so under the pressure inside a cylinder it exists partly as liquid rather than behaving as an ideal gas. Nitrogen and oxygen, in contrast, have critical temperatures far below room temperature, meaning they cannot be liquefied at ambient temperature no matter how much pressure is applied, so they remain gaseous and behave much more closely to an ideal gas. This difference is a property of the gas itself rather than of cylinder filling pressure or cylinder colour, neither of which determines ideal gas behaviour. Nitrous oxide's critical temperature is close to, not far below, room temperature, which is exactly why its behaviour departs so noticeably from ideal gas predictions.
  98. The correct answer is B (Bypassing the vaporiser prevents any volatile agent being delivered during the flush): The oxygen flush valve is deliberately designed to bypass the vaporiser and rotameters entirely, delivering pure oxygen directly to the common gas outlet, which ensures that activating the flush can never inadvertently deliver a bolus of concentrated volatile anaesthetic agent to the patient. If flush oxygen instead passed through the vaporiser, a large, uncontrolled volume of gas could pick up volatile agent vapour and deliver a dangerously high concentration to the breathing circuit. The flush valve substantially increases rather than reduces oxygen flow while activated, and it functions regardless of the vaporiser dial setting since it bypasses the vaporiser altogether. It also does not mix in nitrous oxide, since its purpose is specifically to deliver pure oxygen at high flow.
  99. The correct answer is D (Desflurane has a saturated vapour pressure close to atmospheric pressure at room temperature): Desflurane has an unusually high saturated vapour pressure that approaches atmospheric pressure at normal room temperature, meaning it boils at close to room temperature and would vaporise unpredictably in a standard variable bypass vaporiser. An electrically heated vaporiser instead holds desflurane at a fixed temperature above its boiling point under pressure, delivering a controlled, predictable concentration regardless of ambient temperature or fresh gas flow. Desflurane is not of unusually low potency, and it does react safely with soda lime under normal clinical use, so neither of these properties explains the need for electrical heating. Its critical temperature is well above room temperature, since it is its high vapour pressure and low boiling point, not a low critical temperature, that create the need for this specialised vaporiser design.
  100. The correct answer is A (Flow is proportional to the radius raised to the fourth power): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation shows that flow rate under laminar conditions is proportional to the radius of the tube raised to the fourth power, so halving the radius reduces achievable flow to only about one sixteenth of its previous value, far more than a simple proportional halving would suggest. This dramatic sensitivity to radius explains why even modest narrowing of a breathing circuit connector or airway can produce a disproportionately large fall in flow. A relationship based on the radius alone to the first power, or an inverse square relationship, would understate this powerful fourth power effect. Flow is certainly affected by radius even when the pressure gradient is fixed, since radius is one of the key variables in the underlying equation.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Flow is inversely proportional to viscosity under laminar conditions): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation places viscosity in the denominator of the flow equation, meaning flow rate under laminar conditions is inversely proportional to the viscosity of the fluid, so a more viscous fluid produces a lower flow rate for the same pressure gradient, radius and length. This is why viscous fluids such as blood or concentrated drug solutions require a greater driving pressure, or a wider cannula, to achieve the same flow as a less viscous fluid like normal saline. A direct or squared proportional relationship would incorrectly predict that increasing viscosity increases flow, which contradicts the physical behaviour of viscous fluids resisting motion. Flow is not independent of viscosity under laminar conditions, since viscosity is one of the four key variables the equation depends upon.
  2. The correct answer is E (Under turbulent conditions, resistance depends mainly on gas density rather than viscosity): Once flow becomes turbulent, the physics governing resistance changes fundamentally from the laminar Hagen-Poiseuille relationship, and gas density becomes the dominant factor determining resistance to flow rather than viscosity, which dominated during laminar conditions. This is why breathing a lower density gas mixture, such as helium and oxygen, can reduce the work of breathing specifically in situations where turbulent flow is present, such as significant upper airway obstruction. Tube length and radius both remain relevant to turbulent flow resistance, so resistance does not depend solely on length nor become independent of radius. Viscosity does not disappear as a factor entirely, but it becomes secondary to density once flow is turbulent, rather than remaining the only relevant property as it was under laminar conditions.
  3. The correct answer is B (Pressure is inversely proportional to volume at constant temperature): Boyle's law states that at constant temperature the pressure of a fixed mass of gas is inversely proportional to its volume, expressed as P1V1 equals P2V2. As the gas expands into a larger volume the same number of molecules collide with the container walls less frequently, so pressure falls proportionally. This law underpins the behaviour of gas cylinders and breathing systems whenever temperature can reasonably be assumed constant over the timescale of the measurement.
  4. The correct answer is D (Volume remaining is approximately 340 litres, half of the full capacity): Boyle's law states P1V1 equals P2V2 at constant temperature, so the volume of gas available at atmospheric pressure is directly proportional to the cylinder's gauge pressure. Since the gauge pressure has fallen from 137 bar to approximately 68 bar, which is roughly half of the original pressure, the equivalent volume of gas remaining is also roughly halved, giving approximately 340 litres. This proportional relationship is why the cylinder contents gauge for a compressed gas such as oxygen can be used directly to estimate remaining supply, unlike for a liquefied gas such as nitrous oxide.
  5. The correct answer is A (Volume is directly proportional to absolute temperature at constant pressure): Charles's law states that at constant pressure the volume of a fixed mass of gas is directly proportional to its absolute temperature, expressed as V1 divided by T1 equals V2 divided by T2. As the gas warms and its molecules move faster, they exert greater pressure on the container unless the volume is allowed to expand, so volume rises in step with absolute temperature. This relationship is distinct from Boyle's law, which instead relates pressure and volume at constant temperature.
  6. The correct answer is C (2.67 litres, since volume rises in proportion to absolute temperature): Charles's law states that V1 divided by T1 equals V2 divided by T2 at constant pressure, so the new volume equals the original volume multiplied by the ratio of the new to the old absolute temperature. Here this gives 2 litres multiplied by 400 kelvin divided by 300 kelvin, which equals approximately 2.67 litres. Using absolute temperature in kelvin, rather than degrees Celsius, is essential because the proportional relationship only holds on the absolute temperature scale.
  7. The correct answer is E (Pressure rises in direct proportion to the absolute temperature increase): Gay-Lussac's law states that at constant volume the pressure of a fixed mass of gas is directly proportional to its absolute temperature, expressed as P1 divided by T1 equals P2 divided by T2. Because the rigid cylinder cannot expand, the gas cannot relieve the increased molecular activity by occupying more space, so the pressure it exerts on the cylinder walls rises proportionally with absolute temperature. This is the physical basis for warnings against storing gas cylinders in hot environments, since pressure can rise to dangerous levels.
  8. The correct answer is B (PV equals nRT, where n is moles and R the gas constant): The universal, or ideal, gas law combines Boyle's, Charles's and Gay-Lussac's laws into a single equation, PV equals nRT, linking pressure, volume, the number of moles of gas present, the universal gas constant and absolute temperature. Each of the three named laws is a special case of this equation obtained by holding one variable constant, temperature for Boyle's law, pressure for Charles's law, and volume for Gay-Lussac's law. This single relationship allows calculation of any one variable when the other three are known for a given quantity of gas.
  9. The correct answer is A (Calculate n as PV divided by RT using absolute pressure and temperature): Rearranging the universal gas law PV equals nRT gives n equals PV divided by RT, so the number of moles present can be found from the absolute pressure in pascals, the volume in cubic metres, the universal gas constant and the absolute temperature in kelvin. Using gauge pressure without converting to absolute pressure, or using Celsius instead of kelvin, would give an incorrect result. This calculation demonstrates how the contents of a compressed gas cylinder can be quantified from first principles rather than by direct measurement.
  10. The correct answer is D (The temperature above which a gas cannot be liquefied by pressure alone): The critical temperature of a substance is the temperature above which it cannot be converted into a liquid no matter how great the applied pressure becomes, because the kinetic energy of the molecules is too high to allow intermolecular attractive forces to hold them together as a liquid. Below the critical temperature a gas can be liquefied by pressure, which is why nitrous oxide, with a critical temperature above room temperature, exists partly as liquid in its cylinder. Oxygen has a critical temperature far below room temperature, so it can only exist as compressed gas at normal storage conditions.
  11. The correct answer is C (Room temperature is below its critical temperature of 36.5 degrees Celsius): The table shown lists nitrous oxide as having a critical temperature of 36.5 degrees Celsius, which is above typical UK room temperature of around 20 degrees Celsius. Because room temperature is below this critical temperature, nitrous oxide can be liquefied by the pressure applied during cylinder filling, so the cylinder contains liquid in equilibrium with vapour above it. This contrasts with oxygen, whose critical temperature of minus 118 degrees Celsius is far below room temperature, meaning it can only exist as compressed gas under normal storage conditions.
  12. The correct answer is E (Below minus five point five degrees Celsius, liquid nitrous oxide separates and lamination can occur): Entonox is a premixed cylinder of 50% oxygen and 50% nitrous oxide that behaves as a single gas above its pseudocritical temperature of minus 5.5 degrees Celsius at a pressure of 117 bar, but below this temperature the nitrous oxide component can condense out and separate from the oxygen. This separation, known as lamination, risks delivering a hypoxic gas mixture with a disproportionately high nitrous oxide concentration from the bottom of the cylinder. This is why Entonox cylinders should be stored horizontally and warmed before use if there is any suspicion they have been kept in a cold environment.
  13. The correct answer is A (Oxygen's critical temperature of minus 118 degrees Celsius is far below room temperature): Oxygen has a critical temperature of approximately minus 118 degrees Celsius, meaning that above this temperature no amount of applied pressure can liquefy it. Since UK room and cylinder storage temperatures are always far above this critical temperature, oxygen cylinders can only ever contain compressed gas, never liquid. Nitrous oxide, in contrast, has a critical temperature of 36.5 degrees Celsius, which is above typical storage temperatures, allowing it to be stored as a liquid under pressure.
  14. The correct answer is B (Approximately 100 mmHg, using a conversion factor of 7.5): One kPa is approximately equal to 7.5 mmHg, so a pressure of 13.3 kPa converts to approximately 13.3 multiplied by 7.5, which equals approximately 100 mmHg. This conversion factor is commonly used in anaesthetic practice to move between the SI unit kPa, used on modern blood gas analysers, and the traditional mmHg unit still used in some older texts and monitors. Recognising this conversion is important for interpreting arterial blood gas results consistently across different reporting formats.
  15. The correct answer is E (Approximately 1.5 kPa, using a conversion factor of roughly ten): One kPa is approximately equal to 10.2 cmH2O, so a pressure of 15 cmH2O converts to approximately 15 divided by 10.2, which equals approximately 1.5 kPa. Central venous pressure is traditionally measured and reported in cmH2O using a fluid-filled manometer, whereas many modern electronic transducers display pressure in kPa or mmHg, so this conversion is needed to compare readings from different devices. Recognising the approximate factor of ten allows a rapid mental estimate at the bedside.
  16. The correct answer is C (Approximately 13,700 kPa, since one bar equals one hundred kPa): One bar is defined as exactly 100 kPa, so a cylinder pressure of 137 bar converts to 137 multiplied by 100, which equals 13,700 kPa. Cylinder pressure gauges in the UK conventionally display readings in bar, while physics calculations using the gas laws typically require pressure expressed in kPa or pascals, so this conversion is frequently needed. Being confident with this factor of one hundred avoids errors of several orders of magnitude in exam calculations.
  17. The correct answer is D (Approximately 760 mmHg, as shown directly in the table shown): The table shown states directly that one standard atmosphere equals approximately 760 mmHg, alongside the equivalent values of 101.3 kPa, 1.013 bar and 1033 cmH2O. These figures are all different expressions of the same reference pressure, atmospheric pressure at sea level, and being able to move between them is essential for interpreting pressure readings from different types of equipment used throughout anaesthetic practice. The mmHg unit remains in common clinical use for blood pressure measurement despite kPa being the preferred SI unit for blood gas results.
  18. The correct answer is B (Velocity is greatest at the centre, forming a parabolic profile): Under laminar flow conditions, gas moves in smooth, parallel layers or streamlines, with the layer in contact with the tube wall experiencing the greatest frictional resistance and moving most slowly, while the central layer moves fastest with least resistance. This produces a characteristic parabolic velocity profile across the tube diameter, with velocity decreasing progressively from the centre towards the wall. This orderly pattern is described by the Hagen-Poiseuille equation and occurs at flow rates below the critical Reynolds number for a given tube and gas.
  19. The correct answer is D (Flow becomes proportional to the square root of the pressure gradient): Under turbulent flow conditions, the smooth streamlines of laminar flow break down into disorganised eddies and cross-currents, and the relationship between flow rate and driving pressure changes from a simple direct proportionality to one where flow is proportional to the square root of the pressure gradient. This means considerably more pressure is required to achieve a given increase in flow once turbulence develops, compared with laminar flow through the same segment. This change in relationship is clinically important because narrowed or irregular airways promote turbulence, increasing the work of breathing disproportionately.
  20. The correct answer is A (Reynolds number equals density times velocity times diameter divided by viscosity): The Reynolds number is a dimensionless value calculated as density multiplied by velocity multiplied by diameter, divided by viscosity, and it predicts whether flow through a tube will be laminar or turbulent. A low Reynolds number, favoured by low density, low velocity, small diameter and high viscosity, predicts laminar flow, while a high Reynolds number predicts turbulent flow. This equation explains why gases with a high density but low viscosity, such as nitrous oxide compared with helium, are more prone to turbulent flow at a given velocity and diameter.
  21. The correct answer is E (Flow becomes turbulent above a Reynolds number of approximately 2000): A Reynolds number below approximately 2000 predicts stable laminar flow, while values above this threshold predict a transition towards disorganised turbulent flow, with an intermediate transitional zone often described around this critical value. This critical value is a useful approximation taught for exam purposes, although the exact transition point can vary somewhat depending on tube geometry and surface roughness. Recognising this threshold helps explain why increasing flow rate, and therefore velocity, through a fixed airway diameter can suddenly convert smooth laminar flow into noisy turbulent flow.
  22. The correct answer is C (The laminar line is straight, while the turbulent line flattens with pressure): The chart shown demonstrates that the laminar flow series rises as a straight line, reflecting the direct proportional relationship between flow and pressure gradient described by the Hagen-Poiseuille equation. The turbulent flow series instead curves and flattens at higher pressures, reflecting the square root relationship between flow and pressure gradient that characterises turbulent flow. This visual distinction explains why increasingly large increases in driving pressure are needed to achieve further increases in flow once a system becomes turbulent.
  23. The correct answer is A (Flow rises with the fourth power of radius, producing a steep curve): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation states that laminar flow rate is proportional to the pressure gradient and to the fourth power of the tube radius, and inversely proportional to the length of the tube and the viscosity of the fluid. The chart shown illustrates this fourth power relationship, with flow rising extremely steeply as radius increases, since even a small increase in radius produces a very large increase in flow. This relationship explains the clinical importance of cannula and airway diameter, since halving the radius reduces flow to only one sixteenth of its original value for the same pressure gradient.
  24. The correct answer is C (Flow rate is proportional to the fourth power of the internal radius): The Hagen-Poiseuille equation states that laminar flow rate through a tube is proportional to the fourth power of its internal radius, meaning small increases in bore diameter produce disproportionately large increases in achievable flow. A 14-gauge cannula has a substantially greater internal radius than a 20-gauge cannula, so despite only a modest difference in gauge number, the larger cannula can deliver several times the flow rate for the same driving pressure. This relationship is the physiological basis for choosing the widest practical cannula for rapid fluid resuscitation in haemorrhage.
  25. The correct answer is B (High gas velocity, high density, low viscosity and branching points): The Reynolds number, calculated as density multiplied by velocity multiplied by diameter divided by viscosity, rises and therefore favours turbulent flow when gas velocity and density are high and viscosity is low. Branching points, sudden changes in diameter, and irregular or roughened surfaces also disrupt the smooth streamlines of laminar flow and promote turbulence independently of the Reynolds number itself. This combination explains why turbulence is common at the larynx and at points of airway narrowing, where velocity increases and geometry becomes irregular.
  26. The correct answer is D (Pressure falls as volume rises, producing a hyperbolic curve, with PV constant): The chart shown illustrates Boyle's law, which states that at constant temperature the product of pressure and volume for a fixed mass of gas remains constant, so PV equals a constant value throughout the curve. This produces the characteristic hyperbolic isotherm shown, where pressure falls steeply at small volumes and more gradually as volume increases further. This graphical relationship underlies the behaviour of gas cylinders and syringes used for practical demonstrations of gas physics in anaesthetic teaching.
  27. The correct answer is E (The bobbin rises until the pressure drop across the widening gap balances its weight): A rotameter is a constant-pressure, variable-orifice flowmeter in which the bobbin floats within a tapered tube whose cross-sectional area increases with height. At any given flow rate the bobbin settles at the height where the pressure drop across the annular gap between bobbin and tube wall exactly balances the downward force of gravity on the bobbin, so a higher flow rate requires a wider gap and therefore a higher bobbin position. The tube is calibrated so that bobbin height can be read directly as flow rate for a specific gas.
  28. The correct answer is D (The narrow annular gap at the bottom favours laminar flow, the wider gap at the top favours turbulent flow): Near the bottom of the tapered rotameter tube the annular gap around the bobbin is narrow, so gas flow through this gap behaves in a laminar fashion and is therefore sensitive to gas viscosity rather than density. Higher up the tube the gap becomes wider, gas velocity through it is relatively higher relative to the gap dimensions, and flow becomes turbulent, making the reading more dependent on gas density than viscosity. This dual behaviour means each rotameter tube must be individually calibrated for the specific gas it is intended to measure.
  29. The correct answer is B (Oxygen flowmeter is positioned downstream, so gases combine after the oxygen control): By convention, the oxygen flowmeter is positioned downstream of the nitrous oxide flowmeter on the back bar, so that fresh gases combine only after passing the oxygen control. This arrangement ensures that if a leak develops upstream within the flowmeter block, the leak preferentially affects the nitrous oxide pathway rather than the oxygen pathway, reducing the risk of delivering a hypoxic mixture to the patient. This is one of several deliberate safety features built into the fresh gas delivery pathway of the anaesthetic machine.
  30. The correct answer is C (The ratio of gas flow bypassing the chamber to flow through the chamber): The splitting ratio of a plenum vaporiser describes how incoming fresh gas flow is divided between the pathway that passes through the vaporising chamber, becoming fully saturated with agent vapour, and the pathway that bypasses the chamber entirely, remaining free of agent. Adjusting the concentration dial alters this splitting ratio, changing the proportion of gas directed through each pathway, which in turn alters the final concentration of agent delivered once the two streams recombine. This mechanism allows accurate low percentage outputs to be achieved even though the fully saturated vapour concentration within the chamber itself is much higher.
  31. The correct answer is A (Approximately 49 parts of gas bypass the chamber for every 1 part through it): To achieve an output concentration of 2% from a chamber capable of producing 100% saturated vapour, only a small fraction of total fresh gas flow needs to pass through the chamber, with the remainder bypassing it and diluting the saturated vapour on recombination. Approximating the required split gives a ratio of roughly 1 part through the chamber to 49 parts bypassing it, so that the small saturated fraction is diluted down to approximately 2% of the total flow. This illustrates why low dial settings on modern volatile agent vaporisers correspond to a very large bypass fraction relative to chamber flow.
  32. The correct answer is E (A temperature-sensitive bimetallic strip alters the splitting ratio as temperature falls): As liquid agent evaporates within the vaporising chamber it absorbs latent heat, cooling the remaining liquid and reducing its saturated vapour pressure, which would otherwise cause the delivered concentration to fall progressively during use. Modern plenum vaporisers compensate for this using a temperature-sensitive bimetallic strip valve that automatically increases the proportion of gas directed through the chamber as temperature falls, maintaining a stable splitting ratio and therefore a stable output concentration. This automatic temperature compensation allows the vaporiser to deliver a consistent concentration across a range of ambient and operating temperatures without manual adjustment.
  33. The correct answer is C (A higher saturated vapour pressure at a given temperature indicates a lower boiling point): Saturated vapour pressure and boiling point are inversely related properties of a volatile liquid, since a liquid boils at the temperature at which its saturated vapour pressure equals atmospheric pressure. A liquid with a high saturated vapour pressure at room temperature needs only a small further rise in temperature to reach atmospheric pressure and therefore has a correspondingly low boiling point, while a liquid with low saturated vapour pressure at the same temperature requires much greater heating to boil. This relationship explains why more volatile anaesthetic agents, with higher vapour pressures, tend to have lower boiling points than less volatile agents.
  34. The correct answer is A (Blue body with a blue shoulder, distinguishing it from Entonox): The table shown lists nitrous oxide as having a blue body with a matching blue shoulder, distinguishing a pure nitrous oxide cylinder from an Entonox cylinder, which shares the same blue body but has a blue and white quartered shoulder to indicate the premixed oxygen and nitrous oxide contents. Recognising these traditional colour codes remains part of primary examination knowledge, even though current UK practice has moved towards additional labelling and standardised pin-index or valve fittings to reduce reliance on colour alone. Confusing similarly coloured cylinders has historically contributed to serious wrong-gas administration incidents.
  35. The correct answer is D (Approximately 100 milliamps applied through intact skin causes fibrillation): Macroshock describes an electric shock delivered across a relatively large surface area of intact skin, such as through contact with faulty equipment, and typically requires a current of approximately 100 milliamps passing through the chest to induce ventricular fibrillation. This threshold is considerably higher than the threshold for microshock because intact skin provides substantial electrical resistance, spreading the current over a large tissue volume before it reaches the heart. Standard mains electrical safety measures, such as earthing and residual current devices, are designed to interrupt currents well below this macroshock threshold.
  36. The correct answer is B (As little as 100 microamps applied directly to the myocardium can cause fibrillation): Microshock describes the situation where an electrically conductive pathway, such as a pacing wire or a fluid-filled central venous catheter, provides a direct low-resistance route for current into or onto the heart, bypassing the protective resistance of intact skin. Under these conditions a current as small as approximately 100 microamps, one thousand times smaller than the macroshock threshold, can induce ventricular fibrillation because the entire current is concentrated through a very small area of myocardium. This is why particular care is taken with earthing and equipotential bonding in any patient with an intracardiac or intravascular conductive device in place.
  37. The correct answer is E (Class I relies on an earth connection, while Class II uses double insulation): Class I equipment relies on a low-resistance earth connection so that in the event of an internal fault, fault current is safely conducted to earth rather than through anyone touching the casing, and this pathway typically trips a circuit protection device. Class II equipment instead achieves equivalent protection through double or reinforced insulation between live internal components and any accessible casing, meaning no earth connection is required for basic safety. Both classifications are distinct from the Type B, BF and CF classification, which describes the degree of isolation and permitted leakage current of the applied part that contacts the patient.
  38. The correct answer is D (Type CF, the floating applied part classification safest for direct cardiac contact): Type CF, meaning cardiac floating, is the classification reserved for applied parts intended for direct contact with the heart, such as intracardiac catheters, pulmonary artery catheters and cardiac bypass equipment, and it permits the lowest leakage current of the three classifications. Type B and Type BF applied parts are progressively less isolated and carry higher permitted leakage currents, making them unsuitable for a direct low-resistance pathway to the myocardium. Choosing equipment with the correct body-protection classification for its intended use is an important safeguard against microshock in vulnerable patients.
  39. The correct answer is A (Approximately 10 microamps under normal conditions, the lowest of the three types): Type CF equipment, intended for direct cardiac contact, is subject to the strictest leakage current limits of the three body-protection classifications, typically around 10 microamps under normal operating conditions, considerably lower than the limits permitted for Type B or Type BF equipment. This stringent limit reflects the extreme sensitivity of the myocardium to microshock, where currents far too small to be felt through intact skin can nonetheless induce ventricular fibrillation if applied directly to cardiac tissue. Manufacturers must demonstrate compliance with these limits under both normal and single-fault conditions before equipment can be classified as Type CF.
  40. The correct answer is C (Current density is much higher at the small active electrode than at the large plate): In monopolar diathermy the same current passes through both the small active electrode tip and the large return electrode plate, but current density, and therefore local tissue heating, depends on the surface area through which the current passes. The small surface area of the active electrode concentrates the current into a tiny volume of tissue, generating sufficient heat for cutting or coagulation, while the much larger surface area of the return plate spreads the same current over a wide area, keeping current density and heating there low. If the return plate is poorly applied or its surface area is effectively reduced, current density can rise dangerously at that site, causing an alternate site burn.
  41. The correct answer is B (High frequency avoids neuromuscular and cardiac stimulation, and bipolar keeps current confined between the forceps tips): Diathermy operates at frequencies of several hundred kilohertz because currents at this frequency pass through excitable tissue too quickly to trigger action potentials in nerve and muscle, including cardiac muscle, avoiding the tetanic stimulation and arrhythmia risk that would occur at fifty hertz mains frequency. Bipolar diathermy further improves safety in a patient with a pacemaker because current passes only between the two forceps tips through a small volume of tissue, rather than travelling through the whole body to a distant return electrode as in monopolar diathermy. This combination of high frequency and a confined current pathway substantially reduces the risk of pacemaker interference or inappropriate firing during surgery.
  42. The correct answer is A (The pin-index system, using a unique pin arrangement for each gas): the pin-index safety system places two pins on the yoke at gas-specific positions that must match matching holes drilled into the cylinder valve block, so a nitrous oxide cylinder physically cannot be seated on an oxygen yoke. The Bodok seal only provides an airtight washer once the correct cylinder is already seated and does not itself prevent wrong attachment. Colour coding is a visual check relied upon by staff, not a mechanical interlock, so it can be defeated by human error alone. The screw thread on the regulator is unrelated to the yoke connection of a pin-indexed cylinder.
  43. The correct answer is B (Blue body with a white and blue quartered shoulder): entonox cylinders are coded with a blue cylinder body and a shoulder quartered in white and blue segments, reflecting that the mixture combines oxygen and nitrous oxide. A plain white body and shoulder would indicate oxygen alone, not a mixture. A black body is not used for entonox under the current UK standard. Correct identification at the pre-use check prevents an entonox cylinder being mistaken for a pure gas cylinder during an emergency.
  44. The correct answer is C (The oxygen failure alarm not activating on testing): the oxygen failure warning device is a mandatory safety feature that must audibly alarm and, on most machines, cut off nitrous oxide flow if oxygen supply pressure falls, so a failure to activate on testing means the machine cannot be relied upon to protect the patient from a hypoxic mixture if the oxygen supply fails intraoperatively. The oxygen and nitrous oxide pipeline pressures are within the expected working range of around 400 kPa, though the medical air reading of 290 kPa is also below this expected range and would itself normally warrant checking; however, it is less immediately dangerous than a non-functioning oxygen failure alarm, since medical air is not essential to prevent a hypoxic mixture in the way oxygen supply is. The vacuum reading of minus 55 kPa is an acceptable negative pressure for effective suction. This finding must be escalated and resolved before proceeding, unlike the other readings which are reassuring.
  45. The correct answer is D (The Hagen-Poiseuille equation, relating flow to viscosity and radius): at low flows the annular gap between the bobbin and the narrow part of the tapered tube acts like a long narrow tube, so flow becomes laminar and is governed by the Hagen-Poiseuille relationship, making flow dependent on gas viscosity. At higher flows, where the gap widens and behaves as an orifice, flow instead depends on gas density, which is why rotameters must be individually calibrated for each gas. The Bernoulli principle and Coanda effect describe different aspects of fluid dynamics not directly responsible for this specific low-flow behaviour. Graham's law concerns diffusion, not flow through a tube.
  46. The correct answer is B (Phase II, the expiratory upstroke as alveolar gas mixes with dead space gas): phase II is the rapid rising portion of the capnogram immediately after the flat inspiratory baseline, occurring as anatomical dead space gas is progressively replaced by CO2-rich alveolar gas during early expiration. Phase I is the preceding flat baseline representing dead space gas with negligible CO2. Phase III is the subsequent near-horizontal alveolar plateau, not the upstroke itself. The beta angle describes the transition between phase II and phase III rather than the upstroke on its own.
  47. The correct answer is B (Pulse oximetry, capnography, ECG and non-invasive blood pressure): the AAGBI minimum monitoring standard mandates continuous pulse oximetry, capnography, ECG and regular non-invasive blood pressure for every general and regional anaesthetic, with no exceptions regardless of case duration or apparent simplicity. Invasive arterial pressure, central venous pressure and neuromuscular monitoring are used when clinically indicated but are not part of the universal minimum standard. Temperature monitoring is required but only becomes mandatory beyond a certain procedure duration, unlike the parameters in option B which apply from the very start of every case.
  48. The correct answer is D (The Beer-Lambert law, relating light absorption to concentration and path length): pulse oximetry applies the Beer-Lambert law by comparing the ratio of light absorbed at red and infrared wavelengths during the pulsatile arterial component, since oxyhaemoglobin and deoxyhaemoglobin absorb these wavelengths differently in proportion to their concentration. Fick's principle relates to cardiac output measurement using oxygen consumption, not light absorption. Dalton's law concerns partial pressures within a gas mixture, and Graham's law and Poiseuille's law describe diffusion and flow respectively, none of which underlie the optical method used by a pulse oximeter.
  49. The correct answer is A (Oesophageal intubation, with only trace gastric CO2 detected): a persistently low, poorly-formed trace with only a small amount of CO2 detected, rather than a rising and plateauing waveform, is characteristic of oesophageal intubation, where any trace CO2 reflects swallowed exhaled gas or carbonated stomach contents rather than true alveolar ventilation. Bronchospasm produces a sloped shark-fin expiratory upstroke with a normal or elevated etCO2, not this flattened low pattern. Rebreathing would raise the inspiratory baseline rather than abolish the expiratory plateau. Correct tracheal placement would show a normal rising trace reaching a clear alveolar plateau, which is absent here.
  50.
