The correct answer is E (During controlled ventilation, alveolar gas is vented before fresh gas is lost): the Mapleson A system is efficient during spontaneous ventilation because expiratory flow preferentially vents alveolar gas through the expiratory valve while fresh gas accumulates for the next breath. During controlled ventilation, positive pressure reverses this relationship so that fresh gas is preferentially vented early in expiration while alveolar gas, containing carbon dioxide, is retained, requiring much higher fresh gas flows to prevent rebreathing. The expiratory valve does not close permanently, and neither soda lime nor reservoir bag capacity are relevant to a Mapleson system, which contains no absorbent.
  51. The correct answer is C (A fall in pH as calcium carbonate accumulates and hydroxide is consumed): as soda lime absorbs carbon dioxide, hydroxide is progressively converted to carbonate and water, lowering the pH of the granules, which triggers the pH-sensitive indicator dye such as ethyl violet to change colour, signalling exhaustion. Compound A formation is a separate hazard linked to desiccated, not exhausted, absorbent reacting with sevoflurane. Carbon monoxide and formaldehyde production are also linked to desiccation of the absorbent rather than normal exhaustion. Barium sulfate is not a product of the carbon dioxide neutralisation reaction.
  52. The correct answer is A (20 to 30 cmH2O): maintaining cuff pressure within approximately 20 to 30 cmH2O provides an adequate seal against aspiration and leak while remaining below the tracheal mucosal capillary perfusion pressure, minimising the risk of ischaemic mucosal injury from prolonged compression. A pressure of 5 to 10 cmH2O would risk an inadequate seal and leak around the cuff. Pressures of 40 cmH2O or above exceed capillary perfusion pressure and are associated with mucosal ischaemia, tracheal stenosis and, in severe or prolonged cases, tracheomalacia. Regular manometer checks are recommended, particularly during long procedures or when nitrous oxide is used, since cuff pressure can rise over time.
  53. The correct answer is D (Traction on the hyoepiglottic ligament via the tongue base and vallecula): the Macintosh blade tip sits in the vallecula, and lifting the handle along its axis pulls the tongue base forward, placing tension on the hyoepiglottic ligament that indirectly elevates the epiglottis away from the glottic opening. This indirect mechanism distinguishes the Macintosh from a straight blade such as a Miller, which is designed to directly lift the epiglottis itself. There is no suction effect generated by the blade shape, and epiglottic elevation is a mechanical rather than a reflex neuromuscular event. Gravity alone does not elevate the epiglottis during laryngoscopy.
  54. The correct answer is E (A rigid or preformed stylet is usually needed to match the tube to the curve): because a hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade produces an indirect, highly curved line of sight to the glottis, a standard malleable stylet or straight tube trajectory is often insufficient, and a rigid or preformed stylet shaped to match the blade's curvature is typically required to successfully direct the tube tip through the cords despite good visualisation. The image on most videolaryngoscope monitors is displayed the correct way round, not inverted. Battery life and oxygen saturation monitoring are unrelated to blade angulation, and videolaryngoscopy can still be used across a range of Mallampati scores.
  55. The correct answer is B (The absent capnography waveform for the last two breaths): loss of the capnography waveform during an ongoing anaesthetic is a critical finding that may indicate circuit disconnection, tube displacement, apnoea or complete airway obstruction, and requires immediate assessment of the airway and breathing circuit above all other displayed parameters. The heart rate and blood pressure, while at the lower end of normal, are not immediately life-threatening in isolation. An inspired oxygen concentration of 50 percent is an appropriate intraoperative setting, and the temperature reading is within a normal range. An absent capnography trace always takes priority as a marker of a potentially unrecognised airway or ventilation emergency.
  56. The correct answer is C (A separate drain tube allowing passage of a gastric tube): second-generation supraglottic airways add a channel running alongside the airway tube that opens at the oesophageal inlet, allowing passage of a gastric tube to decompress the stomach and providing a route for regurgitated fluid to bypass the airway, reducing aspiration risk. A larger cuff volume alone does not address gastric content bypassing the airway. An integrated fibreoptic channel and capnography sampling line are not the defining feature distinguishing generations of supraglottic airway. Reinforced tubing addresses kinking resistance, not aspiration risk.
  57. The correct answer is A (Approximately 4 bar, 400 kPa): the pressure-reducing valve on an anaesthetic machine lowers the variable, high cylinder pressure down to a constant working pressure of approximately 4 bar, or 400 kPa, matching the pipeline supply pressure so that flowmeters can be calibrated and operate consistently regardless of whether gas is drawn from a cylinder or the hospital pipeline. A working pressure of 1 bar would be too low to drive adequate flow through the flowmeter block. Pressures of 20 bar, 60 bar or the unregulated cylinder pressure of 137 bar would all be far too high and unsafe for the flowmeter and downstream components.
  58. The correct answer is D (The dicrotic notch, the small downward deflection during diastolic decay): the dicrotic notch appears as a small, sharp downward deflection on the descending limb of the arterial waveform and corresponds to the brief reversal of flow as the aortic valve closes at the end of systole. The initial steep upstroke reflects left ventricular ejection, and the peak represents maximum systolic pressure, neither of which relate to valve closure. The gradual decline after systole reflects diastolic runoff of blood into the peripheral circulation. The lowest point of the trace is the end-diastolic pressure immediately before the next systolic upstroke.
  59. The correct answer is B (Right atrial contraction against a closed tricuspid valve): the a wave of the CVP trace results from right atrial contraction occurring just before ventricular systole, while the tricuspid valve remains closed, producing a small positive pressure rise that appears immediately before the QRS complex on the ECG. Ventricular systole bulging the tricuspid valve upward produces the c wave, which follows the a wave rather than preceding the QRS in this way. Rapid ventricular filling after tricuspid valve opening produces the y descent, not the a wave. Pulmonary valve closure and passive atrial filling are not represented by the a wave.
  60. The correct answer is E (Barotrauma to the machine from occlusion of the outlet or hose): the back-bar pressure relief valve vents excess pressure if the common gas outlet or the attached breathing system hose becomes occluded, preventing dangerous pressure build-up that could damage the vaporiser, flowmeter block or other components of the machine. It does not prevent hypoxic mixtures, which are instead addressed by oxygen failure alarms and mechanical oxygen and nitrous oxide flow linkage. It has no role in preventing vaporiser contamination, controlling relative nitrous oxide flow, or compensating for a pin-index failure, which are separate safety features entirely.
  61. The correct answer is C (Insufficient pulsatile arterial blood flow to generate a plethysmographic signal): pulse oximetry depends on detecting a pulsatile arterial waveform to distinguish arterial blood absorption from that of venous blood, tissue and skin, so severe peripheral vasoconstriction and hypothermia reduce peripheral perfusion enough that no reliable pulsatile signal can be generated, regardless of true arterial oxygenation. Carboxyhaemoglobin and methaemoglobin cause specific falsely altered readings rather than complete signal failure. Ambient light interference and nail varnish can degrade accuracy but typically still allow a reading to be displayed, unlike the complete signal loss described here.
  62. The correct answer is B (Approximately 70 to 100 mL/kg/min, roughly equal to minute ventilation): with controlled ventilation, a Mapleson D system, and its coaxial Bain variant, requires fresh gas flow approximately equal to the patient's minute ventilation, typically around 70 to 100 mL/kg/min, to prevent significant rebreathing of carbon dioxide, since the fresh gas flow arrangement of the D configuration is comparatively efficient during positive pressure ventilation. A flow of only half minute ventilation would risk rebreathing, while twice minute ventilation is unnecessarily wasteful. A fixed flow of 2 litres per minute ignores patient size, and no fresh gas flow at all would rapidly cause severe hypercapnia.
  63. The correct answer is C (Size 4): a size 4 laryngeal mask airway is the standard first choice for an average-sized adult female weighing approximately 50 to 70 kilograms, providing an appropriately sized cuff to seal the hypopharynx without excessive bulk. Sizes 1, 1.5 and 2 are intended for infants and small children, well below adult airway dimensions. A size 6 is not a standard laryngeal mask size, since the adult range typically runs from size 3 to size 5, with size 5 reserved for larger adult males.
  64. The correct answer is A (White body and white shoulder): the current UK medical gas cylinder colour standard designates a white body and white shoulder for medical oxygen, having moved away from the older black body with white shoulder convention to harmonise with the European pin-index colour standard. A blue body and blue shoulder is used for nitrous oxide. Grey is not a standard UK medical oxygen colour. Recognising the current standard is essential, since the earlier black-bodied oxygen cylinders may still occasionally be encountered and could otherwise cause confusion.
  65. The correct answer is D (Rebreathing of exhaled gas containing carbon dioxide): if the expiratory unidirectional valve sticks partially open, exhaled gas containing carbon dioxide can flow backward into the inspiratory limb during the next breath, allowing the patient to rebreathe carbon dioxide despite an intact soda lime canister, since the gas may bypass adequate contact with the absorbent. Oxygen delivery itself is not directly interrupted by valve incompetence. Soda lime exhaustion is a separate, gradual process unrelated to valve function. Excessive positive pressure and loss of volatile agent delivery are not the expected direct consequences of this specific valve fault.
  66. The correct answer is E (Increase the respiratory rate to correct the rising etCO2): pneumoperitoneum from insufflated carbon dioxide during laparoscopic surgery commonly causes a progressive rise in etCO2 as gas is absorbed across the peritoneum, and increasing the respiratory rate increases minute ventilation to restore normocapnia while avoiding a further rise in tidal volume that could worsen peak airway pressure. Reducing tidal volume further would not address the rising etCO2 and could worsen atelectasis. Increasing or reducing PEEP addresses oxygenation and lung compliance rather than the CO2 trend. Switching modes alone does not correct rising etCO2 without also adjusting minute ventilation.
  67. The correct answer is C (To provide a closer estimate of the alveolar and hence brain partial pressure): end-expiratory anaesthetic agent concentration approximates the alveolar partial pressure, which in turn correlates more closely with the partial pressure achieved in the brain than the inspired concentration alone, making it a more clinically useful guide to anaesthetic depth. Inspired concentration only reflects what is being delivered, not what the patient has actually equilibrated to. Vaporiser volume, fresh gas hose leaks and oxygen failure alarms are monitored by entirely separate mechanisms. Expired agent monitoring complements rather than replaces capnography, since the two measure different gases for different purposes.
  68. The correct answer is E (Uneven emptying of alveoli with differing time constants, as in bronchospasm): bronchospasm causes airway narrowing that produces uneven, delayed emptying of alveoli with varying time constants, so the normally near-horizontal alveolar plateau instead rises progressively throughout expiration, producing the characteristic shark-fin shaped waveform. Complete tube obstruction or disconnection would abolish the trace entirely rather than distort its shape. A cuff leak typically produces a low, poorly-formed waveform rather than this specific sloped pattern. Right main bronchus intubation may reduce tidal volume and raise airway pressures but does not specifically produce this shark-fin morphology.
  69. The correct answer is A (Positions 2 and 5): the pin-index system assigns medical oxygen the pin positions 2 and 5, a combination unique to oxygen among the standard medical gases used on an anaesthetic machine, ensuring an oxygen cylinder can only be seated correctly on an oxygen-specific yoke. Other gases such as nitrous oxide and medical air are assigned different pin position combinations under the same system. This mechanical safeguard operates independently of, and in addition to, the visual colour coding of the cylinder body and shoulder. Confusing pin positions between gases would defeat the purpose of this safety system entirely.
  70. The correct answer is B (Rebreathing of exhaled carbon dioxide due to inadequate fresh gas flow): a baseline that fails to return to zero during inspiration indicates that carbon dioxide-containing gas is being reinhaled, most commonly because fresh gas flow through the Mapleson circuit is too low to adequately flush exhaled gas before the next breath. Normal dead space ventilation would show the baseline returning fully to zero between breaths. Complete disconnection would abolish the entire waveform rather than elevate the baseline. Oesophageal sampling and malignant hyperthermia would not specifically produce this persistently elevated baseline pattern without also altering the overall trace shape.
  71. The correct answer is D (Nearest to the common gas outlet, on the right of the bank): positioning the oxygen flowmeter downstream of the others, nearest the common gas outlet, means that if an upstream flowmeter tube such as nitrous oxide develops a leak or crack, any resulting loss of gas occurs before oxygen is added, reducing the risk of a hypoxic mixture reaching the patient. If oxygen were positioned furthest away or in the middle, a downstream leak in another gas's tube could allow oxygen to escape before mixing, increasing hypoxic risk. Gas position within the bank does have a real safety implication and is not arbitrary.
  72. The correct answer is D (Absence of the a wave, since it depends on atrial contraction): because the a wave of the CVP trace is generated specifically by coordinated right atrial contraction against a closed tricuspid valve, atrial fibrillation abolishes organised atrial contraction and therefore the a wave disappears from the trace, sometimes replaced by irregular fibrillatory waves. The c wave, x descent, y descent and v wave arise from ventricular and valve-related events that persist independently of atrial contraction and are not expected to be selectively exaggerated by atrial fibrillation. Recognising the loss of the a wave can support the diagnosis of an arrhythmia from the waveform alone.
  73. The correct answer is A (A glottic view can be obtained without aligning the oral, pharyngeal and laryngeal axes): videolaryngoscopy uses a camera near the blade tip to capture an indirect view of the glottis, meaning a usable image can often be obtained even when the head and neck cannot be positioned to align the three airway axes required for direct line-of-sight laryngoscopy, which is particularly valuable in patients with a fixed cervical spine. It does not eliminate the need for adequate mouth opening to pass the blade and tube. Visual confirmation of tube placement remains an important part of the technique rather than being replaced by feel. It also does not guarantee a grade 1 view in every patient, since other factors can still limit visualisation.
  74. The correct answer is B (Compound A, a vinyl ether formed by reaction with desiccated soda lime): sevoflurane reacts with strongly alkaline, desiccated soda lime to form compound A, a vinyl ether that has been associated with nephrotoxicity in animal studies, which is why prolonged low-flow anaesthesia with desiccated absorbent is generally avoided with sevoflurane. Carbon monoxide production from desiccated absorbent is a separate hazard more classically associated with older absorbents and other volatile agents. Formaldehyde and hydrogen fluoride are not the principal sevoflurane degradation product of concern in this context. Nitrogen dioxide formation is not a recognised soda lime interaction with sevoflurane.
  75. The correct answer is C (Methaemoglobinaemia, which absorbs light equally at both measured wavelengths): dapsone is a recognised cause of methaemoglobinaemia, and because methaemoglobin absorbs red and infrared light almost equally, the pulse oximeter's calculated ratio is driven toward approximately 85 percent regardless of the true oxygen saturation, explaining the fixed reading despite normal arterial oxygen tension. Carboxyhaemoglobin instead causes a falsely high reading, not a fixed value near 85 percent. Peripheral vasoconstriction typically causes signal loss rather than a stable erroneous reading. Fetal haemoglobin and ambient light do not produce this characteristic fixed saturation pattern.
  76. The correct answer is E (Its low apparatus dead space and resistance suit small tidal volumes): the Jackson-Rees modification of Ayre's T-piece has minimal apparatus dead space and very low resistance to flow, with no valves for a small child to overcome, making it well suited to the small tidal volumes and higher respiratory rates typical of paediatric patients. It contains no unidirectional valve and relies instead on adequate fresh gas flow to prevent rebreathing, unlike systems that depend on valves. It does not incorporate soda lime, and fresh gas flow requirements scale with the child's size rather than being fixed. The open-ended reservoir bag arrangement, not a closed bag with no expiratory limb, is characteristic of this system.
  77. The correct answer is B (Excessive natural frequency relative to the physiological signal, with low damping): an underdamped arterial line system, often from stiff, short, non-compliant tubing, produces overshoot and ringing artefact that falsely elevates the displayed systolic pressure while the mean pressure usually remains accurate, reflecting a mismatch between the system's natural frequency and the arterial waveform's harmonics. An air bubble or kinked tubing typically produces the opposite effect, an overdamped trace with a blunted, rounded waveform and falsely low systolic readings. A partially occluded cannula would similarly dampen the signal rather than exaggerate it. Incorrect zeroing shifts the whole trace rather than altering its waveform shape.
  78. The correct answer is D (Approximately 340 litres, half of the full capacity): because oxygen remains a true gas within the cylinder at room temperature, its pressure falls in direct proportion to the remaining volume of gas as it is used, consistent with Boyle's law, so a gauge reading of half the full pressure, 68.5 bar compared with 137 bar, corresponds to approximately half the full contents, around 340 litres. This proportional relationship distinguishes oxygen cylinders from cylinders containing liquefied gases such as nitrous oxide, where gauge pressure remains constant until the liquid phase is exhausted. The other stated volumes do not correctly reflect this proportional relationship.
  79. The correct answer is C (Pharyngolaryngeal morbidity, including sore throat and nerve palsy): overinflating a laryngeal mask cuff beyond the recommended intracuff pressure increases mucosal pressure against the pharyngeal wall, which is associated with a higher incidence of postoperative sore throat, dysphagia and, in more severe cases, transient nerve palsies affecting structures such as the lingual or hypoglossal nerve. Overinflation does not prevent capnography sampling or gastric tube passage where a drain channel exists, and it does not typically reduce fresh gas flow through the airway tube. It also does not reliably cause immediate device displacement, making sore throat and nerve injury the recognised principal complication.
  80. The correct answer is A (30 minutes): the AAGBI recommends establishing core temperature monitoring for any anaesthetic expected to last longer than approximately 30 minutes, or sooner if a significant temperature change is anticipated, reflecting how quickly inadvertent perioperative hypothermia can develop once anaesthesia and surgical exposure begin. Waiting until 4 or 6 hours would allow substantial unmonitored heat loss to occur in most routine procedures. Temperature monitoring is not restricted to paediatric patients and applies across all age groups where the duration threshold is met. A threshold as short as 5 minutes would be impractical and is not the recommended standard.
  81. The correct answer is E (Carbon dioxide is chemically absorbed by soda lime, permitting rebreathing of other exhaled gases): the circle system's soda lime canister chemically removes carbon dioxide from exhaled gas, allowing the remaining exhaled gases, including unused oxygen and volatile agent, to be safely rebreathed rather than vented, which is what makes very low fresh gas flows economically and clinically feasible. A Mapleson D system has no means of removing carbon dioxide and instead relies entirely on high fresh gas flow to flush it from the circuit, making it unsuitable for genuinely low-flow use. The circle system does require a reservoir bag and unidirectional valves to function correctly, and apparatus dead space, while reduced, is not eliminated entirely.
  82. The correct answer is B (White throughout, matching the current oxygen pipeline colour standard): Under the relevant British Standard, pipeline hoses and their Schrader probes are colour-coded so that the correct wall terminal outlet is always used for the correct gas. Oxygen pipeline hose is white, distinct from the blue used for nitrous oxide pipeline hose and the black-and-white quartered code used for medical air. This colour coding is a passive safety layer that reduces the risk of an incorrect gas being connected, alongside the non-interchangeable Schrader probe design itself.
  83. The correct answer is D (Medical air, identified by the grey body with quartered shoulder): Medical air cylinders are coloured grey with a black and white quartered shoulder under the current British Compressed Gases Association standard. This is distinct from oxygen, which uses a white body and shoulder, and nitrous oxide, which uses a blue body and shoulder. Correctly identifying cylinder colour coding before connecting a back-up cylinder is essential to avoid accidentally delivering the wrong gas when pipeline supply fails.
  84. The correct answer is A (Preventing an incorrect gas cylinder being fitted to the wrong yoke): The pin-index system uses a unique arrangement of pin positions for each medical gas, matched to corresponding holes on that gas's cylinder valve block, so that only the correct cylinder can physically be seated on a given yoke. This mechanical safeguard prevents a nitrous oxide cylinder, for example, being fitted where oxygen is expected. It works alongside colour coding as a second, physical layer of protection against wrong-gas delivery.
  85. The correct answer is C (Oesophageal intubation with the tube sitting outside the trachea): A persistently flat or near-zero capnograph trace with no discernible waveform, as shown, is the classic and most reliable sign that the tracheal tube has been placed in the oesophagus rather than the trachea. Some very low, irregular readings can occasionally occur transiently from swallowed gas, but a sustained absence of a normal waveform after intubation should always prompt immediate reassessment and, if confirmed, removal and reinsertion of the tube. Waveform capnography is considered the gold standard for confirming correct tracheal tube placement.
  86. The correct answer is E (Reducing the variable high cylinder pressure to a constant safe working pressure): A full size E oxygen cylinder delivers gas at roughly 137 bar, a pressure that falls as the cylinder empties and that would otherwise be unsafe and unpredictable for direct use on flowmeters and downstream equipment. The pressure regulator reduces this variable high pressure to a constant, much lower working pressure, typically around four bar, before the gas reaches the flowmeter. This ensures consistent, predictable flow regardless of how full the cylinder currently is.
  87. The correct answer is B (Gas flows through an increasing annular gap as the bobbin rises with flow): A rotameter tube is tapered, becoming wider towards the top, so the annular gap between the bobbin and the tube wall increases as the bobbin rises. At a higher position, a larger gap is needed to allow the increased gas flow to pass while the bobbin remains balanced against gravity. The bobbin therefore settles at a height that is directly related to the flow rate, allowing the scale printed on the tube to be read directly.
  88. The correct answer is D (Preventing delivery of a hypoxic gas mixture below a minimum oxygen concentration): The mechanical anti-hypoxic linkage couples the nitrous oxide and oxygen flow controls so that nitrous oxide flow cannot be increased, or oxygen flow decreased, to the point where the delivered mixture would fall below a safe minimum oxygen concentration, typically around twenty-five percent. This device is a critical safety feature specifically protecting against inadvertent hypoxic gas mixture delivery at the common gas outlet, distinct from the separate oxygen failure warning device.
  89. The correct answer is A (Fresh gas flow pushes alveolar gas out through the expiratory valve at the patient end): In the Mapleson A configuration, the expiratory (APL) valve sits at the patient end of the system, while fresh gas enters at the opposite end near the reservoir bag. During the expiratory pause, continuing fresh gas flow along the length of the tubing pushes exhaled alveolar gas preferentially out through the patient-end expiratory valve rather than allowing it to be rebreathed. This arrangement means relatively low fresh gas flows, approximately equal to the patient's minute volume, are sufficient to prevent rebreathing during spontaneous breathing.
  90. The correct answer is C (Rebreathing of carbon dioxide due to exhausted or bypassed soda lime): A rising inspiratory baseline that no longer returns to zero, combined with a climbing alveolar plateau over successive breaths as shown, is the characteristic capnography pattern of rebreathing. In a circle system, this most commonly reflects exhausted soda lime that has lost its capacity to absorb carbon dioxide, a faulty unidirectional valve, or excessive apparatus dead space. Prompt recognition and correction, usually by changing the absorbent, is required to prevent progressive hypercapnia.
  91. The correct answer is E (Positive pressure ventilation flushes alveolar gas towards the distal reservoir and valve): In the Mapleson D and Bain configurations, the expiratory valve sits at the machine end, distal to the patient, with fresh gas delivered close to the patient end. During controlled positive pressure ventilation, the inspiratory pressure wave drives alveolar gas away from the patient towards the distal valve and reservoir, effectively flushing dead space gas ahead of fresh gas. This makes the Mapleson D arrangement relatively efficient for controlled ventilation, unlike the Mapleson A, which is comparatively inefficient in that mode.
  92. The correct answer is B (It allows manual assessment and assistance of ventilation via the open bag): Replacing the open tail of the basic Ayre's T-piece with an open-ended reservoir bag, as in the Jackson-Rees modification, allows the anaesthetist to feel the patient's spontaneous respiratory effort and to gently assist or fully control ventilation by squeezing the bag when needed. This makes the Mapleson F system particularly useful in small children, where close observation and the ability to intervene quickly are valuable. It does not remove the requirement for adequate fresh gas flow to prevent rebreathing.
  93. The correct answer is A (Carbon dioxide reacts with water and calcium hydroxide to form calcium carbonate): Soda lime absorbs carbon dioxide chemically, primarily through carbon dioxide combining with water present in the granules to form carbonic acid, which then reacts with calcium hydroxide to produce calcium carbonate, water, and heat. A small amount of sodium hydroxide acts as an activator, speeding this reaction. This exothermic reaction is why a freshly changed soda lime canister can feel noticeably warm during heavy use, and why granule colour-change indicators are used to signal exhaustion.
  94. The correct answer is D (Compound A, formed from sevoflurane reacting with desiccated strong alkali absorbent): Sevoflurane can break down in the presence of strong alkali absorbents such as soda lime or barium hydroxide lime to form a vinyl ether compound known as Compound A, a reaction that is markedly increased when the absorbent has become desiccated through prolonged exposure to dry gas flows. Compound A has demonstrated nephrotoxic potential in animal studies, which is why manufacturers recommend against very low fresh gas flows with old or dried-out absorbent. Ensuring absorbent is not desiccated and avoiding prolonged very low flows with sevoflurane reduces this risk.
  95. The correct answer is C (Bronchospasm causing uneven, delayed emptying of alveolar gas): A sloping, shark-fin shaped expiratory upstroke that merges gradually into the plateau, rather than rising sharply, reflects uneven and delayed emptying of alveolar gas from lung units with differing time constants, which is characteristic of bronchospasm or other causes of increased airway resistance such as chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. The plateau itself may still be reached, but more slowly than normal. Recognising this pattern intraoperatively should prompt clinical assessment for wheeze and consideration of bronchodilator treatment.
  96. The correct answer is E (It typically improves the glottic view without needing direct line of sight): A videolaryngoscope positions a camera near the tip of the blade, allowing a view of the glottis to be obtained around the curvature of the tongue and pharynx without requiring the traditional direct line of sight from the mouth to the larynx. This typically improves the Cormack-Lehane grade achieved compared with direct laryngoscopy in patients with anatomically difficult airways. It does not guarantee success, remove the need for an assistant or adjuncts, or eliminate cervical spine movement entirely.
  97. The correct answer is B (60 cmH2O, the widely recommended upper limit for supraglottic airway cuffs): Supraglottic airway cuffs should be inflated to the minimum pressure needed to achieve an adequate seal, with widely cited guidance recommending cuff pressures should not exceed approximately sixty cmH2O. Pressures above this threshold are associated with increased risk of pharyngeal mucosal ischaemia, sore throat, and cranial nerve injury, including to the hypoglossal, lingual, and recurrent laryngeal nerves. Using a manometer rather than inflating by feel or a fixed volume is recommended to avoid inadvertent over-inflation.
  98. The correct answer is A (Mucosal ischaemia that may progress to tracheal stenosis or tracheomalacia): Tracheal capillary perfusion pressure is relatively low, so a cuff pressure that exceeds this can compress the mucosa and impair local blood flow, leading to ischaemic injury. If sustained over a prolonged period, as can occur during long procedures, this may progress to mucosal ulceration, tracheomalacia, or tracheal stenosis. Routine use of a manometer to keep cuff pressure within the recommended range, typically twenty to thirty cmH2O, reduces this risk compared with inflating the cuff by feel alone.
  99. The correct answer is D (ECG, pulse oximetry, NIBP, capnography, airway pressure, and inspired agent monitoring): The Association of Anaesthetists' minimum monitoring standard requires continuous ECG, pulse oximetry, non-invasive blood pressure, waveform capnography, airway pressure monitoring, and inspired and expired anaesthetic agent concentration monitoring for every general anaesthetic, together with temperature monitoring for longer procedures. Capnography is mandatory, not optional, whenever a general anaesthetic is administered, reflecting its role in confirming correct airway device placement and detecting ventilatory problems early. Invasive monitoring such as arterial and central venous lines is reserved for specific clinical indications rather than being part of the minimum standard.
  100. The correct answer is B (Do not use the machine until the oxygen failure warning device is repaired): The oxygen failure warning device is a critical safety alarm designed to alert staff immediately if pipeline or cylinder oxygen supply pressure falls dangerously low, and its failure to activate as shown in the checklist represents a significant safety-critical fault. The machine should not be used for patient care until this device has been repaired and retested, regardless of whether other checklist items passed. Continuing to use the machine while relying on an alternative gas source or simply documenting the fault for later does not adequately mitigate the risk of an undetected hypoxic gas supply failure.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (It provides early warning of airway obstruction or hypoventilation before hypoxia develops): Capnography detects changes in ventilation, such as airway obstruction, apnoea, or hypoventilation, before these progress to measurable hypoxaemia on pulse oximetry, since desaturation is typically a later and slower sign. Extending mandatory capnography monitoring into the immediate recovery period, until protective airway reflexes have returned, allows earlier detection of post-anaesthetic airway compromise. It complements, rather than replaces, pulse oximetry, and does not measure blood pressure, temperature, or neuromuscular block reversal.
  2. The correct answer is C (The Beer-Lambert law relating light absorption to concentration and path length): The Beer-Lambert law describes how the absorption of light passing through a solution is related to the concentration of the absorbing substance and the length of the light path. Pulse oximetry applies this principle at two wavelengths, red and infrared, exploiting the fact that oxyhaemoglobin and deoxyhaemoglobin absorb these wavelengths differently, and uses the pulsatile arterial component of the signal to calculate arterial oxygen saturation. Fick's principle, Henry's law, Dalton's law, and Poiseuille's law relate to other physiological or physical measurements entirely.
  3. The correct answer is A (Nail polish absorbing light and interfering with the transmitted light signal): Dark nail polish, particularly blue, green, or black shades, can absorb a significant proportion of the light transmitted through the fingertip, interfering with the light reaching the photodetector and commonly producing a falsely low saturation reading. This is a well recognised, correctable source of error, and the probe should be moved to an unpolished digit or a different measurement site such as the earlobe. Carboxyhaemoglobin and severe anaemia produce different characteristic effects on pulse oximetry readings rather than this pattern.
  4. The correct answer is D (Carboxyhaemoglobin absorbs light similarly to oxyhaemoglobin, giving a falsely high reading): Standard two-wavelength pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhaemoglobin from oxyhaemoglobin because carboxyhaemoglobin absorbs red and infrared light in a pattern similar to oxyhaemoglobin, causing the device to display a falsely reassuring, near-normal saturation reading even in significant carbon monoxide poisoning. Co-oximetry, which uses additional wavelengths, is required to accurately quantify carboxyhaemoglobin and true oxyhaemoglobin saturation. This is why suspected carbon monoxide poisoning should never be excluded on the basis of a normal pulse oximetry reading alone.
  5. The correct answer is E (The dicrotic notch, a small deflection on the descending limb of the waveform): The dicrotic notch is a small deflection seen on the descending limb of the arterial waveform, following the systolic peak, and corresponds to the closure of the aortic valve at the end of ventricular systole. It marks the transition between systolic ejection and diastolic run-off, after which the pressure continues to fall gradually until the next systolic upstroke. The steep upstroke and peak instead correspond to ventricular ejection, while the trough reflects end-diastolic pressure.
  6. The correct answer is B (Systolic pressure variation, reflecting the effect of positive pressure ventilation on preload): Positive pressure ventilation cyclically alters venous return and left ventricular preload, producing a corresponding cyclical variation in arterial systolic pressure amplitude that is exaggerated in hypovolaemic patients and reduced with adequate volume status. This phenomenon, along with related measures such as pulse pressure variation, is used as a dynamic indicator of fluid responsiveness in mechanically ventilated, fully sedated and paralysed patients. It differs from pulsus paradoxus, which is classically an exaggerated fall in systolic pressure during spontaneous inspiration.
  7. The correct answer is C (An underdamped or resonant system, exaggerating systolic and diastolic pressure extremes): An underdamped or resonant arterial line system produces an overshooting, spiked systolic peak with additional oscillations after the main upstroke, exaggerating the true systolic pressure while underestimating diastolic pressure compared with a properly damped reference measurement. This commonly results from excessively long or narrow, highly compliant tubing, or from air within the system creating resonance. Recognising underdamping is important because treatment decisions based on the exaggerated readings could otherwise be inappropriate.
  8. The correct answer is A (Right atrial contraction occurring immediately after the P wave on the ECG): The a wave of the central venous pressure trace corresponds to right atrial contraction, occurring shortly after the P wave is seen on the simultaneously displayed ECG. This is followed by the c wave, reflecting bulging of the tricuspid valve into the atrium during isovolumetric ventricular contraction, and later the v wave, reflecting atrial filling against a closed tricuspid valve during ventricular systole. Understanding this timing relationship allows abnormal waveforms, such as cannon a waves, to be correctly interpreted.
  9. The correct answer is D (Right atrial contraction against a closed tricuspid valve during AV dissociation): In complete heart block, atrial and ventricular contraction become dissociated, so the atrium occasionally contracts while the tricuspid valve is already closed by ventricular systole, generating a large, irregular cannon a wave on the central venous pressure trace. Because atrial and ventricular timing are no longer coordinated, these cannon waves occur intermittently rather than with a fixed relationship to the QRS complex. This differs from the regular, exaggerated a waves seen with tricuspid stenosis, where AV synchrony is preserved.
  10. The correct answer is E (Increased airway resistance from bronchospasm or a partially kinked tracheal tube): A rise in peak airway pressure without a corresponding rise in plateau pressure, as shown on the panel, indicates increased resistance to gas flow within the airway or breathing circuit rather than a change in overall lung or chest wall compliance. Bronchospasm, secretions, or a partially kinked or obstructed tracheal tube all increase resistance in this way. In contrast, causes of reduced compliance, such as worsening pneumoperitoneum or pulmonary oedema, would typically raise both peak and plateau pressure together.
  11. The correct answer is B (The neonatal epiglottis is floppy, long, and angled, needing direct elevation): The neonatal and infant epiglottis is proportionally longer, floppier, and more acutely angled over the laryngeal inlet than the adult epiglottis, making it harder to elevate indirectly by placing the blade tip in the vallecula, as is done with a curved Macintosh blade. A straight blade allows the epiglottis itself to be directly lifted, improving the view of the glottis in this age group. This anatomical difference, along with a relatively larger tongue and more cephalad larynx, underlies several paediatric airway management adaptations.
  12. The correct answer is C (The camera positioned near the blade tip captures a view around the curve of the tongue): Videolaryngoscope blades incorporate a camera close to the blade tip, allowing a view to be obtained from a point further into the oropharynx, around the curvature of the tongue, rather than relying on a direct line of sight from outside the mouth. This typically improves the visualised glottic view in patients with an anatomically difficult airway, converting a poor direct laryngoscopy grade into a better indirect view. It does not remove the value of alignment or eliminate the need for adjuncts such as a bougie in every case.
  13. The correct answer is A (The top of a skirted bobbin, rather than its widest central point): A skirted bobbin has a wider flange, or skirt, at its lower edge, which is designed so the correct reading is taken from the top of the bobbin, consistent with how the flowmeter scale is calibrated for that specific bobbin design. This differs from a simple ball-type bobbin, which is read at its widest, central point, since a ball has no directional orientation. Reading the wrong part of a skirted bobbin can lead to a clinically significant misreading of the actual gas flow being delivered.
  14. The correct answer is D (An incorrect cylinder could potentially be attached, risking delivery of the wrong gas): The pin-index system exists specifically to prevent a cylinder containing one gas being physically attached to a yoke intended for a different gas, using a unique pin pattern for each gas. If the pins were absent or damaged, this physical safeguard would be lost, allowing an incorrect cylinder, potentially containing an entirely different gas, to be seated and used, with serious safety implications. This system operates independently of, but alongside, the separate colour-coding scheme used to identify cylinders visually.
  15. The correct answer is B (An overdamped system, commonly caused by an air bubble or clot in the tubing): A rounded, low-amplitude waveform with loss of the dicrotic notch and narrowed pulse pressure, together with a slow, non-oscillating return to baseline on fast-flush testing, is characteristic of an overdamped arterial line system. Common causes include air bubbles, blood clot, kinking of the tubing, or a partially occluded catheter tip. Overdamping causes systolic pressure to be underestimated and diastolic pressure to be overestimated, which can mislead clinical decisions if not recognised and corrected by flushing or repositioning the line.
  16. The correct answer is E (The indicator dye changes colour, typically from white or pink towards a darker shade): Soda lime contains a pH-sensitive indicator dye that changes colour as the absorbent's pH falls with progressive carbon dioxide absorption and exhaustion, commonly shifting from white towards pink, or from pink towards a deeper purple depending on the specific indicator used. This visible colour change provides a simple way for theatre staff to identify when the canister needs changing, although the colour can sometimes partially revert on standing, so clinical judgement and capnography trends should also be considered. Physical changes such as granule size or magnetism are not reliable indicators of exhaustion.
  17. The correct answer is C (The position of the fresh gas inlet relative to the expiratory valve determines gas washout efficiency): The relative positions of the fresh gas inlet, the patient connection, and the expiratory valve within each Mapleson configuration determine how effectively fresh gas washes out and prevents rebreathing of alveolar gas during a given ventilation mode. In Mapleson A, this arrangement favours spontaneous ventilation, whereas in Mapleson D, E, and F, it favours controlled ventilation. This structural explanation, rather than any difference in valve number or absolute fresh gas flow requirement alone, underlies the classic efficiency differences taught for these systems.
  18. The correct answer is A (Mixing of anatomical dead space gas with alveolar gas as exhalation continues): Phase II of the capnograph waveform represents the transition during exhalation as gas from the anatomical dead space, containing little carbon dioxide, mixes progressively with carbon dioxide-rich alveolar gas being exhaled behind it, producing the characteristic rapid upstroke. This is followed by phase III, the alveolar plateau, once relatively uniform alveolar gas is being sampled. Recognising the normal phases allows abnormal patterns, such as a sloped phase II seen in bronchospasm, to be identified more readily.
  19. The correct answer is D (The gauge pressure falls in direct proportion to the volume of gas remaining): Because oxygen remains entirely gaseous within the cylinder at room temperature rather than existing partly as liquid, its pressure falls in direct, linear proportion to the amount of gas remaining, unlike a cylinder containing a liquefied gas such as nitrous oxide or entonox, where pressure remains relatively constant until the liquid phase is exhausted. This means the pressure gauge reading on an oxygen cylinder can be used directly to estimate the remaining contents and therefore the duration of supply available. This distinction is an important safety point when planning gas supply for transfers or prolonged cases.
  20. The correct answer is E (Thirty minutes, beyond which active warming and temperature monitoring are recommended): UK perioperative guidance from NICE (Clinical Guideline 65, Inadvertent Perioperative Hypothermia) recommends that temperature should be measured and recorded before induction and at intervals throughout any anaesthetic expected to last longer than approximately thirty minutes, alongside active warming measures to prevent inadvertent perioperative hypothermia. Shorter procedures still warrant clinical vigilance but are considered lower risk for clinically significant heat loss. This recommendation reflects evidence that even mild perioperative hypothermia is associated with increased rates of surgical site infection, coagulopathy, and adverse cardiac events.
  21. The correct answer is C (Ordinal data): ASA physical status grade groups patients into ranked categories from I to V, where a higher grade indicates greater systemic disease, but the difference between grade I and grade II is not necessarily the same clinical distance as between grade IV and grade V. This ordering without equal spacing is the defining feature of ordinal data. Nominal data would apply if the categories had no inherent order, such as blood group. Because the intervals are unequal, ASA grade should be summarised with a median and analysed with non-parametric methods rather than treated as continuous or parametric data.
  22. The correct answer is A (Continuous data): mean arterial pressure measured via an arterial line can take any numerical value within a physiological range, including fractions of a millimetre of mercury, and is not restricted to a fixed set of categories. This makes it continuous numerical data rather than categorical or ordinal data. Continuous data of this kind, if approximately normally distributed, is usually summarised using the mean and standard deviation. It can be analysed with parametric tests provided the underlying distributional assumptions are met.
  23. The correct answer is E (The data are approximately normally distributed): parametric tests such as the t-test and ANOVA assume that the underlying data follow a normal distribution, along with assumptions about equal variance between groups where relevant. Sample size alone does not justify using a parametric test if the data are markedly skewed. Ordinal data are generally analysed using non-parametric tests rather than parametric ones. When the normality assumption is not met, a non-parametric alternative such as the Mann-Whitney U test should be used instead.
  24. The correct answer is B (Median): the chart shows a distribution with a long tail towards longer recovery times, which is a positively skewed rather than a symmetrical distribution. In a skewed distribution, the mean is pulled towards the tail and no longer represents the typical value well, whereas the median remains a robust measure of the centre. The mode alone does not summarise the overall pattern of a continuous variable. The median, paired with the interquartile range, is therefore the most appropriate way to describe central tendency in this skewed dataset.
  25. The correct answer is D (Standard error of the mean): the standard error of the mean quantifies how much a sample mean is expected to vary from the true population mean, and it decreases as the sample size increases because larger samples give more precise estimates. Standard deviation, by contrast, describes the spread of individual data points around the mean and does not shrink simply because more patients are added. The standard error is calculated by dividing the standard deviation by the square root of the sample size. It is the appropriate statistic when describing the precision of an estimated mean rather than the variability of individual measurements.
  26. The correct answer is A (The 95% confidence interval becomes narrower): the chart shows confidence interval width falling steadily as sample size rises, which reflects the underlying statistical principle that larger samples produce more precise estimates of the true treatment effect. This narrowing occurs because the standard error of the mean falls as sample size increases, and the confidence interval width is directly related to the standard error. A larger sample does not guarantee statistical significance, since the true effect size and variability also influence the p-value. Increasing sample size generally increases statistical power, which reduces rather than increases the Type II error rate.
  27. The correct answer is C (95%): in a true normal distribution, approximately 68% of values lie within one standard deviation of the mean, approximately 95% lie within two standard deviations, and approximately 99.7% lie within three standard deviations. This relationship is a fixed property of the normal distribution and underlies many statistical calculations, including confidence interval construction. The bell-shaped curve shown is symmetrical about the mean, with the majority of birth weights clustering close to the central value. Values further from the mean become progressively less frequent, following this well-established pattern.
  28. The correct answer is D (Null hypothesis): the null hypothesis is the default statistical assumption that there is no true difference or effect between the groups being compared, and it is what a hypothesis test seeks to either reject or fail to reject. The alternative hypothesis, by contrast, states that a real difference does exist. A Type I error occurs when the null hypothesis is wrongly rejected, and statistical power relates to the ability to detect a true effect. Framing the comparison as starting from an assumption of no difference is the essence of the null hypothesis approach to hypothesis testing.
  29. The correct answer is B (There is a 3% probability of obtaining this result if the null hypothesis were true): a p-value describes the probability of observing data at least as extreme as the actual result, calculated on the assumption that the null hypothesis is correct, and it does not directly state the probability that either hypothesis is true. A p-value below the conventional threshold of 0.05 suggests the result is unlikely to have occurred by chance alone, leading to rejection of the null hypothesis. Statistical significance, indicated by a low p-value, does not automatically mean the finding is clinically important. The p-value also says nothing directly about whether the result would be reproducible in a future study.
  30. The correct answer is E (Type I error): a Type I error occurs when a study concludes that a true difference or effect exists when, in reality, the null hypothesis is correct and no genuine difference is present. This is sometimes described as a false positive result. A Type II error, in contrast, occurs when a real difference is missed and the null hypothesis is wrongly accepted. The scenario described, wrongly concluding benefit where none truly exists, is the classic definition of a false-positive Type I error.
  31. The correct answer is D (Type II error): a Type II error occurs when a study fails to detect a true effect that genuinely exists, wrongly accepting the null hypothesis of no difference. This is often described as a false-negative result and is more likely to occur in small, underpowered studies such as the pilot study described. A Type I error, by contrast, is a false-positive finding of a difference that does not truly exist. Because the sedation protocol genuinely reduces delirium but the study fails to show this, the error described is a Type II error.
  32. The correct answer is C (The power of the study): statistical power is the probability that a study will correctly detect a true difference between groups when one genuinely exists, and it is influenced by sample size, effect size, and the chosen significance threshold. A p-value describes the result of a completed analysis rather than the study's ability to detect an effect in advance. The confidence interval describes the precision of an estimate, and the Type I error rate describes the chance of a false-positive result. Adequate power, conventionally at least 80%, is calculated before a study begins to help determine the required sample size.
  33. The correct answer is A (Independent samples t-test): the independent samples t-test compares the means of a normally distributed continuous variable between two separate, unrelated groups of participants, which matches the scenario of comparing induction times between two different drugs. The chi-squared test is used for categorical outcome data rather than continuous measurements. The Mann-Whitney U test would be appropriate if the data were not normally distributed, and the paired t-test applies only when the same subjects are measured twice. One-way ANOVA is reserved for comparisons involving three or more independent groups.
  34. The correct answer is B (Paired t-test): the paired t-test compares two sets of continuous measurements taken from the same subjects, such as pain scores before and after treatment, accounting for the fact that each patient acts as their own control. The independent samples t-test is used instead when measurements come from two separate, unrelated groups of patients. The chi-squared test applies to categorical data, and the Mann-Whitney U test is the non-parametric alternative used when data are not normally distributed. Because the same patients are measured twice here, the paired design is the correct approach.
  35. The correct answer is E (Chi-squared test): the chi-squared test is used to compare proportions of a categorical outcome, such as presence or absence of nausea, across two or more independent groups. The t-test and ANOVA are designed for continuous, normally distributed data rather than categorical outcomes. The Mann-Whitney U test is used for non-parametric continuous or ordinal data rather than simple present-or-absent categories. Because the outcome here is categorical and there are three groups being compared, the chi-squared test is the most appropriate statistical method.
  36. The correct answer is C (One-way ANOVA): one-way analysis of variance compares the means of a normally distributed continuous variable across three or more independent groups simultaneously, controlling the overall risk of a false-positive result that would arise from performing multiple separate t-tests. The independent samples t-test is designed for comparing only two groups at a time. The chi-squared test applies to categorical rather than continuous data, and the Mann-Whitney U test would be used if the data were non-parametric. Because three independent groups are being compared here using continuous data, one-way ANOVA is the correct test.
  37. The correct answer is D (Mann-Whitney U test): the Mann-Whitney U test is the non-parametric equivalent of the independent samples t-test, used to compare two independent groups when the data are ordinal or not normally distributed. The independent samples t-test and one-way ANOVA both require an assumption of normality that is not met here. The chi-squared test is used for simple categorical data rather than ranked ordinal scores. Because the pain scores are ordinal and non-normally distributed, and the groups are independent rather than paired, the Mann-Whitney U test is the correct choice.
  38. The correct answer is B (80%): sensitivity is calculated as true positives divided by all patients who actually have the disease, which is true positives divided by the sum of true positives and false negatives. From the table, true positives are 80 and false negatives are 20, giving 80 divided by 100, which equals 80%. Sensitivity reflects how well the test identifies patients who genuinely have anaemia, rather than how well it identifies those without the condition. A highly sensitive test is particularly useful for ruling out disease when the result is negative.
  39. The correct answer is A (True negatives divided by (true negatives plus false positives)): specificity measures the proportion of patients without the disease who are correctly identified as test-negative, calculated as true negatives divided by the sum of true negatives and false positives. This differs from sensitivity, which instead considers true positives divided by true positives plus false negatives among those who genuinely have the disease. Positive predictive value uses true positives divided by all positive test results, which is a different calculation entirely. Specificity is particularly useful for ruling in disease when a test result is positive, since a highly specific test produces few false positives.
  40. The correct answer is E (Positive predictive value decreases): positive predictive value describes the proportion of patients with a positive test result who truly have the disease, and it falls as disease prevalence decreases even when the test's sensitivity and specificity remain fixed. This happens because, in a low-prevalence population, a larger proportion of positive results become false positives relative to true positives. Sensitivity and specificity are properties of the test itself and do not change with prevalence. This relationship explains why screening tests can perform poorly in low-risk populations despite unchanged underlying test accuracy.
  41. The correct answer is B (Negative predictive value increases): negative predictive value describes the proportion of patients with a negative test result who truly do not have the disease, and it rises as disease prevalence falls, assuming sensitivity and specificity remain constant. In a very low-prevalence population, most people genuinely do not have the disease, so a negative result is very likely to be a true negative. Sensitivity and specificity are intrinsic properties of the test and are not altered by changes in prevalence. This inverse relationship between prevalence and negative predictive value is the opposite pattern seen with positive predictive value.
  42. The correct answer is D (30%): positive predictive value is calculated as true positives divided by all patients with a positive test result, which is true positives divided by the sum of true positives and false positives. From the table, true positives are 18 and false positives are 42, giving 18 divided by 60, which equals 30%. This relatively low positive predictive value reflects the low prevalence of the complication in this surgical cohort, meaning many positive results are false alarms. This illustrates why prevalence has a major influence on how a positive test result should be interpreted in practice.
  43. The correct answer is C (Randomised controlled trial): a randomised controlled trial involves randomly allocating participants to different interventions, and when neither the clinician nor the patient knows which treatment has been given, this is described as double blinding. This combination of randomisation and blinding is specifically designed to reduce both confounding and bias in the estimate of treatment effect. A cohort study instead follows exposed and unexposed groups forward without random allocation, and a case-control study looks backwards from an outcome. The described design, with random allocation and blinding of a new induction agent, is characteristic of a randomised controlled trial.
  44. The correct answer is E (Cohort study): a cohort study identifies groups based on exposure status, such as prolonged intraoperative hypotension, and follows them forward in time to compare the incidence of an outcome such as acute kidney injury. This forward-looking design distinguishes it from a case-control study, which instead starts with the outcome and looks backwards at exposure. A randomised controlled trial would require active random allocation of the exposure, which is not described here. Because participants are grouped by exposure and followed prospectively for outcomes, this is a cohort study design.
  45. The correct answer is A (Case-control study): a case-control study identifies participants based on outcome status, comparing those who developed a condition such as acute kidney injury with matched controls who did not, then looks backwards to compare prior exposures between the groups. This retrospective, outcome-first approach distinguishes it from a cohort study, which instead starts with exposure and follows participants forward. A randomised controlled trial would require active allocation of exposure, which does not occur in this observational design. Because the study starts with cases and controls and looks back at hypotension exposure, this is a case-control study.
  46. The correct answer is D (Recall bias): recall bias occurs when participants with a particular outcome, such as chronic postsurgical pain, remember and report past events differently from those without the outcome, which can distort the apparent association being studied. This is a particular concern in case-control studies because they rely on participants recalling past exposures rather than measuring them prospectively. Selection bias instead relates to how participants are chosen for a study, and confounding involves a third factor linked to both exposure and outcome. Because the difference here arises specifically from differential memory and reporting after the outcome has occurred, this is recall bias.
  47. The correct answer is B (Confounding factor): a confounding factor is a variable that is independently associated with both the exposure and the outcome, distorting the apparent relationship between them, as surgical duration is here linked to both opioid dose and respiratory complications. Selection bias instead relates to how participants enter a study, and recall bias relates to differential memory of past events. Statistical adjustment or stratification for surgical duration would be needed to isolate the true relationship, if any, between opioid dose and respiratory complications. Recognising and controlling for confounding factors is essential when interpreting observational study findings.
  48. The correct answer is C (To distribute known and unknown confounding factors evenly between groups): randomisation aims to balance both measured and unmeasured patient characteristics between treatment groups, reducing the risk that differences in outcome are due to confounding rather than the intervention itself. Randomisation does not remove the need for statistical analysis, nor does it guarantee a statistically significant result. It also does not necessarily produce exactly equal group sizes, particularly with smaller sample sizes, and a control group remains essential for comparison. This balancing effect is the fundamental statistical rationale for using randomisation in trial design.
  49. The correct answer is E (To reduce observer and reporting bias in outcome assessment): blinding prevents patients and outcome assessors from being influenced by knowledge of treatment allocation, which reduces the risk that expectations or beliefs distort how outcomes are measured or reported. Blinding does not directly increase statistical power or reduce the required sample size, and it cannot eliminate confounding, which is instead addressed through randomisation. It also has no direct effect on guaranteeing a particular p-value. By keeping assessment independent of treatment knowledge, blinding helps ensure that observed differences reflect a true treatment effect rather than bias.
  50. The correct answer is A (Audit cycle): the audit cycle describes the structured process of setting a standard, measuring current practice against it, implementing change where needed, and then re-measuring to assess whether improvement has occurred. This cyclical process is distinct from a randomised controlled trial, which tests an intervention under controlled experimental conditions rather than measuring compliance with an existing standard. A case-control study and systematic review are research methodologies rather than quality improvement processes. Repeating measurement after implementing change to check for sustained improvement is the defining feature of a complete audit cycle.
  51. The correct answer is D (To maintain and continuously improve the quality of patient care): clinical governance is the overarching framework through which healthcare organisations are accountable for continuously improving the quality of their services and safeguarding high standards of care. Components such as risk management, education, clinical effectiveness, and patient involvement all contribute towards this shared goal rather than serving separate, unrelated purposes. Clinical governance does not itself calculate statistical power for research projects, determine drug licensing, or set trainee pay scales. Local clinical audit is one of the practical tools used within clinical governance rather than something it replaces.
  52. The correct answer is B (Interquartile range): the interquartile range describes the spread of the middle 50% of values and is not distorted by extreme values in a skewed tail, making it the appropriate companion to the median in a positively skewed distribution such as this one. Standard deviation and variance are both heavily influenced by extreme values and are more appropriate for symmetrical, normally distributed data reported alongside the mean. The standard error of the mean describes precision of an estimated mean rather than the spread of individual data points. Because the length of stay data are skewed, the interquartile range is the most appropriate measure of spread to report.
  53. The correct answer is E (Mode): the mode is defined as the single most frequently occurring value in a dataset, which directly matches the theatre manager's aim of identifying the most common number of cases per operating list. The mean and median instead describe the average or middle value of the dataset rather than the most frequent one. Standard deviation and interquartile range describe spread rather than central tendency and would not answer this specific question. Because the manager wants the most commonly occurring value specifically, the mode is the correct measure to use.
  54. The correct answer is C (The range containing the middle 50% of extubation times): the interquartile range spans from the 25th percentile to the 75th percentile of a dataset, meaning it contains the middle half of all observed values around the median. It is not the same as the full range, which would include the most extreme minimum and maximum values in the dataset. It is also distinct from the standard deviation, which is a parametric measure of spread more suited to normally distributed data, and from a confidence interval, which describes precision of an estimated statistic. Reporting the interquartile range alongside the median is the standard approach for non-normally distributed data such as skewed extubation times.
  55. The correct answer is A (Correlation alone does not establish that one factor causes the other): a statistically significant correlation demonstrates that two variables tend to change together, but it cannot by itself prove that one factor directly causes the other, since confounding factors or reverse causation may explain the relationship. A low p-value indicates the correlation is unlikely to be due to chance alone but says nothing about clinical importance or causal mechanism. Confounding factors can and frequently do influence observed correlation coefficients, contrary to option D. Careful study design, such as a randomised controlled trial, is generally needed to establish causation rather than relying on observational correlation alone.
  56. The correct answer is B (Patients receiving the technique are twice as likely to develop delirium): a relative risk of 2.0 means that the risk of the outcome in the exposed group is twice the risk in the comparison group, indicating an increased rather than decreased risk with the anaesthetic technique. A relative risk below 1.0 would indicate a protective effect, whereas a relative risk of exactly 1.0 would indicate no association at all. Relative risk and odds ratio are related but distinct measures, and a relative risk of 2.0 does not itself specify the exact absolute risk difference between groups. This relative risk value therefore indicates a doubling of delirium risk associated with the technique.
  57. The correct answer is D (5): the number needed to treat is calculated as one divided by the absolute risk reduction, expressed as a decimal, so one divided by 0.20 equals 5. This means that, on average, five patients would need to be treated with the antiemetic to prevent one additional episode of postoperative nausea and vomiting compared with placebo. A smaller number needed to treat generally indicates a more effective intervention, while a larger number indicates a smaller clinical benefit per patient treated. This calculation is a standard way of translating a relative statistical finding into a clinically interpretable measure of treatment benefit.
  58. The correct answer is E (20 mL): the standard error of the mean is calculated by dividing the standard deviation by the square root of the sample size, so 100 mL divided by the square root of 25, which is 5, gives 100 divided by 5, equalling 20 mL. This value describes the precision of the sample mean as an estimate of the true population mean blood loss, and it will decrease further if the sample size is increased. It should not be confused with the standard deviation itself, which describes the spread of individual patients' blood loss values rather than the precision of the mean estimate. This calculation underlies the construction of confidence intervals around the reported mean.
  59. The correct answer is C (Chi-squared test): the chi-squared test compares the distribution of a categorical outcome, such as infection present or absent, across two or more independent groups, which matches the comparison of infection rates between the two skin preparation solutions shown in the table. The t-test and ANOVA are designed for continuous, normally distributed outcome data rather than categorical counts. Pearson correlation assesses the strength of a linear relationship between two continuous variables and is not appropriate for categorical count data arranged in a contingency table. Because the data here are categorical counts across two independent groups, the chi-squared test is the correct statistical method.
  60. The correct answer is A (Re-audit, closing the loop): re-auditing after implementing a change and re-collecting data against the original standard is the stage of the audit cycle often described as closing the loop, since it determines whether the intervention produced sustained improvement in practice. Setting the standard and selecting the audit topic both occur earlier in the cycle, before any change is implemented. Baseline data collection also occurs at the start of the process, prior to any intervention being introduced. Dissemination of results is a separate communication step and does not itself involve re-measuring compliance against the standard.
  61. The correct answer is A (Continuous data, since it can take any value on a numerical scale): Time to extubation, measured in minutes, is a continuous variable because it can take any value within a range, including fractions of a minute. Continuous data differs from discrete data, which can only take whole number values such as counts. ASA grade is ordinal, since grades can be ranked but intervals between them are not equal, while sex is categorical (nominal). Recognising data type correctly determines which descriptive statistics and hypothesis tests are appropriate.
  62. The correct answer is B (Ordinal data, because categories have a meaningful rank but unequal intervals): ASA grade is ordinal data because the categories can be ranked from least to most severe, but the difference in risk between grade I and II is not necessarily equal to that between grade IV and V. Nominal data has categories with no order, such as blood group. Continuous and interval data require a consistent numerical scale, which ASA grading does not provide. Choosing ordinal rather than parametric statistics is essential when analysing such variables.
  63. The correct answer is C (Ninety five percent of the population values shown): In a normal distribution, approximately ninety five percent of values lie within two standard deviations of the mean, while sixty eight percent lie within one standard deviation and ninety nine point seven percent lie within three. This property allows the curve shown to be used to estimate reference ranges and identify outliers. The symmetrical, bell-shaped appearance confirms the data are normally distributed, which is a prerequisite for using parametric statistical tests. Recognising this rule is essential for interpreting reference intervals in clinical practice.
  64. The correct answer is D (Mode, because it is the value that occurs most often): The mode is defined as the value that occurs most frequently within a dataset, which in this case is three previous anaesthetics. The mean requires summing all values and dividing by the sample size, while the median is the middle value once data are ranked in order. The mode is particularly useful for describing categorical or heavily skewed data, where the mean can be misleading. Datasets can have more than one mode if two or more values share the highest frequency.
  65. The correct answer is E (Standard deviation describes spread of data, standard error describes precision of the mean): Standard deviation quantifies how much individual values vary around the sample mean, describing the spread of the data itself. Standard error of the mean estimates how precisely that sample mean reflects the true population mean, and it decreases as sample size increases because larger samples give more reliable estimates. This is why standard deviation is appropriate for describing baseline characteristics, while standard error underpins confidence intervals when comparing groups. Confusing the two can make a study appear more or less precise than it truly is.
  66. The correct answer is B (Repeated sampling would produce an interval containing the true value ninety five percent of the time): A ninety five percent confidence interval means that if the study were repeated many times, ninety five percent of the calculated intervals would be expected to contain the true population value. It does not describe the probability that any single interval contains the truth, nor does it describe the spread of outcomes across individual patients. Because this interval for the relative risk does not cross one, the result is statistically significant, indicating a genuine reduction in nausea. Confidence intervals also convey precision, with narrower intervals reflecting larger or less variable samples.
  67. The correct answer is A (The central limit theorem, describing the distribution of sample means): The central limit theorem states that, as sample size increases, the distribution of sample means approaches a normal distribution regardless of the shape of the underlying population data. This principle justifies the use of parametric tests on sample means even when individual data points are not normally distributed, provided the sample is sufficiently large. The law of large numbers instead describes how a sample statistic converges towards the true population value as sample size grows. Regression to the mean and Bayes' theorem describe entirely different statistical phenomena unrelated to sampling distributions.
  68. The correct answer is D (Ninety percent): Sensitivity is calculated as true positives divided by the total number of patients who truly have the disease, which from the table shown is 180 divided by the sum of 180 true positives and 20 false negatives, giving 180 over 200, or ninety percent. Sensitivity describes how well a test identifies patients who genuinely have the condition, and a highly sensitive test is useful for ruling out disease when negative. Specificity, in contrast, would use the true negative and false positive figures instead. This assay correctly identifies the large majority of true myocardial infarctions.
  69.
