The correct answer is C (Type I error, because a false positive result was accepted as significant): A Type I error occurs when the null hypothesis is incorrectly rejected, meaning a statistically significant result is reported when no true difference actually exists. The conventional significance threshold of p less than 0.05 accepts a five percent chance of this happening by chance alone. A Type II error is the opposite mistake, failing to detect a genuine difference that truly exists. Confounding and selection bias are separate methodological problems relating to study design rather than the statistical interpretation of chance findings.
  70. The correct answer is E (Type II error, because inadequate power failed to detect a real effect): A Type II error occurs when a study fails to reject the null hypothesis despite a true effect existing, most commonly because the study lacks statistical power. Power is the probability of correctly detecting a true difference and is influenced by sample size, effect size, and variability, with underpowered studies at particular risk of missing genuine effects. This differs from a Type I error, which describes falsely detecting an effect that does not exist. Increasing sample size, reducing variability, or using a more sensitive outcome measure can all improve power in future studies.
  71. The correct answer is D (The exact identity of the surgeon performing each operation): Sample size calculations require the anticipated effect size, the acceptable Type I error rate, the variability of the outcome measure, and the desired statistical power, but do not require knowledge of individual operators performing the procedure. These four statistical inputs determine how many participants are needed to reliably detect a meaningful difference if one truly exists. Ignoring any of these components risks an underpowered study that cannot reliably detect a genuine effect. Surgeon identity might be relevant to the study design as a potential confounder, but it is not a mathematical input to the power calculation itself.
  72. The correct answer is A (Paired t-test, because the same subjects are measured on two occasions): A paired t-test is appropriate when the same subjects are measured twice, such as before and after an intervention, because it accounts for the natural correlation between each patient's own two measurements. An unpaired t-test is used instead when comparing two independent, unrelated groups of subjects. The Mann-Whitney U test would be used if the data were not normally distributed, and ANOVA is reserved for comparing three or more groups. Using an unpaired test on paired data reduces statistical power and can produce misleading results.
  73. The correct answer is E (Repeated measures ANOVA, accounting for correlated measurements over time): Repeated measures ANOVA is designed for data where the same subjects are measured multiple times, such as blood pressure recorded at repeated intervals, because it accounts for correlation between successive readings within each patient. Analysing only the final value would discard most of the information shown in the curve shown across the procedure. A simple correlation coefficient would only describe the relationship between time and pressure, not the difference between treatment groups. Repeated measures ANOVA allows valid comparison of the overall trend between the two vasopressor regimens across the entire study period.
  74. The correct answer is B (Chi-squared test, because both variables are categorical in nature): The chi-squared test is used to compare the distribution of categorical variables between independent groups, and here both the treatment group and the infection outcome are categorical. This test compares observed frequencies in each category with the frequencies that would be expected if there were no association between the variables. A t-test or ANOVA would be inappropriate because the outcome is not a continuous measurement, and the Mann-Whitney U test is reserved for ordinal or non-normally distributed continuous data rather than simple binary categories.
  75. The correct answer is C (Mann-Whitney U test, because the data are ordinal and non-normally distributed): The Mann-Whitney U test is the non-parametric equivalent of the unpaired t-test and is appropriate for comparing two independent groups when the outcome data are ordinal, skewed, or otherwise fail the assumptions required for parametric testing. It compares the ranks of values between groups rather than their means, making it robust to outliers and non-normal distributions. An unpaired t-test would risk an invalid result if applied directly to markedly skewed data. Chi-squared testing is reserved for categorical data rather than an ordered numerical scale such as a pain score.
  76. The correct answer is A (One-way ANOVA, because three independent groups are being compared): One-way ANOVA is the appropriate parametric test for comparing means across three or more independent groups when the data are continuous, normally distributed, and have similar variance. Performing multiple separate unpaired t-tests instead would inflate the overall risk of a Type I error through repeated testing. The Kruskal-Wallis test would only be indicated if the assumptions of normality were not met, and chi-squared testing is reserved for categorical outcomes. If ANOVA shows an overall significant difference, post-hoc testing can then identify which specific pairs of groups differ.
  77. The correct answer is C (Positive predictive value decreases, so more positives are false): Positive predictive value describes the probability that a patient with a positive test result truly has the condition, and it falls as disease prevalence decreases, even when sensitivity and specificity remain constant. This occurs because, in a low-prevalence population, the number of true positives is small relative to the number of false positives generated among the much larger disease-free group. Sensitivity and specificity are intrinsic properties of the test itself and do not change with prevalence, but predictive values are heavily dependent on it. This is why screening rare conditions can generate many false alarms despite an accurate test.
  78. The correct answer is B (Thirty three percent): Positive predictive value is calculated as true positives divided by all positive test results, which from the table shown is 10 divided by the sum of 10 true positives and 20 false positives, giving 10 over 30, or approximately thirty three percent. Despite the test performing reasonably well overall, the low prevalence of disease in this asymptomatic population means most positive results are false alarms. This illustrates why predictive values, unlike sensitivity and specificity, depend heavily on the prevalence of disease in the population being tested. Confirmatory testing would be warranted before acting on a positive result in this context.
  79. The correct answer is E (A negative result reliably excludes anaemia in most tested patients): Negative predictive value describes the probability that a patient with a negative test result is truly free of disease, so a high negative predictive value means that a negative result reliably rules out anaemia in the population tested. This is distinct from sensitivity and specificity, which are fixed properties of the test itself rather than measures that vary with the population's disease prevalence. Positive predictive value, not negative predictive value, describes the reliability of a positive result. Clinicians should remember that predictive values change if the test is applied to a population with a different underlying prevalence.
  80. The correct answer is D (It reduces observer and reporting bias affecting outcome assessment): Double-blinding prevents both participants and outcome assessors from knowing group allocation, reducing observer bias, where an assessor's expectations influence how an outcome is measured or reported, and reporting bias from participants themselves. Randomisation, not blinding, is the process that aims to balance baseline characteristics and confounding variables between groups. Blinding does not increase statistical power, which instead depends on sample size, effect size, and variability. Blinding is particularly important for subjective outcomes, such as pain scores, where knowledge of treatment allocation could otherwise distort results.
  81. The correct answer is B (Cohort study, following an exposed and unexposed group forward in time): A cohort study identifies a group of subjects based on exposure status, in this case intraoperative hypotension, and follows them forward in time to observe the development of an outcome such as acute kidney injury. This design can estimate incidence and relative risk, and can be prospective or retrospective. A case-control study instead starts with patients who already have the outcome and looks backwards for prior exposures. Because exposure is not randomly allocated, a cohort study remains vulnerable to confounding, so measured and unmeasured differences between exposed and unexposed groups must be considered when interpreting the result.
  82. The correct answer is E (Case-control study, comparing exposures between cases and matched controls): A case-control study begins by identifying patients who already have the outcome of interest, here the rare complication of neuraxial anaesthesia, and compares their prior exposures with those of a matched control group who did not develop the outcome. This design is particularly efficient for studying rare outcomes, since a cohort study would require an impractically large sample to observe enough events. Case-control studies are, however, particularly susceptible to recall bias, since exposure information is often gathered retrospectively. Odds ratios, rather than relative risk, are the appropriate measure of association for this design.
  83. The correct answer is A (Unpaired t-test, because two independent normally distributed groups are compared): An unpaired t-test is appropriate when comparing the means of two independent groups of different patients, provided the data are approximately normally distributed with similar variance, both of which are stated for the data shown. Each patient received only one of the two techniques, so this is not a paired design comparing repeated measurements in the same individuals. Mobilisation time is a continuous variable rather than a categorical one, ruling out the chi-squared test, and the data meet parametric assumptions so a non-parametric alternative is unnecessary. The unpaired t-test will compare the two reported means while accounting for the spread described by each group's standard deviation.
  84. The correct answer is D (Confounding, because comorbidity status is linked to both exposure and outcome): Confounding occurs when a third variable, here baseline fitness and comorbidity burden, is associated with both the exposure being studied and the outcome, distorting the apparent relationship between them. In this example, fitter patients may have been preferentially selected for regional anaesthesia and would likely have had lower mortality regardless of anaesthetic technique. Statistical methods such as multivariable regression or matching can help adjust for known confounders, though unmeasured confounding can never be entirely excluded in observational studies. This differs from bias, which arises from flaws in how data are collected or measured rather than from a genuine third variable.
  85. The correct answer is C (Selection bias, because the sample is not representative of all patients): Selection bias arises when the method used to recruit participants systematically excludes certain groups, meaning the study sample does not represent the wider population intended for study. Here, excluding frailer patients unable to attend a preoperative clinic in person means the results may not apply to the sicker patients who could benefit most, or least, from the fast-track programme. Recall bias relates instead to inaccurate recollection of past exposures, while observer bias involves assessor expectations influencing measurement. Careful attention to recruitment methods is essential to ensure study findings can be generalised appropriately.
  86. The correct answer is D (Re-measure compliance to assess whether practice has improved): The audit cycle involves setting a standard, measuring current practice against that standard, implementing change where needed, and then re-auditing to determine whether the intervention has actually improved practice, closing the loop. Simply implementing an intervention without re-measurement cannot confirm that any genuine improvement has occurred. Unlike research, audit does not test a new hypothesis and generally does not require formal ethical approval, since it measures compliance with an already agreed standard rather than generating new generalisable knowledge. Repeating this cycle over time drives continuous quality improvement in clinical practice.
  87. The correct answer is B (Clinical audit, to systematically review and improve care against standards): Clinical audit is one of the seven recognised pillars of clinical governance, alongside risk management, education and training, patient and public involvement, use of information, staffing and staff management, and clinical effectiveness. These domains together form a framework through which healthcare organisations are accountable for continuously improving the quality of care and maintaining high standards. Financial forecasting, marketing, recruitment planning, and litigation defence are administrative or legal functions rather than recognised governance pillars. Understanding these domains is important for trainees participating in departmental quality improvement activities.
  88. The correct answer is C (Median, because it is less affected by the skewed extreme values): When a dataset is skewed, as shown by the long tail towards prolonged stays in the curve shown, the median is a more representative measure of central tendency than the mean because it is not pulled towards extreme outlying values. The mean would be artificially inflated by the small number of patients with very long stays, giving a misleading impression of the typical patient experience. Range and standard deviation describe spread rather than central tendency, and standard error describes precision of an estimated mean rather than a typical value. Reporting the median alongside the interquartile range is the standard approach for describing skewed continuous data.
  89. The correct answer is A (Correlation does not necessarily imply a direct causal relationship): A statistically significant correlation between two variables demonstrates that they tend to change together, but it does not prove that one variable causes the other, since an unmeasured third factor, such as staffing levels or ward culture, could independently influence both. Establishing causation typically requires additional evidence, such as a plausible biological or causal mechanism, a dose-response relationship, and ideally experimental evidence from a randomised trial. Statistical significance only indicates that an association is unlikely to have arisen by chance, not that it reflects a genuine causal pathway. This distinction is a common source of misinterpretation in observational research.
  90. The correct answer is E (Case-control studies cannot calculate true incidence, so relative risk is invalid): Case-control studies select participants based on outcome status rather than following a defined population forward in time, so the true incidence of the outcome in exposed and unexposed groups cannot be determined, making relative risk impossible to calculate directly. The odds ratio can instead be calculated from the same data and, when the outcome is rare, closely approximates the relative risk. Cohort studies and randomised controlled trials, which follow subjects forward in time, are able to calculate true incidence and therefore relative risk directly. Understanding which measure of association matches the underlying study design is essential for interpreting published research correctly.
  91. The correct answer is C (Five patients need treatment to prevent one case): Number needed to treat is calculated as the reciprocal of the absolute risk reduction, which here is forty percent minus twenty percent, giving an absolute risk reduction of twenty percent, or 0.2. Dividing one by 0.2 gives a number needed to treat of five, meaning five patients must receive the antiemetic to prevent one additional case of nausea and vomiting. A smaller number needed to treat generally indicates a more clinically effective intervention. This measure is a useful way of communicating the practical impact of a treatment effect beyond simply reporting statistical significance.
  92. The correct answer is A (Meta-analysis, statistically pooling results from multiple similar studies): A meta-analysis statistically combines results from multiple individual studies addressing a similar question, producing a single pooled estimate of effect with greater statistical power than any individual study alone. It is typically conducted as part of a systematic review, which follows an explicit, reproducible methodology for identifying and appraising relevant evidence. This differs from a cohort study or case series, which each generate new primary data from a single group of patients rather than synthesising existing published results. Heterogeneity between the included studies must be carefully assessed, since combining very different studies can produce a misleading pooled result.
  93. The correct answer is D (Eighty percent): Specificity is calculated as true negatives divided by the total number of patients who truly do not have the disease, which from the table shown is 240 divided by the sum of 240 true negatives and 60 false positives, giving 240 over 300, or eighty percent. Specificity describes how well a test correctly identifies patients without the condition, and a highly specific test is useful for confirming disease when the result is positive. Sensitivity would instead be calculated using the true positive and false negative figures. This questionnaire correctly excludes the large majority of patients without obstructive sleep apnoea.
  94. The correct answer is E (Regression to the mean, because extreme values tend to normalise on repeat testing): Regression to the mean describes the statistical tendency for an unusually extreme measurement to be followed by a value closer to the true average on repeat testing, simply because of natural biological and measurement variability rather than any true change in the underlying condition. This phenomenon can mislead clinicians into believing an intervention has been effective when no intervention was actually given, as illustrated here where the reading normalised without treatment. It is particularly important to consider when interpreting studies that select patients based on an extreme baseline value. Repeated measurement over time is the best way to distinguish a true trend from regression to the mean.
  95. The correct answer is B (It prevents selection bias by stopping investigators anticipating the next allocation): Allocation concealment prevents investigators enrolling participants from knowing or predicting the next treatment assignment, which stops them from consciously or unconsciously selecting which patients enter the trial based on anticipated group allocation, a form of selection bias. This is a distinct concept from blinding, which conceals allocation from patients and assessors after enrolment has already occurred. Allocation concealment does not affect statistical power, which depends on sample size and effect size, nor does it prevent loss to follow-up during the trial. Robust allocation concealment is an important marker of methodological quality when critically appraising a randomised controlled trial.
  96. The correct answer is E (Publication bias, because negative studies are less likely to be published): A funnel plot displays study effect size against a measure of study precision, and it should normally appear symmetrical if all relevant studies, positive and negative, have been included. Asymmetry, particularly a missing corner of small studies with negative or non-significant results, suggests publication bias, since studies with disappointing or null findings are less likely to be submitted or accepted for publication than those with positive results. This can lead a meta-analysis to overestimate the true benefit of an intervention. Statistical tests such as Egger's test can formally assess funnel plot asymmetry alongside visual inspection.
  97. The correct answer is D (Kaplan-Meier survival analysis, estimating the probability of survival over time): Kaplan-Meier survival analysis estimates the cumulative probability of survival, or of remaining free from an event, at successive time points, producing a stepped curve that falls each time an event such as death occurs. It can accommodate patients who are lost to follow-up or who leave the study before the event occurs, known as censoring, which is represented by tick marks on the curve. Linear regression and logistic regression instead model relationships between continuous or binary outcomes and predictor variables, without accounting for time-to-event data or censoring. Comparing Kaplan-Meier curves between groups is typically performed using the log-rank test.
  98. The correct answer is C (The data are approximately normally distributed with similar variance): Parametric tests such as the t-test and ANOVA assume that the underlying data are approximately normally distributed and that variance is similar between groups being compared, and confirming this assumption supports using a parametric approach. Ordinal data, categorical outcomes, small sample sizes, and datasets containing extreme outliers are all situations where a non-parametric test, such as the Mann-Whitney U test, is generally more appropriate and robust. Checking assumptions using a histogram, a normality test, or simply examining skewness is an essential step before selecting the correct statistical test. Applying a parametric test to data that violate these assumptions risks producing an invalid or misleading result.
  99. The correct answer is B (Observer bias, because knowledge of allocation may influence assessment): Observer bias occurs when an assessor's knowledge of a patient's treatment allocation influences how an outcome is measured or interpreted, which is a particular risk here since the same clinician who delivered the treatment also performed the outcome assessment. This risk is reduced by using a blinded assessor who is unaware of group allocation, particularly for outcomes involving an element of subjective judgement, such as range of motion measurement. Recall bias instead relates to inaccurate patient recollection of past events, and confounding describes an unmeasured variable linked to both exposure and outcome. Blinding of outcome assessment, even when full blinding of participants is not possible, remains an important safeguard against this bias.
  100. The correct answer is A (The p-value describes the probability of the observed data given the null hypothesis is true): The p-value represents the probability of observing data at least as extreme as that actually found, assuming the null hypothesis is true, rather than the probability that the null hypothesis itself is true or false. A p-value of 0.04 therefore means that, if there truly were no difference between the regimens, a difference this large or larger would be expected to occur by chance only four percent of the time. The p-value also gives no direct information about the size or clinical importance of an effect, which instead requires reporting an effect size and confidence interval. This is a frequently misunderstood aspect of statistical inference that trainees should be able to explain clearly.
